\displaystyle I

\displaystyle:=\int_{[|k|^{-1},\infty]^{2}}|G^{\bowtie}_{p}(k;x,y)-G^{\bowtie}_{p^{\prime}}(k;x,y)|^{2}\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y

(
5.21
)

\displaystyle\leq 2c^{2}\int_{|k|^{-1}}^{\infty}\int_{y}^{\infty}\mathrm{e}^{-2\,\mathrm{Im}(z)(x-y)}\left|\left(\frac{x}{y}\right)^{-\mathrm{i}\lambda}-\left(\frac{x}{y}\right)^{-\mathrm{i}\lambda^{\prime}}\right|^{2}\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y.

Next we change variables to
y,t
with
x=ty
. This gives

\displaystyle I

\displaystyle\leq\int_{1}^{\infty}\int_{|k|^{-1}}^{\infty}y\mathrm{e}^{-2|\mathrm{Im}(k)|(t-1)y}|t^{-\mathrm{i}\lambda}-t^{-\mathrm{i}\lambda^{\prime}}|^{2}\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}t

(
5.22
)

\displaystyle=\int_{1}^{\infty}\frac{|k|+2|\mathrm{Im}(k)|(t-1)}{4|\mathrm{Im}(k)|^{2}|k|(t-1)^{2}}\mathrm{e}^{-2\frac{|\mathrm{Im}(k)|}{|k|}(t-1)}|t^{-\mathrm{i}\lambda}-t^{-\mathrm{i}\lambda^{\prime}}|^{2}\mathrm{d}t,

where we have computed an elementary integral over
y
. The remaining integrand is bounded for
t\to 1
and decays exponentially for
t\to\infty
. Therefore the integral converges.
∎

Resolvents of operators
D_{p}
for distinct

p\in\mathcal{M}_{-\frac{1}{2}}
are close to each other in the sense specified
by Proposition
23
. Therefore, it is
useful to know that for some
p

their integral kernels are particularly simple. These are provided in the Appendix
A.3
.

By the essential spectrum (resp. essential spectrum of index zero) of a closed operator
R
we mean the set
\sigma_{\mathrm{ess}}(R)
(resp.
\sigma_{\mathrm{ess},0}(R)
) of all
k\in\mathbb{C}
such that
R-k
is not a Fredholm operator (resp. Fredholm operator of index zero). Clearly
\sigma_{\mathrm{ess}}(R)\subset\sigma_{\mathrm{ess},0}(R)
.

Lemma 24
.

Let
R,S
be closed operators such that there exists
k_{0}
in the intersection of resolvent sets of
R
and
S
such that
(R-k_{0})^{-1}-(S-k_{0})^{-1}
is a compact operator. Then
\sigma_{\mathrm{ess}}(R)=\sigma_{\mathrm{ess}}(S)
and
\sigma_{\mathrm{ess},0}(R)=\sigma_{\mathrm{ess},0}(S)
.

Proof.

By assumption,
(S-k_{0})^{-1}
and
(R-k_{0})^{-1}
have the same essential spectra. The spectral mapping theorem proven in
[
4
]
gives

\displaystyle\sigma_{\mathrm{ess}}(S)

\displaystyle=\{k\in\mathbb{C}\,|\,\exists q\in\sigma_{\mathrm{ess}}((S-k_{0})^{-1})\ (k-k_{0})q=1\}

(
5.23
)

\displaystyle=\{k\in\mathbb{C}\,|\,\exists q\in\sigma_{\mathrm{ess}}((R-k_{0})^{-1})\ (k-k_{0})q=1\}=\sigma_{\mathrm{ess}}(R).

The same argument works also for
\sigma_{\mathrm{ess},0}
.
∎

Corollary 25
.

For any
p\in\mathcal{M}_{{}-\frac{1}{2}}
we have

\sigma_{\mathrm{ess}}(D_{p})=\sigma_{\mathrm{ess},0}(D_{p})=\mathbb{R}
.

Proof.

There exists
p
such that
\sigma(D_{p})=\mathbb{R}
. By Lemma

24
, it is sufficient to prove our statement for such
p
. Clearly,
\sigma_{\mathrm{ess}}(D_{p})\subset\sigma_{\mathrm{ess},0}(D_{p})\subset\sigma(D_{p})=\mathbb{R}
. If
k\in\mathbb{R}
, then
D_{p}-k
is injective and its range is dense, hence not closed, for otherwise
(D_{p}-k)^{-1}
would be bounded.
∎

Corollary 26
.

Let
\omega,\lambda
be such that
|\mathrm{Re}\sqrt{\omega^{2}-\lambda^{2}}|<\frac{1}{2}
. Then
\sigma_{\mathrm{ess}}(D_{\omega,\lambda}^{\min})=\sigma_{\mathrm{ess}}(D_{\omega,\lambda}^{\max})=\mathbb{R}
. If
k\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{R}
, then
D_{\omega,\lambda}^{\min}-k
and
D_{\omega,\lambda}^{\max}-k
are Fredholm operators with indices
-1
and
+1
, respectively. If
D
is an operator satisfying
D_{\omega,\lambda}^{\min}\subsetneq D\subsetneq D_{\omega,\lambda}^{\max}
, then
\sigma_{\mathrm{ess}}(D)=\sigma_{\mathrm{ess},0}(D)=\mathbb{R}
.

Proof.

Follows from Theorem
13
and Corollary
25
.
∎

5.3
Limiting absorption principle

Let
s\in\mathbb{R}
. The
Hilbert space
L^{2}_{s}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathbb{C}^{2})
is defined as the completion of

C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathbb{C}^{2})
with respect to the norm induced by the
scalar product
(f|g)_{s}=\int_{0}^{\infty}(1+x^{2})^{s}\overline{f(x)}g(x)\mathrm{d}x
. For any
t\in\mathbb{R}
we have a
unitary operator
\langle X\rangle^{t}:L^{2}_{s}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathbb{C}^{2})\to L^{2}_{s-t}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathbb{C}^{2})
given by
(\langle X\rangle^{t}f)(x)=(1+x^{2})^{\frac{t}{2}}f(x)
, alternatively regarded as an (unbounded for
t>0
) positive operator on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathbb{C}^{2})
.

Proposition 27
.

Let
p\in\mathcal{M}_{-\frac{1}{2}}\backslash\mathcal{E}^{\pm}
,
k\in\mathbb{R}^{\times}
. The limit
G^{\bowtie}_{p}(k\pm\mathrm{i}0):=\lim\limits_{\epsilon\downarrow 0}G^{\bowtie}_{p}(k\pm\mathrm{i}\epsilon)
exists as a compact operator
L^{2}_{s}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathbb{C}^{2})\to L^{2}_{-s}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathbb{C}^{2})
for any
s>|\mathrm{Im}(\lambda)|+\frac{1}{2}
and depends continuously on
p,k
.

If
\mathrm{Re}(\mu)>0
, then
\mathbb{R}^{\times}
may be replaced by
\mathbb{R}
in the
above statement and
G_{p}^{\bowtie}(\pm\mathrm{i}0)
has the kernel

G_{p}^{\bowtie}(0;x,y)=\frac{1}{2}{\mathchoice{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.5mul}{\rm 1\mskip-5.0mul}}_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}(y-x)\left(\frac{x}{y}\right)^{\mu}\begin{bmatrix}z&-1\\
1&-z^{-1}\end{bmatrix}+\frac{1}{2}{\mathchoice{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.5mul}{\rm 1\mskip-5.0mul}}_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}(x-y)\left(\frac{y}{x}\right)^{\mu}\begin{bmatrix}z&1\\
-1&-z^{-1}\end{bmatrix}.

(5.24)

If
\mathrm{Re}(\mu)\leq 0
, then
\|G_{p}^{\bowtie}(k)\|_{B(L^{2}_{s},L^{2}_{-s})}=O(|k|^{2\mathrm{Re}(\mu)})
for
k\to 0
.

Therefore in both cases we have
\|G_{p}^{\bowtie}(\cdot\pm\mathrm{i}0)\|_{B(L^{2}_{s},L^{2}_{-s})}\in L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R})
.

Proof.

It is sufficient to cover the case of
k
approaching the real axis
from below. Asymptotics of
G^{\bowtie}(k;x,y)
are such that

(1+x^{2})^{-\frac{s}{2}}(1+y^{2})^{-\frac{s}{2}}G^{\bowtie}_{p}(k;x,y)
is an
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2},\mathrm{End}(\mathbb{C}^{2}))
function. Dominated convergence theorem implies
that it depends continuously (in the
L^{2}
sense) on
p,k
, including the boundary set
\mathrm{Im}(k)=0
. Therefore
\langle X\rangle^{-s}G^{\bowtie}_{p}(k)\langle X\rangle^{-s}
is a continuous family of Hilbert-Schmidt (and hence compact) operators on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathbb{C}^{2})
, so
G^{\bowtie}_{p}(k)
defines an operator
L^{2}_{s}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathbb{C}^{2})\to L^{2}_{-s}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathbb{C}^{2})
which may be written as a composition of two unitaries and a compact operator.

The second part follows from the asymptotics of
\xi^{\pm}_{p}
and
\zeta^{\pm}_{p}
functions for small arguments and the dominated convergence theorem.
∎

5.4
Generalized eigenvectors

Point spectrum of
D_{p}
, when present, possesses quite
counter-intuitive properties. Note that in this subsection an
important role is played by the bilinear product

\langle\cdot|\cdot\rangle
.

Proposition 28
.

Let
n,m\in\mathbb{N}
. If
f\in\mathrm{Ker}((D_{p}-k)^{n})
,
g\in\mathrm{Ker}((D_{p}-k^{\prime})^{m})
, then
\langle f|g\rangle=0
.

Proof.

Assume at first that
k^{\prime}\neq k
. We induct on
m
. If
m=1
, then

0=\langle(D_{p}-k)^{n}f|g\rangle=\sum_{j=0}^{n}\binom{n}{j}(k^{\prime}-k)^{n-j}\langle f|(D_{p}-k^{\prime})^{j}g\rangle=(k^{\prime}-k)^{n}\langle f|g\rangle.

(5.25)

Cancelling
(k^{\prime}-k)^{n}
we obtain the induction base. Assume that the
claim is true for
m
and let
g\in\mathrm{Ker}((D_{p}-k^{\prime})^{m+1})
. By a similar calculation

0=\sum_{j=0}^{n}\binom{n}{j}(k^{\prime}-k)^{n-j}\langle f|(D_{p}-k^{\prime})^{j}g\rangle=(k-k^{\prime})^{n}\langle f|g\rangle,

(5.26)

where the last equality follows from
(D_{p}-k^{\prime})^{j}g\in\mathrm{Ker}((D_{p}-k^{\prime})^{m})
for
j\geq 1
and the induction hypothesis. This
completes the proof for
k\neq k^{\prime}
.

So far we used only the self-transposedness of
D_{p}
. Next
we will also use
its homogeneity.

Let
k^{\prime}=k
. Then for any
\tau\in\mathbb{R}^{\times}
we have
U_{\tau}g\in\mathrm{Ker}((D_{p}-k^{\prime\prime})^{m})
for some
k^{\prime\prime}\neq k
. Hence
\langle f|U_{\tau}g\rangle=0
. Now take
\tau\to 0
.
∎

Proposition 29
.

If
p\in\mathcal{E}^{\pm}
and
k\in\mathbb{C}_{\pm}
, then for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
we have
\dim(\mathrm{Ker}((D_{p}-k)^{n}))=n
.

Proof.

We proceed by induction on
n
. Case
n=0
is trivial and
n=1
is
already established. By the inductive hypothesis, there exists
f\in\mathrm{Ker}((D_{p}-k)^{n})\backslash\mathrm{Ker}((D_{p}-k)^{n-1})
, unique up to elements
of
\mathrm{Ker}((D_{p}-k)^{n-1})
and multiplication by nonzero scalars. Then

f\in\mathrm{Ker}(D_{p}-k)^{\mathrm{perp}}
by Proposition
28
. On the other hand

\mathrm{Ker}(D_{p}-k)^{\mathrm{perp}}=\left(\mathrm{Ran}(D_{p}-k)^{\mathrm{perp}}\right)^{\mathrm{perp}}=\mathrm{Ran}(D_{p}-k)
. Here the last equality holds because
D_{p}-k
has closed range, see Corollary
25
. Thus there exists
g\in\mathrm{Dom}(D_{p}-k)
, unique up to elements of
\mathrm{Ker}(D_{p}-k)
, such that
(D_{p}-k)g=f
. Clearly,
g\in\mathrm{Ker}((D_{p}-k)^{n+1})\backslash\mathrm{Ker}((D_{p}-k)^{n})
and we have a vector space decomposition
\mathrm{Ker}((D_{p}-k)^{n+1})=\mathbb{C}g\oplus\mathrm{Ker}((D_{p}-k)^{n})
.
∎

Question 1
.

Let
p\in\mathcal{E}^{\pm}
,
k\in\mathbb{C}_{\pm}
. We denote the
L^{2}
closure of
\bigcup\limits_{n=0}^{\infty}\mathrm{Ker}((D_{p}-k)^{n})
by
\mathcal{N}_{p}(k)
. By Lemma
28
we have
\mathcal{N}_{p}(k)\subset\mathcal{N}_{p}(k)^{\mathrm{perp}}
. In Appendix
A.3
we have verified that in the case
\omega=0
subspace
\mathcal{N}_{p}(k)
does not depend on the choice of
k\in\mathbb{C}_{\pm}
and
\mathcal{N}_{p}(k)=\mathcal{N}_{p}(k)^{\mathrm{perp}}
(equivalently,
\mathcal{N}_{p}(k)\oplus\overline{\mathcal{N}_{p}(k)}=L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathbb{C}^{2})
). We leave open the question whether these assertions remain true for
\omega\neq 0
.

6
Diagonalization

Let
k\in\mathbb{R}^{\times}
. Recall that
\varepsilon_{k}=\mathrm{sgn}(\mathrm{Re}(k))
.
On
the real line,
it is convenient to rewrite the formulas for
\xi^{\pm}
and
\zeta^{\pm}

(
3.13
,
3.15
)
in terms of trigonometric Whittaker functions
(
D.28
,
D.31
):

\displaystyle\xi_{p}^{\pm}(k,x)=\frac{\mathrm{i}^{\mp\varepsilon_{k}\mu}}{2N_{p}^{\pm}\mu}\left(\varepsilon_{k}\omega\mathcal{J}_{\varepsilon_{k}\lambda,\mu+\frac{1}{2}}(2|k|x)\begin{bmatrix}-z\\
1\end{bmatrix}+\mathcal{J}_{\varepsilon_{k}\lambda,\mu-\frac{1}{2}}(2|k|x)\begin{bmatrix}z\\
1\end{bmatrix}\right),

(6.1a)

\displaystyle\zeta_{p}^{\pm}(k,x)=\frac{\mathrm{i}^{\pm\varepsilon_{k}\mu}}{2}\left(\pm\mathrm{i}\varepsilon_{k}(z\pm\mathrm{i})\mathcal{H}^{\pm\varepsilon_{k}}_{\varepsilon_{k}\lambda,\mu+\frac{1}{2}}(2|k|x)\begin{bmatrix}-1\\
z^{-1}\end{bmatrix}+(z\mp\mathrm{i})\mathcal{H}^{\pm\varepsilon_{k}}_{\varepsilon_{k}\lambda,\mu-\frac{1}{2}}(2|k|x)\begin{bmatrix}1\\
z^{-1}\end{bmatrix}\right).

(6.1b)

For
\mu
near
0
it is convenient instead of
(
6.1a
) to use a version of (
3.16a
):

\displaystyle\xi_{p}^{\pm}(k,x)=\frac{\mathrm{i}^{\mp\varepsilon_{k}\mu}}{2N_{p}^{\pm}}\left(\varepsilon_{k}\mathcal{J}_{\varepsilon_{k}\lambda,\mu+\frac{1}{2}}(2|k|x)\begin{bmatrix}1\\
-z\end{bmatrix}+\frac{\mathcal{J}_{\varepsilon_{k}\lambda,\mu-\frac{1}{2}}(2|k|x)+\varepsilon_{k}\lambda\mathcal{J}_{\varepsilon_{k}\lambda,\mu+\frac{1}{2}}(2|k|x)}{\mu}\begin{bmatrix}z\\
1\end{bmatrix}\right).

(6.2)

The leading terms of
\xi_{p}^{\pm}
and
\zeta_{p}^{\pm}
for large
kx
are

\displaystyle\xi_{p}^{\pm}(k,x)

\displaystyle\sim\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\varepsilon_{k}\pi\lambda}{2}}}{2}\left(\mathrm{e}^{\mp\mathrm{i}kx}(2|k|x)^{\mp\mathrm{i}\lambda}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
\mp\mathrm{i}\end{bmatrix}+(S_{p}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\varepsilon_{k}\pi\mu})^{\pm 1}\mathrm{e}^{\pm\mathrm{i}kx}(2|k|x)^{\pm\mathrm{i}\lambda}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
\pm\mathrm{i}\end{bmatrix}\right),

(6.3a)

\displaystyle\zeta_{p}^{\pm}(k,x)

\displaystyle\sim\mp\mathrm{i}\,\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\varepsilon_{k}\pi\lambda}{2}}\mathrm{e}^{\pm\mathrm{i}kx}(2|k|x)^{\pm\mathrm{i}\lambda}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
\pm\mathrm{i}\end{bmatrix}.

(6.3b)

Because of the long-range nature of the perturbation and of the
presence of spin degrees of freedom, it is not obvious what should be chosen as the definition of the outgoing and incoming waves. Let us call

\mathrm{i}\zeta^{+}(k,x)
the
outgoing
wave

and
-\mathrm{i}\zeta^{-}(k,x)
the

incoming wave
.
Then the ratio of the outgoing wave and the incoming wave in
\xi^{+}(k,x)

is

\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\varepsilon_{k}\mu}S_{p}

and can be called the
(full) scattering amplitude at energy
k
.

Proposition 30
.

Let
p\in\mathcal{M}_{-\frac{1}{2}}\backslash(\mathcal{E}^{+}\cup\mathcal{E}^{-})
,

k\in\mathbb{R}^{\times}
,
s>|\mathrm{Im}(\lambda)|+\frac{1}{2}
. Then the spectral density

\Pi_{p}(k):=(2\pi\mathrm{i})^{-1}\left(G^{\bowtie}_{p}(k+\mathrm{i}0)-G^{\bowtie}_{p}(k-\mathrm{i}0)\right)

(6.4)

is
well defined as a compact operator
L^{2}_{s}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathbb{C}^{2})\to L^{2}_{-s}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathbb{C}^{2})
and has the integral kernel

\Pi_{p}(k;x,y)=\frac{\mathrm{e}^{\varepsilon_{k}\pi\lambda}}{\pi}\xi_{p}^{+}(k,x)\xi_{p}^{-}(k,y)^{\mathrm{T}}=\frac{\mathrm{e}^{\varepsilon_{k}\pi\lambda}}{\pi}\xi_{p}^{-}(k,x)\xi_{p}^{+}(k,y)^{\mathrm{T}}.

(6.5)

As
k\to 0
, it admits the expansion

\Pi_{p}(k)=\mathrm{e}^{\varepsilon_{k}\pi\lambda}|k|^{2\mu}\Pi_{p}^{0}+O(|k|^{2\mu+1}),

(6.6)

where the remainder is estimated in the
B(L^{2}_{s},L^{2}_{-s})
norm and

\Pi_{p}^{0}
has the integral kernel

\Pi_{p}^{0}(x,y)=\frac{(4xy)^{\mu}}{\pi\,\Gamma(2\mu+1)^{2}N_{p}^{+}N_{p}^{-}}\begin{bmatrix}z^{2}&z\\
z&1\end{bmatrix}.

(6.7)

Proof.

The first statement follows from Proposition
27
. By (
3.27
), it is sufficient to prove (
6.5
) for
x<y
. Plugging (
3.21a
) into (
3.26
) we find

G_{p}^{\bowtie}(k+\mathrm{i}0;x,y)-G_{p}^{\bowtie}(k-\mathrm{i}0;x,y)=\xi_{p}^{-}(k,x)\left(\zeta_{p}^{-}(k,y)-\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\varepsilon_{k}\pi\mu}S_{p}\zeta_{p}^{+}(k,y)\right)^{\mathrm{T}}.

(6.8)

Plugging in (
3.21b
) we obtain (
6.5
).

The last part of the statement follows from asymptotics of
\xi
functions for small arguments and the dominated convergence theorem.
∎

We refer to Appendix
C
for definitions used in
the lemma below. Note also the identity

\xi_{p}^{\pm}(k,x)=\xi_{p}^{\pm}(\varepsilon_{k},|k|x)
, which allows us to
restrict our attention to

\xi_{p}^{\pm}(\varepsilon_{k},x)
. The following fact follows immediately from Lemma
73
and (
6.2
).

Lemma 31
.

\xi_{p}^{\pm}(\varepsilon_{k},x)
,
p\notin\mathcal{E}^{\pm}
, is a tempered
distribution in
x\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
, in the sense explained
in Appendix
C
. Its
Mellin transform is

\displaystyle\Xi_{p}^{\pm}(\varepsilon_{k},s):=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{e}^{-0x}x^{-\frac{1}{2}-\mathrm{i}s}\xi_{p}^{\pm}(\varepsilon_{k},x)\mathrm{d}x

(6.9)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathrm{i}^{\mp\varepsilon_{k}\mu-\frac{3}{2}-\mu+\mathrm{i}s}2^{\mu-1}\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{2}+\mu-\mathrm{i}s\right)\frac{1}{N_{p}^{\pm}\mu}

\displaystyle\times

\displaystyle\Bigg(2\varepsilon_{k}\omega\Big(\frac{1}{2}+\mu-\mathrm{i}s\Big)\,{}_{2}\mathbf{F}_{1}\left(1+\mu+\mathrm{i}\varepsilon_{k}\lambda,{\scriptstyle\frac{3}{2}}+\mu-\mathrm{i}s;2\mu+2;2+\mathrm{i}0\right)\begin{bmatrix}-z\\
1\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle+\mathrm{i}\,{}_{2}\mathbf{F}_{1}\left(\mu+\mathrm{i}\varepsilon_{k}\lambda,{\scriptstyle\frac{1}{2}}+\mu-\mathrm{i}s;2\mu;2+\mathrm{i}0\right)\begin{bmatrix}z\\
1\end{bmatrix}\Bigg),

is analytic in
s
and bounded by
c_{p}^{\pm}(1+s^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}|\mathrm{Im}(\lambda)|}
locally uniformly in
p
.

We define
\mathcal{U}_{p}^{\pm,\mathrm{pre}}
,
p\in\mathcal{M}\backslash\mathcal{E}^{\pm}
, as
the integral operator
C_{\mathrm{c}}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathbb{C}^{2})\to C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})

with the kernel

\mathcal{U}^{\pm}_{p}(k,x)=\frac{\mathrm{e}^{\frac{1}{2}\varepsilon_{k}\pi\lambda}}{\sqrt{\pi}}\xi_{p}^{\pm}(k,x)^{\mathrm{T}}.

(6.10)

By construction, the kernel of the spectral density operator factors as

\Pi_{p}(k;x,y)=\mathcal{U}_{p}^{+}(k,x)^{\mathrm{T}}\mathcal{U}_{p}^{-}(k,y)=\mathcal{U}_{p}^{-}(k,x)^{\mathrm{T}}\mathcal{U}_{p}^{+}(k,y).

(6.11)

We note also the relations

\mathcal{U}_{p}^{+}(k,x)=\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\varepsilon_{k}\pi\mu}S_{p}\,\mathcal{U}_{p}^{-}(k,x),\qquad\overline{\mathcal{U}_{\overline{p}}^{\pm}(k,x)}=\mathcal{U}_{p}^{\mp}(k,x)

(6.12)

and the intertwining property

(\mathcal{U}_{p}^{\pm,\mathrm{pre}}D_{p}f)(k)=k(\mathcal{U}_{p}^{\pm,\mathrm{pre}}f)(k),\qquad f\in C_{\mathrm{c}}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathbb{C}^{2}).

(6.13)

Recall from Subsection

1.1
that
J
is the inversion and
A
is the generator of
dilations, and
K
is the multiplication operator on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})

by the variable
k\in\mathbb{R}
.

Below we will consider level sets
\{\lambda=\lambda_{0}\}\subset\mathcal{M}_{\frac{1}{2}}
. Recall from the discussion around equation
(
2.9
) that it is a submanifold for

\lambda_{0}\neq 0
, but for
\lambda_{0}=0
it is the union of three submanifolds singular along the intersection. We will say that a function on the locus
\{\lambda=0\}
is holomorphic if its restriction to each of the three components is holomorphic.

Proposition 32
.

\mathcal{U}_{p}^{\pm,\mathrm{pre}}
are densely defined closable operators
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathbb{C}^{2})\to L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
with the closures given by

\mathcal{U}_{p}^{\pm}f(k)=\frac{\mathrm{e}^{\frac{1}{2}\varepsilon_{k}\pi\lambda}}{\sqrt{\pi}}\Xi_{p}^{\pm\mathrm{T}}(\varepsilon_{k},A)Jf(|k|),\qquad k\in\mathbb{R}.

(6.14)

Hence
\mathcal{U}^{\pm}_{p}(1+A^{2})^{-\frac{1}{2}|\mathrm{Im}(\lambda)|}
is bounded. In
particular
\mathcal{U}^{\pm}_{p}
are bounded if
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
. If
\lambda_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
, they form a holomorphic
family of operators on the level set

\{\lambda=\lambda_{0}\}\backslash\mathcal{E}^{\pm}
.
Furthermore,
\mathcal{U}_{p}^{\pm*}=\mathcal{U}_{\overline{p}}^{\mp\mathrm{T}}
.

Proof.

The first part follows from Lemma
31
and discussion in Appendix
C
. Now fix
\lambda_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
and consider
p
in a component
S
of the level set
\{\lambda=\lambda_{0}\}\backslash\mathcal{E}^{\pm}
. If
f\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
,
g\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathbb{C}^{2})
, then
(f|\mathcal{U}_{p}^{\pm}g)
is a holomorphic function of
p\in S
. Since
C_{c}^{\infty}
spaces are dense in
L^{2}
and
\mathcal{U}_{p}^{\pm}
are bounded locally uniformly in
p
,
\mathcal{U}_{p}^{\pm}
is a holomorphic operator-valued function. The last claim follows from the formula (
6.12
).
∎

In a sense, operators
\mathcal{U}^{\pm}_{p}
diagonalize
D_{p}
for
p\in\mathcal{M}_{-\frac{1}{2}}\backslash\mathcal{E}^{\pm}
. If
p=\overline{p}
, then
D_{p}
are
self-adjoint and
\mathcal{U}_{p}^{\pm}
are unitary. If we assume only that

\lambda
is real, then
\mathcal{U}_{p}^{\pm}
are still bounded with bounded inverses, so they are almost as good as in the self-adjoint case. This will be made precise below.

Proposition 33
.

If
p=\overline{p}
, then for any bounded interval
[a,b]\subset\mathbb{R}
and
f,g\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathbb{C}^{2})

(g|{\mathchoice{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.5mul}{\rm 1\mskip-5.0mul}}_{[a,b]}(D_{p})f)=\int_{a}^{b}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}g(x)^{*}\Pi_{p}(k;x,y)f(y)\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}k.

(6.15)

Besides,
\mathcal{U}_{p}^{\pm}
is a unitary operator and

D_{p}=\mathcal{U}_{p}^{\pm*}K\mathcal{U}_{p}^{\pm}.

(6.16)

Proof.

Since the point spectrum of
D_{p}
is trivial for
p=\overline{p}
, Stone’s formula gives

(g|{\mathchoice{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.5mul}{\rm 1\mskip-5.0mul}}_{[a,b]}(D_{p})f)=\lim_{\epsilon\downarrow 0}\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{[a,b]\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}}g(x)^{*}(G_{p}^{\bowtie}(k+\mathrm{i}\epsilon;x,y)-G_{p}^{\bowtie}(k-\mathrm{i}\epsilon;x,y))f(y)\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}k.

(6.17)

It follows from the asymptotics of functions
\xi_{p}^{\pm}
and

\zeta_{p}
that on
[a,b]\times\mathrm{supp}(f)\times\mathrm{supp}(g)
we have

|G_{p}^{\bowtie}(k\pm\mathrm{i}\epsilon;x,y)|\leq c|k|^{\mathrm{Re}(\mu)-|\mathrm{Re}(\mu)|}
with
c
independent of
k
. This function is
integrable, because
\mathrm{Re}(\mu)-|\mathrm{Re}(\mu)|>-1
. Therefore by the
dominated convergence theorem, the limit
\epsilon\downarrow 0
may
be taken under the integral.
This proves (
6.15
).

Let us prove the unitarity of
\mathcal{U}_{p}^{\pm}
.
Let
f\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathbb{C}^{2})
and let
[a,b]
be a bounded interval. Then

\displaystyle\int_{a}^{b}|\mathcal{U}_{p}^{\pm}f(k)|^{2}\mathrm{d}k

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{a}^{b}\int_{0}^{\infty}f(x)^{*}\Pi_{p}(k;x,y)f(y)\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}k\mathrm{d}x

(6.18)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}f(x)^{*}({\mathchoice{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.5mul}{\rm 1\mskip-5.0mul}}_{[a,b]}(D_{p})f)(x)\mathrm{d}x=(f|{\mathchoice{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.5mul}{\rm 1\mskip-5.0mul}}_{[a,b]}(D_{p})f),

where in the first step we used the definition of
\mathcal{U}_{p}^{\pm}
,
conjugation formula (
6.12
) and the
factorization (
6.11
). The order of integrals is immaterial, because the integrand is compactly supported and its only possible singularity (at
k=0
, if
0\in[a,b]
) is integrable. In the second step we used Proposition
33
. Taking the limit
b\to\infty
,
a\to-\infty
we find

\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}|\mathcal{U}_{p}^{\pm}f(k)|^{2}\mathrm{d}k=(f|f).

(6.19)

Hence
\mathcal{U}_{p}^{\pm}
is an isometry. Equation (
6.18
) implies that

{\mathchoice{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.5mul}{\rm 1\mskip-5.0mul}}_{[a,b]}(D_{p})=\mathcal{U}_{p}^{\pm*}{\mathchoice{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.5mul}{\rm 1\mskip-5.0mul}}_{[a,b]}(K)\mathcal{U}_{p}^{\pm}.

(6.20)

It remains to show that
\mathcal{U}_{p}^{\pm}\mathcal{U}_{p}^{\pm*}=1
. The proof of
this fact follows closely the proof of (3.37) of Theorem 3.16 in

[
13
]
.
∎

Proposition 34
.

If
p\in\mathcal{M}_{-\frac{1}{2}}\backslash(\mathcal{E}^{+}\cup\mathcal{E}^{-})
is such that

\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
, then
(\mathcal{U}_{p}^{\pm})^{-1}=\mathcal{U}^{\mp\mathrm{T}}
and

\displaystyle D_{p}=\mathcal{U}_{p}^{\pm-1}K\mathcal{U}_{p}^{\pm}.

(6.21a)

In particular
D_{p}
is similar to a self-adjoint operator.

Proof.

We fix
\lambda_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
. Then
\mathcal{U}_{p}^{\pm}\mathcal{U}_{p}^{\mp\mathrm{T}}-1
and
\mathcal{U}_{p}^{\pm\mathrm{T}}\mathcal{U}_{p}^{\mp}-1
form
holomorphic families of bounded operators on (one-dimensional)
\{\lambda=\lambda_{0}\}\backslash(\mathcal{E}^{+}\cup\mathcal{E}^{-})
. They vanish on the set of real points, which has an accumulation point in each component of the domain. Thus they vanish everywhere.

Now take
k\in\mathbb{C}\backslash\mathbb{R}
. Arguing as in the previous paragraph we obtain

\mathcal{U}_{p}^{\pm-1}(K-k)^{-1}\mathcal{U}_{p}^{\pm}=(D_{p}-k)^{-1},

(6.22)

from which (
6.21a
) follows immediately.
∎

Question 2
.

If
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
, then
D_{p}
is similar to a
self-adjoint operator. Hence it enjoys a very good functional
calculus–for any bounded Borel function
f
the operator
f(D_{p})
is well defined
and bounded.

If
\lambda\notin\mathbb{R}
this is probably no longer true, because the
diagonalizing operators
\mathcal{U}_{p}^{\pm}
are unbounded. However, they are
unbounded in a controlled manner: they are continuous on the domain
of some power of the dilation operator. One may hope that this is
sufficient to allow for a rich functional calculus for Dirac-Coulomb
Hamiltonians. We pose an open problem: for a given
\mathrm{Im}(\lambda)
,
characterize
functions that allow for a functional calculus for
D_{p}
.
In particular, one could ask when
\mathrm{i}D_{p}
generates a
C^{0}
semigroup of bounded operators.

7
Numerical range and dissipative properties

In this section we give a complete analysis
of the numerical range of various
realizations of 1d Dirac-Coulomb Hamiltonians studied in this paper.

Proposition 35
.

One of the following mutually exclusive statements is true:

1.

\omega
and
\lambda
are real. Then
\mathrm{Num}(D_{\omega,\lambda}^{\mathrm{pre}})=\mathbb{R}
.

2.

|\mathrm{Im}(\omega)|<|\mathrm{Im}(\lambda)|
. Then
\mathrm{Num}(D_{\omega,\lambda}^{\mathrm{pre}})=\mathbb{C}_{-\mathrm{sgn}(\mathrm{Im}(\lambda))}
.

3.

|\mathrm{Im}(\omega)|=|\mathrm{Im}(\lambda)|\neq 0
. Then
\mathrm{Num}(D_{\omega,\lambda}^{\mathrm{pre}})=\mathbb{C}_{-\mathrm{sgn}(\mathrm{Im}(\lambda))}\cup\{0\}
.

4.

|\mathrm{Im}(\omega)|>|\mathrm{Im}(\lambda)|
. Then
\mathrm{Num}(D_{\omega,\lambda}^{\mathrm{pre}})=\mathbb{C}
.

The same is true with
D_{\omega,\lambda}^{\mathrm{pre}}
replaced by
D_{\omega,\lambda}^{\min}
throughout.

Proof.

Integrating by parts we find that for
f=\begin{bmatrix}f_{1}\\
f_{2}\end{bmatrix}\in C_{\mathrm{c}}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathbb{C}^{2})
we have

\mathrm{Im}(f|D_{\omega,\lambda}f)=-\mathrm{Im}(\lambda+\omega)\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{|f_{1}(x)|^{2}}{x}\mathrm{d}x-\mathrm{Im}(\lambda-\omega)\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{|f_{2}(x)|^{2}}{x}\mathrm{d}x.

(7.1)

In the four cases listed in the proposition we have: both terms are zero in Case
1.
, both terms are nonzero (except for
f=0
) and have the same sign as
-\mathrm{Im}(\lambda)
in Case
2.
, one term is zero and the other has the same sign as
-\mathrm{Im}(\lambda)
in Case
3.
and the two terms have opposite signs in the last case. Therefore inclusions of numerical ranges in the specified sets are clear, except for the third case. Then in order for
\mathrm{Im}(f|D_{\omega,\lambda}f)
to vanish, one of the two
f_{j}
has to be zero. It is easy to check that this implies
(f|D_{\omega,\lambda}f)=0
(but not
f=0
).

We have to show that the obtained inclusions are saturated. The
homogeneity of
D_{\omega,\lambda}^{\mathrm{pre}}
implies that

\mathrm{Num}(D_{\omega,\lambda}^{\mathrm{pre}})
is a convex cone. Thus to establish
the result in Case
1.
it is sufficient to show that both signs of
(f|D_{\omega,\lambda}f)
are possible. We choose a nonzero
\varphi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathbb{C}^{2})
with
\|\varphi\|_{H_{0}^{1}}=1
and put
f_{\pm,t}(x)=\begin{bmatrix}\varphi(x-t)\\
\pm\varphi^{\prime}(x-t)\end{bmatrix}
for
t\geq 0
. Then
\|f_{\pm,t}\|_{L^{2}}=1
and

(f_{\pm,t}|D_{\omega,\lambda}f_{\pm,t})=\pm 2\int_{0}^{\infty}|\varphi^{\prime}(x)|^{2}\mathrm{d}x-\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{x+t}\left((\lambda+\omega)|\varphi(x)|^{2}+(\lambda-\omega)|\varphi^{\prime}(x)|^{2}\right)\mathrm{d}x.

(7.2)

The first term is nonzero, has sign
\pm
and does not depend on
t
, while the other converges to zero for
t\to\infty
. Therefore
\pm(f_{\pm,t}|D_{\omega,\lambda}f_{\pm,t})\geq c_{\pm}>0
for large enough
t
.

Next we suppose that
|\mathrm{Im}(\omega)|\leq|\mathrm{Im}(\lambda)|\neq 0
. It
is sufficient to show that
\mathbb{C}_{-}
is included in the numerical range
for
\mathrm{Im}(\lambda)<0
. Arguing as below (
7.2
), we deduce
that there exist constants
t_{0}>0
and
c_{\pm}>0
such that
\pm\mathrm{Re}\,(f_{\pm,t}|D_{\omega,\lambda}f_{\pm,t})\geq c_{\pm}

for
t\geq t_{0}
. Let
\delta=\mathrm{Im}\,(f_{\pm,t_{0}}|D_{\omega,\lambda}f_{\pm,t_{0}})
. Then
\delta>0
. The function
t\mapsto\mathrm{Im}\,(f_{\pm,t}|D_{\omega,\lambda}f_{\pm,t})
is continuous and converges to zero for
t\to\infty
, so for every
\epsilon\in]0,\delta]
there exists
t\geq t_{0}
such that
\mathrm{Im}\,(f_{\pm,t}|D_{\omega,\lambda}f_{\pm,t})=\epsilon
. By convexity of numerical ranges this implies
[-c_{-}+\mathrm{i}\epsilon,c_{+}+\mathrm{i}\epsilon]\subset\mathrm{Num}(D_{\omega,\lambda})
. Homogeneity implies that for every
s>0
we have
\left[-\frac{c_{-}s}{\epsilon}+\mathrm{i}s,\frac{c_{+}s}{\epsilon}+\mathrm{i}s\right]\subset\mathrm{Num}(D_{\omega,\lambda})
. Every
k
with
\mathrm{Im}(k)=s
is in this interval for small enough
\epsilon
.

Similar argument shows that in Case
4.
there exist
c_{\pm}>0
and
\delta>0
such that for every
\epsilon\in]0,\delta]
there exist
g_{\pm,\epsilon}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathbb{C}^{2})
with
\|g_{\pm,\epsilon}\|_{L^{2}}=1
,
\pm\mathrm{Re}(g_{\pm,\epsilon}|D_{\omega,\lambda}g_{\pm,\epsilon})\geq c_{\pm}
and
\left|\mathrm{Im}(g_{\pm,\epsilon}|D_{\omega,\lambda}g_{\pm,\epsilon})\right|\leq\epsilon
. On the other hand for nonzero
f\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathbb{C}^{2})
with
f_{1}=0
or
f_{2}=0
we have that
(f|D_{\omega,\lambda}f)
is proportional to
\omega-\lambda
or
-\omega-\lambda
, respectively, with a positive proportionality constant. Using homogeneity we can even construct functions
f
with the proportionality constant equal to
1
and
\|f\|=1
. Next we observe that if
\epsilon
is taken to be sufficiently small, the convex hull of
(g_{+,\epsilon}|D_{\omega,\lambda}g_{+,\epsilon})
,
(g_{-,\epsilon}|D_{\omega,\lambda}g_{-,\epsilon})
,
\omega-\lambda
and
-\omega-\lambda
contains zero in its interior. Therefore the smallest convex cone containing it is the whole
\mathbb{C}
.

To prove the last statement, first note that
\mathrm{Num}(D_{\omega,\lambda}^{\min})
is contained in the closure of
\mathrm{Num}(D_{\omega,\lambda}^{\mathrm{pre}})
. Therefore in Cases
1.
and
4.
there is nothing to prove. We consider Case
2.
We have to show that if
g\in\mathrm{Dom}(D_{\omega,\lambda}^{\min})
is such that
\mathrm{Im}(g|D_{\omega,\lambda}g)=0
, then
g=0
. We choose
\epsilon>0
and
f\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathbb{C}^{2})
such that
\|f-g\|_{\mathrm{Dom}(D_{\omega,\lambda}^{\min})}<\epsilon
. Then

\mathrm{Im}(f|D_{\omega,\lambda}f)=\mathrm{Im}\left((g|D_{\omega,\lambda}^{\min}(f-g))+(f-g|D_{\omega,\lambda}^{\min}g)+(f-g|D_{\omega,\lambda}^{\min}(f-g))\right),

(7.3)

so
|\mathrm{Im}(f|D_{\omega,\lambda}f)|\leq 2\epsilon\|g\|_{\mathrm{Dom}(D_{\omega,\lambda}^{\min})}+\epsilon^{2}
. On the other hand for any
t>0
we have

\displaystyle|\mathrm{Im}(f|D_{\omega,\lambda}f)|\geq\frac{|\mathrm{Im}(\lambda+\omega)|}{t}\int_{0}^{t}|f_{1}(x)|^{2}\mathrm{d}x+\frac{|\mathrm{Im}(\lambda-\omega)|}{t}\int_{0}^{t}|f_{2}(x)|^{2}\mathrm{d}x

(
7.4
)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{|\mathrm{Im}(\lambda+\omega)|}{t}\left(\int_{0}^{t}|g_{1}(x)|^{2}\mathrm{d}x-2\epsilon\|g\|^{2}\right)+\frac{|\mathrm{Im}(\lambda-\omega)|}{t}\left(\int_{0}^{t}|g_{2}(x)|^{2}\mathrm{d}x-2\epsilon\|g\|^{2}\right).

Comparing the two derived inequalities and taking
\epsilon\to 0
we find that

\int_{0}^{t}|g_{1}(x)|^{2}\mathrm{d}x=\int_{0}^{t}|g_{2}(x)|^{2}\mathrm{d}x=0.

(7.5)

Since
t
was arbitrary,
g=0
. Case
3.
may be handled analogously.
∎

It is convenient to describe the numerical ranges of operators
D_{p}

in terms of
[a:b]
. It can be related to parameters
\omega,\lambda,\mu
by recalling that
[a:b]=[-\mu:\omega+\lambda]

if
\omega+\lambda\neq 0
and
[a:b]=[\omega-\lambda:-\mu]

if
\omega-\lambda\neq 0
. No such expression exists on the zero
fiber. We will also choose a representative
(a,b)\in[a:b]
. We note
that the condition
\mathrm{Im}(\overline{b}a)=0
is equivalent to the existence of a real representative
(a,b)
, which is also equivalent to the statement that
[a:b]
belongs to the real projective line
\mathbb{RP}^{1}
. If
[a:b]\notin\mathbb{RP}^{1}
, then
\mathrm{sgn}(\mathrm{Im}(\overline{b}a))=\mathrm{sgn}\left(\mathrm{Im}\left(\frac{a}{b}\right)\right)
.

Proposition 36
.

The numerical range of
D_{p}
may be characterized as follows.

1.

If
\omega,\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
and
[a:b]\notin\mathbb{RP}^{1}
, then
\mathrm{Num}(D_{p})=\mathbb{R}\cup\mathbb{C}_{-\mathrm{Im}\left(\overline{b}a\right)}
.

2.

If
\mathrm{Re}(\mu)=0
and
\mathrm{Im}(\overline{b}a)\mathrm{Im}(\lambda)<0
, then
\mathrm{Num}(D_{p})=\mathbb{C}
.

3.

If
\mathrm{Re}(\mu)<0
and
[a:b]\notin\mathbb{RP}^{1}
, then
\mathrm{Num}(D_{p})=\mathbb{C}
.

4.

In every other case
\mathrm{Num}(D_{p})=\mathrm{Num}(D_{\omega,\lambda}^{\min})
.

Proof.

If
p=\overline{p}
, then
D_{p}
is self-adjoint, so
\mathrm{Num}(D_{p})\subset\mathbb{R}=\mathrm{Num}(D_{\omega,\lambda}^{\min})\subset\mathrm{Num}(D_{p})
. If
|\mathrm{Im}(\omega)|>|\mathrm{Im}(\lambda)|
, then
\mathbb{C}=\mathrm{Num}(D_{\omega,\lambda}^{\min})\subset\mathrm{Num}(D_{p})
.

Let
\eta(x)=x^{\mu}\begin{bmatrix}a\\
b\end{bmatrix}
and consider
f=\begin{bmatrix}f_{1}\\
f_{2}\end{bmatrix}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathbb{C}^{2})+\mathrm{span}\{\chi\eta\}
. Then

\mathrm{Im}(f|D_{p}f)=\mathrm{Im}\int_{0}^{\infty}\left[\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}x}\left(\overline{f_{2}(x)}f_{1}(x)\right)-\frac{(\lambda+\omega)|f_{1}(x)|^{2}+(\lambda-\omega)|f_{2}(x)|^{2}}{x}\right]\mathrm{d}x.

(7.6)

By construction, there exist
x_{0}>0
and
c\in\mathbb{C}
such that for
x<x_{0}
we have
f(x)=c\,\eta(x)
, and hence
\overline{f_{2}(x)}f_{1}(x)=\mathrm{Im}(\overline{b}a)x^{2\mathrm{Re}(\mu)}
. If
\mathrm{Re}(\mu)>0
or
\mathrm{Im}(\overline{b}a)=0
(which is equivalent to
[a:b]\in\mathbb{RP}^{1}\subset\mathbb{CP}^{1}
), then
\overline{f_{2}(x)}f_{1}(x)
vanishes for
x
sufficiently large and for
x\to 0
. Therefore
\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}x}\left(\overline{f_{2}(x)}f_{1}(x)\right)\mathrm{d}x=0
and the proof goes as for Proposition
35
.

We consider the case
\mathrm{Re}(\mu)=0
and
\mathrm{Im}(\overline{b}a)\neq 0
. Then

\mathrm{Im}(f|D_{p}f)=-|c|^{2}\mathrm{Im}(\overline{b}a)-\mathrm{Im}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{(\lambda+\omega)|f_{1}(x)|^{2}+(\lambda-\omega)|f_{2}(x)|^{2}}{x}\mathrm{d}x.

(7.7)

If
\omega,\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
, then
\mathrm{Im}(f|D_{p}f)=-|c|^{2}\mathrm{Im}(\overline{b}a)
and we have
\mathbb{R}=\mathrm{Num}(D_{\omega,\lambda}^{\min})\subset\mathrm{Num}(D_{p})
, so
\mathrm{Num}(D_{p})=\{k\in\mathbb{C}\,|\,\mathrm{Im}(\overline{b}a)\mathrm{Im}(k)\leq 0\}
. In the case
|\mathrm{Im}(\omega)|\leq|\mathrm{Im}(\lambda)|\neq 0
there are two possibilities. If
\mathrm{Im}(\overline{b}a)\mathrm{Im}(\lambda)>0
, then all terms in (
7.7
) have the same sign and one has
\mathrm{Num}(D_{p})=\mathrm{Num}(D_{\omega,\lambda}^{\min})
. Otherwise
\mathrm{Num}(D_{p})=\mathbb{C}
. Indeed, consider
f=\frac{\chi\eta}{\|\chi\eta\|}
with shrinking support of
\chi\geq 0
. A simple calculation shows that for these functions the integrand in (
7.7
) vanishes, while the first term grows without bound.

Next, we suppose that
\mathrm{Re}(\mu)<0
,
\mathrm{Im}(\overline{b}a)\neq 0
. Put
f=\varphi\eta
with
\varphi\in C^{\infty}([0,\infty[,\mathbb{R})
vanishing exponentially at infinity. Then
f\in\mathrm{Dom}(D_{p})
and
(D_{p}f)(x)=\varphi^{\prime}(x)\begin{bmatrix}0&-1\\
1&0\end{bmatrix}\eta(x)
. Thus

(f|D_{p}f)=2\mathrm{i}\,\mathrm{Im}(\overline{b}a)\int_{0}^{\infty}\varphi(x)\varphi^{\prime}(x)x^{2\mathrm{Re}(\mu)}\mathrm{d}x.

(7.8)

If
\varphi\neq 0
vanishes at zero, the integral is positive, as can be seen by integrating by parts:

\int_{0}^{\infty}\varphi(x)\varphi^{\prime}(x)x^{2\mathrm{Re}(\mu)}\mathrm{d}x=\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}x}(\varphi(x)^{2})x^{2\mathrm{Re}(\mu)}\mathrm{d}x=-\mathrm{Re}(\mu)\int_{0}^{\infty}\varphi(x)^{2}x^{2\mathrm{Re}(\mu)-1}\mathrm{d}x>0.

(7.9)

On the other hand, for
\varphi(x)=\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{x}{2}}
the integral is negative:

\int_{0}^{\infty}\varphi(x)\varphi^{\prime}(x)x^{2\mathrm{Re}(\mu)}\mathrm{d}x=-\frac{\Gamma(2\mathrm{Re}(\mu)+1)}{2}<0.

(7.10)

By Proposition
35
and the fact that
\mathrm{Num}(D_{p})
is a convex cone, we have
\mathrm{Num}(D_{p})=\mathbb{C}
.
∎

We adopt the convention saying that operators with the numerical range
contained in the closed upper half-plane are called
dissipative
. Dissipative operators which are not properly contained
in another dissipative operator are said to be
maximally
dissipative
. This condition is equivalent to the inclusion of the spectrum in the closed upper half plane. Maximally dissipative operators may also be characterized as operators
D
such that
\mathrm{i}D
is the generator of a semigroup of contractions.

Corollary 37
.

\pm D_{p}
is a dissipative operator if and only if one of the following (mutually exclusive) statements holds:

•

\omega,\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
and
\mp\mathrm{Im}(\overline{b}a)\geq 0
.

•

\pm\mathrm{Im}(\lambda)<0
,
|\mathrm{Im}(\omega)|\leq|\mathrm{Im}(\lambda)|
and
\mathrm{Re}(\mu)>0
.

•

\pm\mathrm{Im}(\lambda)<0
,
|\mathrm{Im}(\omega)|\leq|\mathrm{Im}(\lambda)|
,
\mathrm{Re}(\mu)=0
and
\pm\mathrm{Im}(\overline{b}a)\leq 0
.

•

\pm\mathrm{Im}(\lambda)<0
,
|\mathrm{Im}(\omega)|\leq|\mathrm{Im}(\lambda)|
,
\mathrm{Re}(\mu)<0
and

\mathrm{Im}(\overline{b}a)=0
.

Furthermore, if these conditions are satisfied then
\pm D_{p}
is maximally dissipative.

Corollary 38
.

Let
\omega,\lambda
be such that
\pm D_{\omega,\lambda}^{\min}
is dissipative, i.e.
|\mathrm{Im}(\omega)|\leq|\mathrm{Im}(\lambda)|
,
\pm\mathrm{Im}(\lambda)\leq 0
. There exists
p\in\mathcal{M}_{-\frac{1}{2}}
such that
D_{\omega,\lambda}^{\min}\subset D_{p}
and
\pm D_{p}
is maximally dissipative. In particular
\pm D_{\omega,\lambda}^{\min}
admits a maximally dissipative extension which is homogeneous and contained in
\pm D_{\omega,\lambda}^{\max}
.

Proof.

We present the proof for the upper sign. The other part of the statement then follows by taking complex conjugates. If
\omega,\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
, it is possible to choose
p
with
\mp\mathrm{Im}(\overline{b}a)\geq 0
. Now let
\mathrm{Im}(\lambda)<0
,
|\mathrm{Im}(\omega)|\leq|\mathrm{Im}(\lambda)|
. If
\omega^{2}-\lambda^{2}\notin]-\infty,0]
, we can choose
\mu
with
\mathrm{Re}(\mu)>0
.

Next suppose that
\omega^{2}-\lambda^{2}\leq 0
. If the inequality is strict, then there exist two possible choices of
\mu
differing by a sign, so the condition
\mathrm{Im}(\overline{b}a)\leq 0
is satisfied for at least one choice. If
\omega^{2}-\lambda^{2}=0
, then either
\omega+\lambda
or
\omega-\lambda
vanishes. We may assume that it is not true that both vanish, because this is covered by the case
\omega,\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
. Then
[a:b]=[0:1]
or
[a:b]=[1:0]
.
∎

8
Mixed boundary conditions

In this section we discuss operators
D_{\omega,\lambda}^{f}
introduced around equation (
4.8
). Hence
\omega,\lambda
are restricted to the region
|\mathrm{Re}\sqrt{\omega^{2}-\lambda^{2}}|<\frac{1}{2}
.

Proposition 39
.

D_{\omega,\lambda}^{f}
is closed, self-transposed and
\sigma_{\mathrm{ess}}(D_{\omega,\lambda}^{f})=\sigma_{\mathrm{ess},0}(D_{\omega,\lambda}^{f})=\mathbb{R}
.

Proof.

The self-transposedness follows from
[
11
, Proposition
3.21]
. The statement about the essential spectrum follows from Corollary
26
.
∎

Operators
D_{\omega,\lambda}^{f}
can be organized in a holomorphic family as follows. Let

\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{mix}}=\left\{(\omega,\lambda,[a:b])\in\mathbb{C}^{2}\times\mathbb{CP}^{1}\,|\,\mathrm{Re}\sqrt{\omega^{2}-\lambda^{2}}|<\frac{1}{2}\right\}.

(8.1)

We define
D^{\mathrm{mix}}_{\omega,\lambda,[a:b]}
to be
D_{\omega,\lambda}^{f_{\omega,\lambda,[a:b]}}
, where
f_{\omega,\lambda,[a:b]}
is a (unique up to a multiplicative constant) solution of
D_{\omega,\lambda}f_{\omega,\lambda,[a:b]}=0
whose value at
x=1
belongs to the ray
[a:b]
in
\mathbb{C}^{2}
.

Proposition 40
.

D_{\omega,\lambda,[a:b]}^{\mathrm{mix}}
form a holomorphic family of operators on
\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{mix}}
. One has
\overline{D_{\omega,\lambda,[a:b]}^{\mathrm{mix}}}=D_{\overline{\vphantom{\lambda}\omega},\overline{\lambda},[\overline{\vphantom{\lambda}a}:\overline{\vphantom{\lambda}b}]}^{\mathrm{mix}}
, so
D_{\omega,\lambda,[a:b]}^{\mathrm{mix}}
is self-adjoint if and only if
\omega,\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
,
[a:b]\in\mathbb{RP}^{1}
.

Proof.

Only the holomorphy of
D_{\omega,\lambda,[a:b]}^{\mathrm{mix}}
requires some justification. Define

T_{\omega,\lambda,[a:b]}:H_{0}^{1}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathbb{C}^{2})\oplus\mathbb{C}\ni(g,t)\mapsto g+t\chi f_{\omega,\lambda,[a:b]},

(8.2)

where
\chi\in C_{c}^{\infty}([\mathbb{R}_{+},\infty[)
is equal to
1
near
0
. It is easy to check that
T_{\omega,\lambda,[a:b]}
form a holomorphic family of bounded injective operators with
\mathrm{Ran}(T_{\omega,\lambda,[a:b]})=\mathrm{Dom}(D_{\omega,\lambda,[a:b]}^{\mathrm{mix}})
such that
D_{\omega,\lambda,[a:b]}^{\mathrm{mix}}T_{\omega,\lambda,[a:b]}
form a holomorphic family of bounded operators.
∎

Next we describe the point spectra of nonhomogeneous operators

D_{\omega,\lambda}^{f}
. For this purpose it is not very convenient to
use the parametrization by points of
\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{mix}}
.

Below we treat the logarithm, denoted
\mathrm{Ln}
, as a set-valued function, more
precisely,

\mathrm{Ln}(z):=\{u\ |\ z=\mathrm{e}^{u}\}.

(8.3)

Proposition 41
.

Consider the point spectrum of
D_{\omega,\lambda}^{f}
for various
\omega,\lambda,f
. All eigenvalues are non-degenerate and zero is never an eigenvalue. For
k\neq 0
, we split the discussion into several cases. We say that a pair
(k,\pm)
is admissible if either
k\in\mathbb{R}^{\times}
,
|\mathrm{Im}(\lambda)|>\frac{1}{2}
and
\pm=\mathrm{sgn}(\mathrm{Im}(\lambda))
or
k\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{R}
and
\pm=\mathrm{sgn}(\mathrm{Im}(k))
.

1.

Case
\mu\neq 0
. We select
select a square root
\mu=\sqrt{\omega^{2}-\lambda^{2}}
, or
equivalently, we fix
p\in\mathcal{M}_{-\frac{1}{2}}
lying over
\omega,\lambda
.
All nonhomogeneous realizations of
D_{\omega,\lambda}
correspond
to

f(x)=\begin{bmatrix}\omega-\lambda\\
-\mu\end{bmatrix}x^{\mu}+\kappa\begin{bmatrix}\omega-\lambda\\
\mu\end{bmatrix}x^{-\mu}

(8.4)

with
\kappa\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}
. Let

c_{p,\pm}=\frac{\omega}{\lambda\mp\mathrm{i}\mu}\frac{\Gamma(2\mu+1)}{\Gamma(-2\mu+1)}\frac{\Gamma(1-\mu\mp\mathrm{i}\lambda)}{\Gamma(1+\mu\mp\mathrm{i}\lambda)}

(8.5)

Away from
\mu=0
,
c_{p,\pm}
is a holomorphic function of
\omega,\lambda,\mu
valued in
\mathbb{C}\cup\{\infty\}
.
k
is an eigenvalue if and only if
\kappa(\mp 2\mathrm{i}k)^{2\mu}=c_{p,\pm}
and
(k,\pm)
is admissible.
D_{\omega,\lambda}^{f}
has no eigenvalues in
\mathbb{C}_{\pm}
if
c_{p,\pm}\in\{0,\infty\}
. Away from these loci, eigenvalues in
\mathbb{C}_{\pm}
vary continuously with parameters, possibly (dis)appearing on the real axis. They form a discrete subset of a half-line if
\mu\in\mathrm{i}\mathbb{R}
, of a circle if
\mu\in\mathbb{R}
and of a logarithmic spiral otherwise. If
\mu\not\in\mathrm{i}\mathbb{R}
, the set of eigenvalues is finite. More precisely, it is given by the union of the following two sets:

\displaystyle\left\{k=\pm\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\mathrm{e}^{w}\ |\ w\in\frac{1}{2\mu}\mathrm{Ln}(c_{p,\pm}),\quad-\frac{\pi}{2}<\mathrm{Im}(w)<\frac{\pi}{2}\right\},

\displaystyle\quad|\mathrm{Im}(\lambda)|\leq\frac{1}{2},

(8.6)

\displaystyle\left\{k=\pm\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\mathrm{e}^{w}\ |\ w\in\frac{1}{2\mu}\mathrm{Ln}(c_{p,\pm}),\quad-\frac{\pi}{2}\leq\mathrm{Im}(w)\leq\frac{\pi}{2}\right\},

\displaystyle\quad|\mathrm{Im}(\lambda)|>\frac{1}{2}.

(8.7)

2.

Case
\mu=0
,
(\omega,\lambda)\neq(0,0)
.
All nonhomogeneous realizations of
D_{\omega,\lambda}
are
parametrized
by
\nu\in\mathbb{C}
and

\displaystyle f(x)=\begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\end{bmatrix}-2\lambda(\ln(\mathrm{e}^{2\gamma}x)+\nu)\begin{bmatrix}0\\
1\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle\quad\text{for}\quad\omega=\lambda\neq 0,

(8.8)

\displaystyle f(x)=\begin{bmatrix}0\\
1\end{bmatrix}+2\lambda(\ln(\mathrm{e}^{2\gamma}x)+\nu)\begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle\quad\text{for}\quad\omega=-\lambda\neq 0.

(8.9)

In both cases
k
is an eigenvalue if and only if

\ln(\mp 2\mathrm{i}k)+\psi(1\mp\mathrm{i}\lambda)\mp\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2\lambda}=\nu
and
(k,\pm)
is
admissible.
There is at most one eigenvalue in
\mathbb{C}_{+}
and at most one eigenvalue in
\mathbb{C}_{-}
. The eigenvalue in
\mathbb{C}_{\pm}
exists if and only if
\pm\mathrm{i}\lambda\not\in\mathbb{N}
and
\mathrm{Re}\left(\exp\left(\nu-\psi(1\mp\mathrm{i}\lambda)\mp\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2\lambda}\right)\right)>0
.

3.

Case
\omega=\lambda=0
,
f(x)=\begin{bmatrix}1\\
\kappa\end{bmatrix}
.
k
is an eigenvalue if and only if
k\not\in\mathbb{R}
and
\kappa=\mathrm{i}\,\mathrm{sgn}(\mathrm{Im}(k))
.

Proof.

An eigenvector of
D_{\omega,\lambda}
square integrable away from the origin is necessarily of the form
\zeta_{p}^{\pm}(k,\cdot)
with an admissible
(k,\pm)
. It belongs to the domain of
D_{\omega,\lambda}^{f}
if its asymptotic form for
x\to 0
, obtained from (
D.24
), is proportional to
f
. This yields conditions described in 1.-3.

Function
c_{p,\pm}
is meromorphic. In the region
|\mathrm{Re}(\mu)|<\frac{1}{2}
functions
\frac{\omega}{\Gamma(1+\mu\mp\mathrm{i}\lambda)}
and
\frac{\lambda\mp\mathrm{i}\mu}{\Gamma(1-\mu\mp\mathrm{i}\lambda)}
do not simultaneously vanish anywhere, while
\frac{\Gamma(2\mu+1)}{\Gamma(-2\mu+1)}
is holomorphic and nowhere vanishing. Hence
c_{p,\pm}
is not of the indeterminate form
\frac{0}{0}
anywhere.
∎

Let us note that eigenfunctions corresponding to real eigenvalues (which exist only for
|\mathrm{Im}(\lambda)|>\frac{1}{2}
) decay at infinity only as fast as
x^{-|\mathrm{Im}(\lambda)|}
, not exponentially.

Consider a homogeneous operator
D_{p}
with
p\in\mathcal{E}^{\pm}
and its deformations
D_{\omega,\lambda}^{f}
, with
f
parametrized by
\kappa
so that
D_{\omega,\lambda}^{f}=D_{p}
for
\kappa=0
. Then for
\kappa=0
the point spectrum of
D_{\omega,\lambda}^{f}
is
\mathbb{C}_{\pm}
, but for every
\kappa\neq 0
it is disjoint from
\mathbb{C}_{\pm}
.

Appendix A
1-dimensional Dirac operators

A.1
General formalism

By a
1d Dirac operator on the halfline
we will mean a differential
operator of the form

D=\begin{bmatrix}a(x)&-\partial_{x}\\
\partial_{x}&b(x)\end{bmatrix},

(A.1)

where
a,b
are smooth functions on
\mathbb{R}_{+}=]0,\infty[
.
In this subsection we treat it as a formal operator acting, say, on the space of distributions on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
valued
in
\mathbb{C}^{2}
.
We first describe a few integral kernels closely related to
D
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{C}
and

\xi(k,x)=\begin{bmatrix}\xi_{\uparrow}(k,x)\\
\xi_{\downarrow}(k,x)\end{bmatrix},\qquad\zeta(k,x)=\begin{bmatrix}\zeta_{\uparrow}(k,x)\\
\zeta_{\downarrow}(k,x)\end{bmatrix}

(A.2)

be a pair of linearly independent solutions of the Dirac equation:

(D-k)\xi(k,\cdot)=(D-k)\zeta(k,\cdot)=0.

(A.3)

Let

d(k,x):=\det\left[\xi(k,x),\zeta(k,x)\right]=\det\begin{bmatrix}\xi_{\uparrow}(k,x)&\zeta_{\uparrow}(k,x)\\
\xi_{\downarrow}(k,x)&\zeta_{\downarrow}(k,x).\end{bmatrix}

(A.4)

Then
d(k,x)
does not depend on
x
, so that one can write
d(k)
instead.
We define

\displaystyle G^{\leftrightarrow}(k;x,y):=d(k)^{-1}\xi(k,x)\zeta(k,y)^{\mathrm{T}}-d(k)^{-1}\zeta(k,x)\xi(k,y)^{\mathrm{T}}

(A.5)

\displaystyle=d(k)^{-1}\begin{bmatrix}\xi_{\uparrow}(k,x)\zeta_{\uparrow}(k,y)&\xi_{\uparrow}(k,x)\zeta_{\downarrow}(k,y)\\[4.30554pt]
\xi_{\downarrow}(k,x)\zeta_{\uparrow}(k,y)&\xi_{\downarrow}(k,x)\zeta_{\downarrow}(k,y)\end{bmatrix}-d(k)^{-1}\begin{bmatrix}\zeta_{\uparrow}(k,x)\xi_{\uparrow}(k,y)&\zeta_{\uparrow}(k,x)\xi_{\downarrow}(k,y)\\[4.30554pt]
\zeta_{\downarrow}(k,x)\xi_{\uparrow}(k,y)&\zeta_{\downarrow}(k,x)\xi_{\downarrow}(k,y)\end{bmatrix}.

Note that
G^{\leftrightarrow}(k,x,y)
is uniquely defined by

(D-k)G^{\leftrightarrow}(k;x,y)=0,\qquad G^{\leftrightarrow}(k;x,x)=\begin{bmatrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{bmatrix}.

(A.6)

We will call it the
canonical bisolution.

We also have the
forward and backward Green’s operators
given by the kernels

\displaystyle G^{\rightarrow}(k;x,y)=

\displaystyle G^{\leftrightarrow}(k;x,y){\mathchoice{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.5mul}{\rm 1\mskip-5.0mul}}_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}(x-y),

(A.7a)

\displaystyle G^{\leftarrow}(k;x,y)=

\displaystyle-G^{\leftrightarrow}(k;x,y){\mathchoice{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.5mul}{\rm 1\mskip-5.0mul}}_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}(y-x).

(A.7b)

They are uniquely defined by

\displaystyle(D-k)G^{\rightarrow}(k;x,y)=\delta(x-y)\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\
0&1\end{bmatrix},

\displaystyle\quad x<y\Rightarrow G^{\rightarrow}(x,y)=0;

(A.8a)

\displaystyle(D-k)G^{\leftarrow}(k;x,y)=\delta(x-y)\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\
0&1\end{bmatrix},

\displaystyle\quad x>y\Rightarrow G^{\leftarrow}(x,y)=0.

(A.8b)

Note that
G^{\leftrightarrow},G^{\leftarrow},G^{\rightarrow}
do not depend on the choice of
\xi,\zeta
.

Using the eigensolutions
\xi,\zeta
, we can introduce yet another important integral kernel:

\displaystyle G^{\bowtie}(k;x,y):=

\displaystyle-d(k)^{-1}\begin{bmatrix}\xi_{\uparrow}(k,x)\zeta_{\uparrow}(k,y)&\xi_{\uparrow}(k,x)\zeta_{\downarrow}(k,y)\\
\xi_{\downarrow}(k,x)\zeta_{\uparrow}(k,y)&\xi_{\downarrow}(k,x)\zeta_{\downarrow}(k,y)\end{bmatrix}{\mathchoice{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.5mul}{\rm 1\mskip-5.0mul}}_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}(y-x)

\displaystyle-d(k)^{-1}\begin{bmatrix}\zeta_{\uparrow}(k,x)\xi_{\uparrow}(k,y)&\zeta_{\uparrow}(k,x)\xi_{\downarrow}(k,y)\\
\zeta_{\downarrow}(k,x)\xi_{\uparrow}(k,y)&\zeta_{\downarrow}(k,x)\xi_{\downarrow}(k,y)\end{bmatrix}{\mathchoice{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.0mul}{\rm 1\mskip-4.5mul}{\rm 1\mskip-5.0mul}}_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}(x-y).

(A.9)

It is also
Green’s kernel
, because
it satisfies

\displaystyle(D-k)G^{\bowtie}(k;x,y)=\delta(x-y)\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}.

(A.10)

G^{\bowtie}(k;x,y)

depends on the choice of the pair of 1-dimensional subspaces
\mathbb{C}\xi(k,\cdot)
,

\mathbb{C}\zeta(k,\cdot)
of
\mathrm{Ker}(D-k)
. The resolvents of various closed
realizations
of
D
are often of this form.

Two classes of 1d Dirac operators have special properties.
The case
a(x)=b(x)
can be fully diagonalized:

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}a(x)&-\partial_{x}\\
\partial_{x}&a(x)\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\begin{bmatrix}1&\mathrm{i}\\
\mathrm{i}&1\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}-\mathrm{i}\partial_{x}+a(x)&0\\
0&\mathrm{i}\partial_{x}+a(x)\end{bmatrix}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\begin{bmatrix}1&-\mathrm{i}\\
-\mathrm{i}&1\end{bmatrix}.

(A.11)

We will analyze 1d Dirac-Coulomb operators of this form in Subsection

A.3
.

The case
a(x)=-b(x)
can be brought to an antidiagonal
form, used in supersymmetry:

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}a(x)&-\partial_{x}\\
\partial_{x}&-a(x)\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\begin{bmatrix}1&-1\\
1&1\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}0&-\partial_{x}-a(x)\\
\partial_{x}-a(x)&0\end{bmatrix}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\begin{bmatrix}1&1\\
-1&1\end{bmatrix}.

(A.12)

We will analyze 1d Dirac-Coulomb operators of this form in Subsection

A.5
.

A.2
Homogeneous first order scalar operators

Let
\alpha\in\mathbb{C}
. In this subsection we discuss the differential operator

A_{\alpha}=x^{\alpha}\partial_{x}x^{-\alpha}=\partial_{x}-\frac{\alpha}{x}

(A.13)

acting on scalar functions. It will be a building block of some
special 1d Dirac-Coulomb operators considered in subsections

A.3

and
A.5
.

Let us briefly recall basic results about realizations of

A_{\alpha}
as a closed operator in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
following

[
3
]
. Proofs of all statements stated in this subsection
without justification can be found therein.
(In
[
3
]
a different convention was used:

A_{\alpha}=-\mathrm{i}\partial_{x}+\frac{\mathrm{i}\alpha}{x}
. Thus

A_{\alpha}^{\rm new}=\mathrm{i}A_{\alpha}^{\rm old}
.)

We let
A_{\alpha}^{\min}
be the closure (in the sense of operators on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
) of the restriction of
A_{\alpha}
to
C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
and
A_{\alpha}^{\max}
the restriction of
A_{\alpha}
to
\mathrm{Dom}(A_{\alpha}^{\max})=\{f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+})\,|\,A_{\alpha}f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})\}
. Operators
A_{\alpha}^{\min}
and
-A_{-\overline{\alpha}}^{\max}
are adjoint to each other.

Proposition 42
.

We have
A_{\alpha}^{\min}=A_{\alpha}^{\max}
if and only if

|\mathrm{Re}(\alpha)|\geq\frac{1}{2}
. If
|\mathrm{Re}(\alpha)|<\frac{1}{2}
, then

\mathrm{Dom}(A_{\alpha}^{\max})=\mathrm{Dom}(A_{\alpha}^{\min})+\mathbb{C}\chi x^{\alpha}
, where
\chi\in C_{\mathrm{c}}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
and
\chi=1

near
0
. If
\mathrm{Re}(\alpha)\neq\frac{1}{2}
, then
\mathrm{Dom}(A_{\alpha}^{\min})=H_{0}^{1}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
.

Closed realizations of
A_{\alpha}
are of two types,
described in the following pair of propositions.

Proposition 43
.

Let
\mathrm{Re}(\alpha)>-\frac{1}{2}
,

1.

\sigma(-\mathrm{i}A_{\alpha}^{\max})=\mathbb{R}\cup\mathbb{C}_{+}

and one has

(A^{\max}_{\alpha}-\mathrm{i}k)^{-1}f(x)=-\int_{x}^{\infty}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}k(x-y)}\left(\frac{x}{y}\right)^{\alpha}f(y)\mathrm{d}y,\qquad\mathrm{Im}(k)<0.

(A.14)

2.

If
k\in\mathbb{C}_{+}
and
n\geq 1
, then
\mathrm{Ker}\left((A_{\alpha}^{\max}-\mathrm{i}k)^{n}\right)
is the space of functions of
the form
x^{\alpha}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}kx}q(x)
with
q
polynomial of degree
at most
n-1
. In particular
\bigcup\limits_{n=0}^{\infty}\mathrm{Ker}\left((A_{\alpha}^{\max}-\mathrm{i}k)^{n}\right)
is dense in

L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
.

3.

If
k\in\mathbb{C}_{+}
, then
A_{\alpha}^{\max}-\mathrm{i}k
is a Fredholm operator of index
1
.

Proposition 44
.

Let
\mathrm{Re}(\alpha)<\frac{1}{2}
.

1.

\sigma(-\mathrm{i}A_{\alpha}^{\min})=\mathbb{R}\cup\mathbb{C}_{-}
and one has

(A^{\min}_{\alpha}-\mathrm{i}k)^{-1}f(x)=\int_{0}^{x}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}k(x-y)}\left(\frac{x}{y}\right)^{\alpha}f(y)\mathrm{d}y,\qquad\mathrm{Im}(k)>0.

(A.15)

2.

A_{\alpha}^{\min}
has no eigenvectors.

3.

If
k\in\mathbb{C}_{-}
, then
A_{\alpha}^{\min}-\mathrm{i}k
is a Fredholm
operator of index
-1
.

Proof of Propositions
43
and
44
.

Statements 1. are proven in
[
3
]
.

2. requires justification only for the first part in the first proposition. We factorize

x^{\alpha}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}kx}q(x)=x^{\mathrm{i}\,\mathrm{Im}(\alpha)}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\,\mathrm{Re}(k)x}\left(x^{\mathrm{Re}(\alpha)}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{Im}(k)x}q(x)\right).

(A.16)

Functions in the parenthesis form a dense set, because for any real
numbers
c>0
,
\beta>-1
functions
e^{-\frac{cx}{2}}x^{\frac{\beta}{2}}L_{n}^{(\beta)}(cx)
, with
L_{n}^{(\beta)}

Laguerre polynomials, form an orthogonal basis (see e.g.
[
41
]
). Clearly density is unaffected by the prefactor, which amounts to the action of a certain unitary operator on
L^{2}
.

Let us show 3. We consider first the case
|\mathrm{Re}(\alpha)|<\frac{1}{2}
. Then we have explicit inverses modulo rank one operators.

If
\mathrm{Im}(k)>0
, then
A^{\min}_{\alpha}-\mathrm{i}k
is invertible and its inverse is a right inverse for
A_{\alpha}^{\max}-\mathrm{i}k
. Thus
A_{\alpha}^{\max}-\mathrm{i}k
is surjective. We already know that its kernel is one-dimensional.

If
\mathrm{Im}(k)<0
, then
(A_{\alpha}^{\max}-\mathrm{i}k)^{-1}:L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+})\to\mathrm{Dom}(A_{\alpha}^{\max})
is continuous. The range of
A_{\alpha}^{\min}-\mathrm{i}k
is the preimage of
\mathrm{Dom}(A_{\alpha}^{\min})
, which is a closed subspace of
\mathrm{Dom}(A_{\alpha}^{\max})
of codimension one. Hence
\mathrm{Ran}(A_{\alpha}^{\min}-\mathrm{i}k)
is a closed subspace of
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
of codimension one.

To extended the result beyond the strip
|\mathrm{Re}(\alpha)|<\frac{1}{2}
, note that
(A_{\alpha}^{\min}-\mathrm{i}k)^{-1}-(A_{\beta}^{\min}-\mathrm{i}k)^{-1}
(resp.
(A_{\alpha}^{\max}-\mathrm{i}k)^{-1}-(A_{\beta}^{\max}-\mathrm{i}k)^{-1}
) has a square-integrable integral kernel for

\mathrm{Re}(\alpha),\,\mathrm{Re}(\beta)<\frac{1}{2}
and
k\in\mathbb{C}_{+}

(resp.
\mathrm{Re}(\alpha),\,\mathrm{Re}(\beta)>-\frac{1}{2}
and
k\in\mathbb{C}_{-}
). Therefore it is a Hilbert-Schmidt operator, in particular
compact. By Corollary
25
, the essential spectrum of

A_{\alpha}^{\min}
and
A_{\alpha}^{\max}
does not depend on

\alpha
. From the case
|\mathrm{Re}(\alpha)|<\frac{1}{2}
we know that it is

\mathbb{R}
. The statement about the value of the index is clear.
∎

Proposition 45
.

A_{\alpha}^{\max}
is the generator of a
C^{0}
-semigroup if and only if
\mathrm{Re}(\alpha)\geq 0
. If this condition is satisfied, it generates the semigroup of contractions

(\mathrm{e}^{tA_{\alpha}^{\max}}f)(x)=x^{\alpha}(x+t)^{-\alpha}f(x+t),\qquad t\geq 0,

(A.17)

-A_{\alpha}^{\min}
is the generator of a
C^{0}
-semigroup if and only if
\mathrm{Re}(\alpha)\leq 0
. If this condition is satisfied, it generated a semigroup of contractions

(\mathrm{e}^{-tA_{\alpha}^{\min}}f)(x)=x^{\alpha}(x-t)^{-\alpha}f(x-t),\qquad t\geq 0.

(A.18)

Here we put
f(x-t)=0
if
x-t<0
.

If
|\mathrm{Re}(\alpha)|<\frac{1}{2}
, the operators
-A_{\alpha}^{\max}
and
A_{\alpha}^{\min}
are not generators of
C^{0}
-semigroups.

Proof.

We present a proof of the statements concerning

A_{\alpha}^{\max}
. The others can be proven analogously. It is
elementary to check that for
\mathrm{Re}(\alpha)\geq 0
the right hand side
of (
A.17
) defines a
C^{0}
-semigroup of contractions
with the generator
A_{\max}^{\alpha}
. If
\mathrm{Re}(\alpha)<0
, we consider the same expression for
f\in C_{c}^{\infty}(]1,\infty[)
. Then for
t\leq 1
it is the unique solution of the Cauchy problem
\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}f_{t}=A_{\alpha}^{\max}f_{t}
,
f_{0}=f
. However, there exists no constant
c
such that
\|f_{t}\|\leq c\|f\|
for every
t\in[0,1]
and
f
. Thus
A_{\alpha}^{\max}
is not a generator. If
|\mathrm{Re}(\alpha)|<\frac{1}{2}
, then
\sigma(-A_{\alpha}^{\max})
is the right closed complex half-plane, so
-A_{\alpha}^{\max}
is not a generator.
∎

A.3
Dirac-Coulomb Hamiltonians with
\omega=0

Dirac-Coulomb Hamiltonians with
\omega=0
can be reduced to operators

A_{\alpha}
studied in Subsection
A.2
.
Therefore, they can be analyzed using elementary functions only.

Let us set
W:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\begin{bmatrix}1&\mathrm{i}\\
\mathrm{i}&1\end{bmatrix}
.
Using (
A.11
) we obtain for all
\lambda

\displaystyle D_{0,\lambda}^{\min}=W\begin{bmatrix}-\mathrm{i}A_{\mathrm{i}\lambda}^{\min}&0\\
0&\mathrm{i}A_{-\mathrm{i}\lambda}^{\min}\end{bmatrix}W^{-1},\qquad D_{0,\lambda}^{\max}=W\begin{bmatrix}-\mathrm{i}A_{\mathrm{i}\lambda}^{\max}&0\\
0&\mathrm{i}A_{-\mathrm{i}\lambda}^{\max}\end{bmatrix}W^{-1}.

(A.19)

Consider now the homogeneous holomorphic family. Note first that

\omega=0
implies
\mu=\pm\mathrm{i}\lambda
.
We set
D_{\lambda}^{\pm}:=D_{0,\lambda,\pm\mathrm{i}\lambda,[\mp\mathrm{i}:1]}
. Note that
(0,\lambda,\pm\mathrm{i}\lambda,[\mp\mathrm{i}:1])\in\mathcal{E}^{\pm}
. We have:

\displaystyle D_{\lambda}^{+}

\displaystyle=W\begin{bmatrix}-\mathrm{i}A_{\mathrm{i}\lambda}^{\max}&0\\
0&\mathrm{i}A_{-\mathrm{i}\lambda}^{\min}\end{bmatrix}W^{-1},\qquad\mathrm{Re}(\mathrm{i}\lambda)>-\frac{1}{2},

(A.20a)

\displaystyle D_{\lambda}^{-}

\displaystyle=W\begin{bmatrix}-\mathrm{i}A_{\mathrm{i}\lambda}^{\min}&0\\
0&\mathrm{i}A_{-\mathrm{i}\lambda}^{\max}\end{bmatrix}W^{-1},\qquad\mathrm{Re}(-\mathrm{i}\lambda)>-\frac{1}{2},

(A.20b)

Below
\sigma_{2}
is the Pauli matrix
\begin{bmatrix}0&-\mathrm{i}\\
\mathrm{i}&0\end{bmatrix}
. Matrices
\frac{1\pm\sigma_{2}}{2}
are its spectral projections.

Proposition 46
.

We have
\sigma(D_{\lambda}^{+})=\mathbb{R}\cup\mathbb{C}_{+}
and

(D_{\lambda}^{+}-k)^{-1}=(-\mathrm{i}A_{\mathrm{i}\lambda}^{\max}-k)^{-1}\frac{1+\sigma_{2}}{2}-(-\mathrm{i}A_{-\mathrm{i}\lambda}^{\min}+k)^{-1}\frac{1-\sigma_{2}}{2},\qquad\mathrm{Im}(k)<0,

(A.21)

whereas
\sigma(D_{\lambda}^{-})=\mathbb{R}\cup\mathbb{C}_{-}
and

(D_{\lambda}^{-}-k)^{-1}=(-\mathrm{i}A_{\mathrm{i}\lambda}^{\min}-k)^{-1}\frac{1+\sigma_{2}}{2}-(-\mathrm{i}A_{-\mathrm{i}\lambda}^{\max}+k)^{-1}\frac{1-\sigma_{2}}{2},\qquad\mathrm{Im}(k)>0.

(A.22)

Proof.

Follows from identities (
A.20b
) and Proposition
43
,
44
.
∎

Proposition 47
.

D_{\lambda}^{\pm}-k
with
k\in\mathbb{C}_{\pm}
are Fredholm of
index
0
.

Proof.

Indeed, by Propositions
43
and
44

they are direct sums of two Fredholm operators with indices
1
and

-1
. ∎

Proposition 48
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{C}_{\pm}
. Then
\bigcup\limits_{n=0}^{\infty}\mathrm{Ker}((D_{\lambda}^{\pm}-k)^{n})
is a dense subspace of
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+})\begin{bmatrix}\mp\mathrm{i}\\
1\end{bmatrix}
.

Proposition 49
.

\mathrm{i}D_{\lambda}^{+}
is the generator of a
C^{0}
-semigroup if and only if
\mathrm{Im}(\lambda)\leq 0
. Then it generates the semigroup of contractions

\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}tD_{\lambda}^{+}}=\mathrm{e}^{tA_{\mathrm{i}\lambda}^{\max}}\frac{1+\sigma_{2}}{2}+\mathrm{e}^{-tA^{\min}_{-\mathrm{i}\lambda}}\frac{1-\sigma_{2}}{2},\qquad t\geq 0.

(A.23)

-\mathrm{i}D_{\lambda}^{-}
is the generator of a
C^{0}
-semigroup if and only if
\mathrm{Im}(\lambda)\geq 0
. Then it generates the semigroup of contractions

\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}tD_{\lambda}^{-}}=\mathrm{e}^{-tA_{\mathrm{i}\lambda}^{\min}}\frac{1+\sigma_{2}}{2}+\mathrm{e}^{tA^{\max}_{-\mathrm{i}\lambda}}\frac{1-\sigma_{2}}{2},\qquad t\geq 0.

(A.24)

Operators
-\mathrm{i}D_{\lambda}^{+}
and
\mathrm{i}D_{\lambda}^{-}
are not generators of
C^{0}
-semigroups.

A.4
Hankel transformation

The following proposition is proven e.g. in
[
3
]
.

Proposition 50
.

Let
\mathrm{Re}(m)\geq-1
. We define

(\mathcal{F}_{m}^{\mathrm{pre}}f)(x)=\int_{0}^{\infty}J_{m}(xy)\sqrt{xy}f(y)\mathrm{d}y,\qquad f\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{+}),

(A.25)

where
J_{m}
is the Bessel function. Then
\mathcal{F}_{m}^{\mathrm{pre}}
extends
to a bounded operator
\mathcal{F}_{m}
on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
, known as the Hankel transformation.
\mathcal{F}_{m}
is a self-transposed involution, unitary if
m
is real.

Recall from Subsection
1.1

that the operator
X
is defined by

(Xf)(x)=xf(x),\qquad\mathrm{Dom}(X)=\{f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+})\,|\,xf(x)\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+})\}.

Proposition 51
.

If
\mathrm{Re}(\alpha)>-\frac{1}{2}
, one has

\mathcal{F}_{\alpha+\frac{1}{2}}A_{\alpha}^{\max}\mathcal{F}_{\alpha-\frac{1}{2}}=-X,\qquad\mathcal{F}_{\alpha-\frac{1}{2}}A_{-\alpha}^{\min}\mathcal{F}_{\alpha+\frac{1}{2}}=X.

(A.26)

Proof.

Using the identity

x^{-m}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}x}x^{m}J_{m}(x)=J_{m-1}(x)

(A.27)

one checks that

(\mathcal{F}_{\alpha+\frac{1}{2}}^{\mathrm{pre}}A_{\alpha}f)(x)=-x(\mathcal{F}_{\alpha-\frac{1}{2}}^{\mathrm{pre}}f)(x)

(A.28)

for
f\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
. If
|\mathrm{Re}(\alpha)|<\frac{1}{2}
, (
A.28
) may be checked to hold also for
f(x)=\chi(x)x^{\alpha}
. Taking closures we obtain
\mathcal{F}_{\alpha+\frac{1}{2}}A_{\alpha}^{\max}\subset-X\mathcal{F}_{\alpha-\frac{1}{2}}
, so
\mathcal{F}_{\alpha+\frac{1}{2}}A_{\alpha}^{\max}\mathcal{F}_{\alpha-\frac{1}{2}}\subset-X
. Since
\mathcal{F}_{\alpha+\frac{1}{2}}A_{\alpha}^{\max}\mathcal{F}_{\alpha-\frac{1}{2}}
is a closed operator and
C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
is a dense subspace of
\mathrm{Dom}(X)
with respect to the graph topology, the opposite inclusion will be established by demonstrating that
C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{+})\subset\mathrm{Dom}(\mathcal{F}_{\alpha+\frac{1}{2}}A_{\alpha}^{\max}\mathcal{F}_{\alpha-\frac{1}{2}})
. Let
f\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
. It is clear that
\mathcal{F}_{\alpha-\frac{1}{2}}f
is a smooth function. Using the identity
\left(x\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}x}-y\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}y}\right)\sqrt{xy}J_{m}(xy)=0
we find

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}x}(\mathcal{F}_{\alpha-\frac{1}{2}}f)(x)=\frac{1}{x}\int_{0}^{\infty}\sqrt{xy}J_{m}(xy)\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}y}\frac{f(y)}{y}\mathrm{d}y.

(A.29)

Since
\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}y}\frac{f(y)}{y}
is in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
, we get that
\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}x}(\mathcal{F}_{\alpha-\frac{1}{2}}f)(x)
is square-integrable over
[1,\infty[
. Next we use the series expansion of
J_{m}
to find that for small
x

(\mathcal{F}_{\alpha-\frac{1}{2}}f)(x)=\frac{\int_{0}^{\infty}y^{\alpha}f(y)\mathrm{d}y}{2^{\alpha-\frac{1}{2}}\Gamma(\alpha+\frac{1}{2})}x^{\alpha}+O(x^{\alpha+1}).

(A.30)

Hence
\mathcal{F}_{\alpha-\frac{1}{2}}f\in\mathrm{Dom}(A_{\alpha}^{\max})
, so
f\in\mathrm{Dom}(A_{\alpha}^{\max}\mathcal{F}_{\alpha-\frac{1}{2}})=\mathrm{Dom}(\mathcal{F}_{\alpha+\frac{1}{2}}A_{\alpha}^{\max}\mathcal{F}_{\alpha-\frac{1}{2}})
. We proved the first equality in (
A.26
). The other one may be obtained by taking the transpose.
∎

Following
[
12
]
(see also
[
3
]
), we consider the formal differential operator

L_{m^{2}}=-\partial_{x}^{2}+\frac{m^{2}-\frac{1}{4}}{x^{2}}.

(A.31)

We let
L_{m^{2}}^{\min}
be the closure of its restriction to

C_{\mathrm{c}}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
and
L_{m^{2}}^{\max}
be the restriction to

\mathrm{Dom}(L_{m^{2}}^{\max})=\{f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+})\,|\,L_{m^{2}}f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+})\}
. If
\mathrm{Re}(m)>-1
, operator
H_{m}
is defined as the restriction of

L_{m^{2}}
to
\mathrm{Dom}(L_{m^{2}}^{\min})+\mathbb{C}\chi x^{m+\frac{1}{2}}
,
where
\chi
is a smooth function equal to one in a neighborhood of
zero. We remark that
H_{\frac{1}{2}}
and
H_{-\frac{1}{2}}
are the
Dirichlet Laplacian and the Neuman Laplacian,
respectively. Furthermore,
H_{m}
can be diagonalized as follows:

H_{m}=\mathcal{F}_{m}X^{2}\mathcal{F}_{m}.

(A.32)

A.5
Dirac-Coulomb Hamiltonians with
\lambda=0

Dirac-Coulomb Hamiltonians with
\lambda=0

can be analyzed without Whittaker functions, just with Bessel functions.

Let us set
U:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\begin{bmatrix}1&-1\\
1&1\end{bmatrix}
.
Using (
A.12
) we obtain for all
\omega

\displaystyle D_{\omega,0}^{\min}=U\begin{bmatrix}0&-A_{\omega}^{\min}\\
A_{-\omega}^{\min}&0\end{bmatrix}U^{-1},\qquad D_{\omega,0}^{\max}=U\begin{bmatrix}0&-A_{\omega}^{\max}\\
A_{-\omega}^{\max}&0\end{bmatrix}U^{-1}.

(A.33)

Using Proposition
42
we rewrite the operators
D^{\prime\pm}_{\omega}:=D_{\omega,0,\pm\omega,[\mp 1:1]}

as

\displaystyle D^{\prime+}_{\omega}

\displaystyle=U\begin{bmatrix}0&-A_{\omega}^{\max}\\
A_{-\omega}^{\min}&0\end{bmatrix}U^{-1},\quad\mathrm{Re}(\omega)>-\frac{1}{2};

(A.34a)

\displaystyle D^{\prime-}_{\omega}

\displaystyle=U\begin{bmatrix}0&-A_{\omega}^{\min}\\
A_{-\omega}^{\max}&0\end{bmatrix}U^{-1},\quad-\mathrm{Re}(\omega)>-\frac{1}{2}.

(A.34b)

Proposition 52
.

Introduce

\mathcal{W}_{\omega}^{\prime\pm}:=\frac{1}{2}\begin{bmatrix}\mathcal{F}_{\pm\omega\pm\frac{1}{2}}{+}\mathcal{F}_{\pm\omega\mp\frac{1}{2}}&\mathcal{F}_{\pm\omega\pm\frac{1}{2}}{-}\mathcal{F}_{\pm\omega\mp\frac{1}{2}}\\
\mathcal{F}_{\pm\omega\pm\frac{1}{2}}{-}\mathcal{F}_{\pm\omega\mp\frac{1}{2}}&\mathcal{F}_{\pm\omega\pm\frac{1}{2}}{+}\mathcal{F}_{\pm\omega\mp\frac{1}{2}}\end{bmatrix},\quad\pm\mathrm{Re}(\omega)>-\frac{1}{2}.

(A.35)

Then

\mathcal{W}_{\omega}^{\prime\pm}
are involutions and
we have the following diagonalizations

D_{\omega}^{\prime\pm}=\mathcal{W}_{\omega}^{\prime\pm}\begin{bmatrix}\mp X&0\\
0&\pm X\end{bmatrix}\mathcal{W}_{\omega}^{\prime\pm}.

(A.36)

Proof.

We insert (
A.26
) into (
A.34
):

\displaystyle D_{\omega}^{\prime+}=U\begin{bmatrix}\mathcal{F}_{\omega+\frac{1}{2}}&0\\
0&\mathcal{F}_{\omega-\frac{1}{2}}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}0&X\\
X&0\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\mathcal{F}_{\omega+\frac{1}{2}}&0\\
0&\mathcal{F}_{\omega-\frac{1}{2}}\end{bmatrix}U^{-1},

(A.37a)

\displaystyle D_{\omega}^{\prime-}=U\begin{bmatrix}\mathcal{F}_{-\omega-\frac{1}{2}}&0\\
0&\mathcal{F}_{-\omega+\frac{1}{2}}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}0&-X\\
-X&0\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\mathcal{F}_{-\omega-\frac{1}{2}}&0\\
0&\mathcal{F}_{-\omega+\frac{1}{2}}\end{bmatrix}U^{-1}.

(A.37b)

Then we use

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}0&\pm X\\
\pm X&0\end{bmatrix}=U^{-1}\begin{bmatrix}\mp X&0\\
0&\pm X\end{bmatrix}U.

(A.38)

∎

Corollary 53
.

We have

\displaystyle(D^{\prime+}_{\omega})^{2}=U\begin{bmatrix}H_{\omega+\frac{1}{2}}&0\\
0&H_{\omega-\frac{1}{2}}\end{bmatrix}U^{-1},

(A.39a)

\displaystyle(D^{\prime-}_{\omega})^{2}=U\begin{bmatrix}H_{-\omega-\frac{1}{2}}&0\\
0&H_{-\omega+\frac{1}{2}}\end{bmatrix}U^{-1}.

(A.39b)

Remark 54
.

At least formally, operators
D_{\omega}^{\prime\pm}
,
X\sigma_{2}

(declared to be odd) and
(D_{\omega}^{\prime\pm})^{2}
,
X^{2}
,
A
(declared
to be even) furnish a representation of the Lie superalgebra

\mathfrak{osp}(1|2)
. We leave a detailed description of this
representation for a future study.

Appendix B
Dirac Hamiltonian in
d
dimensions

Separation of variables of a spherically symmetric Dirac Hamiltonian
in dimension 3
is described in many texts and belongs to the standard curriculum of
relativistic quantum mechanics
[
15
, p. 267]
.
Of course, it is even more
straightforward to solve a rotationally symmetric Dirac Hamiltonian in
dimension 2. However, to our knowledge, the first treatment
in any dimension is due to Gu, Ma and Dong
[
28
]
.

In this appendix we show that a spherically symmetric Dirac Hamiltonian
in an arbitrary dimension can be reduced to 1 dimension.
Unlike in
[
28
]
,
we arrive at the radial Dirac equation by relatively simple
algebraic computations which do not involve a detailed analysis of
representations of the Lie algebra

\mathfrak{so}(d)
.

The main role in this separation is played by a certain operator

\kappa
that commutes with the Dirac operator. This operator in dimension 3
goes back to Dirac himself.
It seems that for the
first time it has been generalized to other dimensions in

[
28
]
. We analyze this operator in detail.

Recall that operators belonging to the center of the envelopping
algebra of

\mathfrak{so}(d)
are called
Casimir operators of
\mathfrak{so}(d)
.

One of them, built in a standard way as a bilinear form in the
generators, will be called the
square of angular momentum
or
simply the
quadratic Casimir
(even though it is not the only Casimir bilinear in generators: these form a vector space generically of dimension
1
, and of dimension
2
if
d=4
).

\kappa
does not coincide with the quadratic Casimir.
One can ask whether
\kappa
is also a Casimir operator.
We will analyse this question in detail. It turns out that the answer
is positive in even, and negative in odd dimensions.

B.1
Laplacian in
d
dimensions

Spherical coordinates can be interpreted as a map

\displaystyle\mathbb{R}^{d}\backslash\{0\}\ni x

\displaystyle\mapsto(r,\hat{x})\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1},

(B.1a)

\displaystyle\hat{x}=\frac{x}{|x|},

\displaystyle\quad r=|x|.

(B.1b)

It induces a unitary map

L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+},r^{d-1})\otimes L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1}).

(B.2)

We also have the obvious map

L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+},r^{d-1})\ni f\mapsto r^{\frac{d-1}{2}}f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+}).

(B.3)

The product of (
B.3
) and (
B.2
)
will be denoted

U:L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+})\otimes L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1}).

(B.4)

The momentum is defined as

p_{i}:=-\mathrm{i}\partial_{i}.

We also introduce the radial momentum

\displaystyle R

\displaystyle:=\frac{x}{2|x|}p+p\frac{x}{2|x|}=\frac{x}{|x|}p-\mathrm{i}\frac{d-1}{2|x|}.

(B.5)

Here is the radial momentum and its square in spherical coordinates:

\displaystyle R

\displaystyle=-\mathrm{i}\partial_{r}-\mathrm{i}\frac{d-1}{2r},

(B.6a)

\displaystyle R^{2}

\displaystyle=-\partial_{r}^{2}-\frac{d-1}{r}\partial_{r}+\left(\frac{1}{4}-\Big(\frac{d-2}{2}\Big)^{2}\right)\frac{1}{r^{2}}.

(B.6b)

After applying
U
we obtain

URU^{-1}=-\mathrm{i}\partial_{r}.

In the standard way we introduce the angular momentum and its square:

\displaystyle L_{ij}

\displaystyle:=x_{i}p_{j}-x_{j}p_{i},

(B.7a)

\displaystyle L^{2}

\displaystyle:=\sum_{i<j}L_{ij}^{2}.

(B.7b)

They furnish the standard representation of the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{so}(d)

on
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
:

\displaystyle[L_{ij},x_{k}]

\displaystyle=-\mathrm{i}\delta_{jk}x_{i}+\mathrm{i}\delta_{ik}x_{j},

(B.8a)

\displaystyle[L_{ij},p_{k}]

\displaystyle=-\mathrm{i}\delta_{jk}p_{i}+\mathrm{i}\delta_{ik}p_{j},

(B.8b)

\displaystyle\Big[L_{ij},L_{kl}\Big]

\displaystyle=-\mathrm{i}\delta_{jk}L_{il}-\mathrm{i}\delta_{il}L_{jk}+\mathrm{i}\delta_{ik}L_{jl}+\mathrm{i}\delta_{jl}L_{ik}.

(B.8c)

The angular momentum squared
L^{2}
is the quadratic Casimir operator of
\mathfrak{so}(d)
.

The representation (
B.7a
) is decomposed into subspaces of
spherical harmonics of the order
\ell
. The
representation
of
\mathfrak{so}(d)
of this type will be called
spherical of degree

\ell
. On this representation we have

\displaystyle L^{2}=

\displaystyle\ell(\ell+d-2)=\Big(\ell+\frac{d-2}{2}\Big)^{2}-\Bigl(\frac{d-2}{2}\Bigr)^{2}.

(B.9)

The Laplacian on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
in the spherical coordinates is

\displaystyle-\Delta

\displaystyle=-\partial_{r}^{2}-\frac{(d-1)}{r}\partial_{r}+\frac{L_{d}^{2}}{r^{2}},

(B.10a)

\displaystyle=R^{2}+\Big(-\frac{1}{4}+\Big(\ell+\frac{d-2}{2}\Big)^{2}\Big)\frac{1}{r^{2}}.

(B.10b)

Sandwiching it with
U
we obtain

U(-\Delta)U^{-1}=-\partial_{r}^{2}+\Big(-\frac{1}{4}+\Big(\ell+\frac{d-2}{2}\Big)^{2}\Big)\frac{1}{r^{2}}.

(B.11)

Remark 55
.

Discussion above is valid even for
d=1
, with
\mathbb{S}^{0}:=\{\pm 1\}
. This case is peculiar in that the only allowed values of
\ell
are
0
and
1
, corresponding to even and odd functions.
d=2
is also special:
\ell
takes arbitrary integer values, while for
d\geq 3
one has
\ell\geq 0
.

B.2
Dirac operator in
d
dimensions

Let
\alpha_{i}
,
i=1,\ldots,d
and
\beta
be the Clifford matrices
acting irreducibly in a finite dimensional space
\mathcal{K}
. They satisfy the Clifford relations

[\alpha_{i},\alpha_{j}]_{+}=2\delta_{ij},\quad[\alpha_{i},\beta]_{+}=0,\quad\beta^{2}=1.

(B.12)

We recall that
\dim(\mathcal{K})=2^{\lfloor\frac{d+1}{2}\rfloor}
and
that for even
d
one has
\beta=\pm\mathrm{i}^{\frac{d}{2}}\prod\limits_{i=1}^{d}\alpha_{i}
. The two sign choices give non-isomorphic representations of the Clifford algebra. By averaging arguments,
\mathcal{K}
admits a
positive definite hermitian form such that
\beta
and
\alpha_{i}
are unitary and hence hermitian. This form is unique up to positive scalars; we fix one once and for all.

Using the Einstein summation convention unless there is a summation
sign, we introduce the following operators on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\otimes\mathcal{K}
:

\displaystyle D

\displaystyle:=\alpha_{i}p_{i},

(B.13a)

\displaystyle T

\displaystyle:=-\mathrm{i}\sum_{i<j}\alpha_{i}\alpha_{j}L_{ij}+\frac{d-1}{2},

(B.13b)

\displaystyle S

\displaystyle:=\frac{\alpha_{i}x_{i}}{|x|},

(B.13c)

\displaystyle\kappa

\displaystyle:=\beta T=T\beta.

(B.13d)

Proposition 56
.

We have

\displaystyle SD

\displaystyle=R+\frac{\mathrm{i}}{|x|}T,

\displaystyle DS

\displaystyle=R-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{|x|}T,

(B.14a)

\displaystyle SR

\displaystyle=RS,

\displaystyle ST

\displaystyle=-TS,

(B.14b)

\displaystyle S\beta

\displaystyle=-\beta S

\displaystyle\beta D

\displaystyle=-D\beta,

(B.14c)

\displaystyle DT

\displaystyle=-TD,

\displaystyle D\kappa

\displaystyle=\kappa D.

(B.14d)

Proof.

Let us prove the first identity of
(
B.14d
). We have

\displaystyle[\alpha_{i}\alpha_{j}L_{ij},\alpha_{k}p_{k}]_{+}

(B.15)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle[\alpha_{i}\alpha_{j},\alpha_{k}]L_{ij}p_{k}+\alpha_{k}\alpha_{i}\alpha_{j}[L_{ij},p_{k}]_{+}.

Using

\displaystyle\alpha_{j}\alpha_{k}\alpha_{i}

\displaystyle=\alpha_{k}\alpha_{i}\alpha_{j}+2\delta_{jk}\alpha_{i}-2\delta_{ij}\alpha_{k},

(B.16a)

\displaystyle\alpha_{i}\alpha_{j}\alpha_{k}

\displaystyle=\alpha_{k}\alpha_{i}\alpha_{j}+2\delta_{jk}\alpha_{i}-2\delta_{ik}\alpha_{j},

(B.16b)

we obtain

\displaystyle 3\alpha_{k}\alpha_{i}\alpha_{j}[L_{ij},p_{k}]_{+}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\alpha_{k}\alpha_{i}\alpha_{j}[L_{ij},p_{k}]_{+}+\alpha_{j}\alpha_{k}\alpha_{i}[L_{ki},p_{j}]_{+}+\alpha_{i}\alpha_{j}\alpha_{k}[L_{jk},p_{i}]_{+}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\alpha_{k}\alpha_{i}\alpha_{j}\big([L_{ij},p_{k}]_{+}+[L_{ki},p_{j}]_{+}+[L_{jk},p_{i}]_{+}\big)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle 2\alpha_{i}[L_{ji},p_{j}]_{+}-2\alpha_{i}[L_{ij},p_{j}]_{+}-2\alpha_{i}[L_{ij},p_{i}]_{+}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 6\alpha_{i}[L_{ji},p_{j}]_{+}=-6\mathrm{i}\alpha_{j}p_{j}(d-1)-12\alpha_{i}L_{ij}p_{j}.

(B.17)

Moreover,

\displaystyle[\alpha_{i}\alpha_{j},\alpha_{k}]L_{ij}p_{k}

\displaystyle=2(\alpha_{i}\delta_{jk}-\delta_{ik}\alpha_{j})L_{ij}p_{k}

\displaystyle=4\alpha_{i}L_{ij}p_{j}.

(B.18)

Now the sum of
\frac{\mathrm{i}}{6}
(
B.17
) and
\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}
(
B.18
) is
(d-1)\alpha_{i}p_{i}
.
∎

B.3
Decomposition into incoming and outgoing Dirac waves

Let

\Pi_{\pm}:=\frac{1}{2}(1\pm S)

be the spectral projections of
S
onto
\pm 1
. Define

\mathcal{H}_{\pm}:=\Pi_{\pm}(L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\otimes\mathcal{K}),\ \text{so that }\ L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\otimes\mathcal{K}=\mathcal{H}_{+}\oplus\mathcal{H}_{-}.

(B.19)

For an operator
B
on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\otimes\mathcal{K}
let us write

B_{\pm\pm}=\Pi_{\pm}B\Pi_{\pm},\qquad B_{\pm\mp}=\Pi_{\pm}B\Pi_{\mp}.

(B.20)

Clearly,

\displaystyle S=\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\
0&-1\end{bmatrix},

\displaystyle\qquad R=\begin{bmatrix}R_{++}&0\\
0&R_{--}\end{bmatrix},

(B.21a)

\displaystyle\beta=\begin{bmatrix}0&\beta_{+-}\\
\beta_{-+}&0\end{bmatrix},

\displaystyle\qquad T=\begin{bmatrix}0&T_{+-}\\
T_{-+}&0\end{bmatrix},

(B.21b)

\displaystyle D=\begin{bmatrix}R_{++}&\frac{\mathrm{i}}{|x|}T_{+-}\\
-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{|x|}T_{-+}&-R_{--}\end{bmatrix},

\displaystyle\qquad\kappa=\begin{bmatrix}\beta_{+-}T_{-+}&0\\
0&\beta_{-+}T_{+-}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}T_{+-}\beta_{-+}&0\\
0&T_{-+}\beta_{+-}\end{bmatrix}.

(B.21c)

D
commutes with the self-adjoint operator
\kappa
. We can
therefore reduce ourselves to the eigenspace of
\kappa
with eigenvalue

\omega\in\mathbb{R}
, denoted
\mathcal{H}_{\omega}
(see subsection
B.7
for a description of these eigenspaces). We can write

D=\begin{bmatrix}R_{++}&\frac{\mathrm{i}\omega}{|x|}\beta_{+-}\\
-\frac{\mathrm{i}\omega}{|x|}\beta_{-+}&-R_{--}\end{bmatrix}.

(B.22)

Using spherical coordinates, we can identify

L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\otimes\mathcal{K}
with

L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+},r^{d-1})\otimes L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})\otimes\mathcal{K}
. Applying (
B.3
) and treating
\beta_{\pm\mp}
as
identifications, we can rewrite the above equation as

D=\begin{bmatrix}-\mathrm{i}\partial_{r}&\frac{\mathrm{i}\omega}{r}\\
-\frac{\mathrm{i}\omega}{r}&\mathrm{i}\partial_{r}\end{bmatrix}.

(B.23)

The
d
-dimensional Dirac Hamiltonian can be reduced to
1 dimension (with
2\times 2
matrix structure) if it
is perturbed by four kinds of radial terms: the electric
potential

V(r)
, the mass
m(r)
(called also the
Lorentz scalar
), the radial vector potential
A(r)
and the anomalous (Pauli) coupling to the electric field
E(r)
.
The reduction (
B.23
) leads to

\displaystyle D_{V,m,A,E}\,:=\,

\displaystyle D+V(r)+m(r)\beta+\frac{A(r)\alpha_{i}x_{i}}{r}+\frac{\mathrm{i}E(r)\beta\alpha_{i}x_{i}}{r}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}-\mathrm{i}\partial_{r}+V(r)+A(r)&\frac{\mathrm{i}\omega}{r}+m(r)-\mathrm{i}E(r)\\
-\frac{\mathrm{i}\omega}{r}+m(r)+\mathrm{i}E(r)&\mathrm{i}\partial_{r}+V(r)-A(r)\end{bmatrix}.

(B.24)

We prefer another form, related by a similarity transformation:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\begin{bmatrix}1&-\mathrm{i}\\
\mathrm{i}&-1\end{bmatrix}D_{V,m,A,E}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\begin{bmatrix}1&-\mathrm{i}\\
\mathrm{i}&-1\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}-\frac{\omega}{r}+E(r)+V(r)&-\partial_{r}-\mathrm{i}A(r)-m(r)\\
\partial_{r}+\mathrm{i}A(r)-m(r)&\frac{\omega}{r}-E(r)+V(r)\end{bmatrix}.

(B.25)

For
m=A=E=0
and
V=-\frac{\lambda}{r}
, this is the 1-dimensional Dirac operator studied in our paper.

We remark that the radial electromagnetic potential
A(r)
is necessarily pure gauge. Indeed, it enters the Dirac operator only in the combination
\partial_{r}+\mathrm{i}A(r)
, which may be written as
\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\phi(r)}\partial_{r}\mathrm{e}^{i\phi(r)}
for a function
\phi(r)
such that
\phi^{\prime}(r)=A(r)
. Coupling
E(r)
arises if the Dirac Lagrangian is extended by the Pauli term, proportional to
\overline{\psi}\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\gamma^{\mu}\gamma^{\nu}F_{\mu\nu}\psi
with a purely electric and radial field strength tensor
F
.

B.4
Composite angular momentum

Introduce the spin operators

\displaystyle\sigma_{ij}

\displaystyle:=-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}[\alpha_{i},\alpha_{j}].

(B.26)

\frac{1}{2}\sigma_{ij}
yield a representation of
\mathfrak{so}(d)
on the spin space
\mathcal{K}
:

\displaystyle\Big[\frac{1}{2}\sigma_{ij},\alpha_{k}\Big]

\displaystyle=-\mathrm{i}\delta_{jk}\alpha_{i}+\mathrm{i}\delta_{ik}\alpha_{j},

(B.27a)

\displaystyle\Big[\frac{1}{2}\sigma_{ij},\frac{1}{2}\sigma_{kl}\Big]

\displaystyle=-\mathrm{i}\delta_{jk}\frac{1}{2}\sigma_{il}-\mathrm{i}\delta_{il}\frac{1}{2}\sigma_{jk}+\mathrm{i}\delta_{ik}\frac{1}{2}\sigma_{jl}+\mathrm{i}\delta_{jl}\frac{1}{2}\sigma_{ik}.

(B.27b)

Irreducible representations of
\mathfrak{so}(d)

contained in
\mathcal{K}
will be called
spinor representations
.
Their quadratic Casimir is given by

\displaystyle\frac{\sigma^{2}}{4}=\frac{1}{4}\sum_{i<j}\sigma_{ij}^{2}=

\displaystyle\frac{d(d-1)}{8}.

(B.28)

If
d
is even, then there are two inequivalent spinor
representations. They correspond to the eigenspaces of
\beta
with
eigenvalues
\pm 1
.

If
d
is odd, then
\mathcal{K}
is also a direct sum of two spinor
