representations, however they are equivalent
to one another. The decomposition of
\mathcal{K}
into irreducible components
exists but
is clearly non-unique. One possible choice
corresponds to the eigenvalues
\pm 1

of
\beta
.

We also have the composite representation of
\mathfrak{so}(d)
given by

\displaystyle J_{ij}

\displaystyle:=L_{ij}+\frac{1}{2}\sigma_{ij}.

(B.29)

Clearly,

\displaystyle[J_{ij},J_{kl}]

\displaystyle=-\mathrm{i}\delta_{jk}J_{il}-\mathrm{i}\delta_{il}J_{jk}+\mathrm{i}\delta_{ik}J_{jl}+\mathrm{i}\delta_{jl}J_{ik},

(B.30a)

\displaystyle[J,x\cdot\alpha]

\displaystyle=[J,p\cdot\alpha]=[J,p^{2}]=[J,x^{2}]=0.

(B.30b)

The quadratic Casimir of this representation, also called the
square of the
total angular momentum
, is

\displaystyle J^{2}=

\displaystyle\sum_{i<j}J_{ij}^{2}=L^{2}+L\sigma+\frac{\sigma^{2}}{4},

(B.31a)

\displaystyle\text{where }\qquad L\sigma

\displaystyle:=\sum_{i<j}L_{ij}\sigma_{ij}.

(B.31b)

Proposition 57
.

We have the following relation:

\kappa^{2}=J^{2}+\frac{(d-1)(d-2)}{8}.

(B.32)

Proof.

Directly from the definition we have

\kappa^{2}=\frac{(d-1)^{2}}{4}+(d-1)L\sigma+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i<j\\
k<l\end{subarray}}L_{ij}L_{kl}\sigma_{ij}\sigma_{kl}.

(B.33)

To simplify the last term we write

\sigma_{ij}\sigma_{kl}=\frac{1}{2}[\sigma_{ij},\sigma_{kl}]+\frac{1}{2}[\sigma_{ij},\sigma_{kl}]_{+}.

(B.34)

A simple expression for the first term is given by (
B.27b
). The second one is

\frac{1}{2}[\sigma_{ij},\sigma_{kl}]_{+}=-\alpha_{[i}\alpha_{j}\alpha_{k}\alpha_{l]}+2\delta^{[i}_{[k}\delta^{j]}_{l]}

(B.35)

in which
[\cdots]
denotes skew-symmetrization of the enclosed indices. In order to prove this formula, first note that both sides are skew-symmetric with respect to the transposition of
i
and
j
or
k
and
l
, so we may assume that
i\neq j
and
k\neq l
. We have three cases. If sets
A=\{i,j\}
and
B=\{k,l\}
are disjoint, then all
\alpha
matrices involved anticommute and hence both sides are equal to
-\alpha_{i}\alpha_{j}\alpha_{k}\alpha_{l}
. If
A\cap B
has one element, one checks that both sides vanish. Finally, if
A=B
then both sides are equal to
\pm 1
, the sign depending on the order of indices.

Now plug (
B.34
) and (
B.34
) into (
B.33
). The term with
\alpha_{[i}\alpha_{j}\alpha_{k}\alpha_{l]}
drops out after summing over indices because
L_{[ij}L_{kl]}=0
. In the term with (
B.27b
) we can replace
L_{ij}L_{kl}
by
\frac{1}{2}[L_{ij},L_{kl}]
, by skew-symmetry with respect to
ij\leftrightarrow kl
. After simplifications with (
B.28
) and (
B.31a
) we obtain the claim.
∎

Recall that on
\mathcal{H}_{\omega}
the operator
\kappa
acts as multiplication by
\omega
. We will now characterize
\mathcal{H}_{\omega}
more closely.

Proposition 58
.

Let
\omega
be such that
\mathcal{H}_{\omega}\neq\{0\}
. Then there exist
\ell
and subspaces
\mathcal{W}_{\ell},\mathcal{W}_{\ell-1}\subset L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
spherical of degree

\ell
resp.
\ell-1
such that

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{\omega}

\displaystyle\subset(\mathcal{W}_{\ell}{\oplus}\mathcal{W}_{\ell-1})\otimes\mathcal{K},

(B.36a)

\displaystyle|\omega|

\displaystyle=\ell+\frac{d-3}{2},

(B.36b)

\displaystyle\left.J^{2}\right|_{\mathcal{H}_{\omega}}

\displaystyle=\ell^{2}+\ell(d-3)+\frac{d^{2}-9d+16}{8}.

(B.36c)

Proof.

Exceptional cases
d=1,2
are easy to analyze separately: one has
\kappa=0
in the former case and
\kappa=\pm L_{12}+\frac{1}{2}\beta
(with the sign depending on the choice of Clifford matrices) in the latter. From now on we assume that
d\geq 3
. We note that (
B.32
) and
J^{2}\geq 0
imply that
\omega\neq 0
.

\kappa
commutes with
\beta
, hence also with

L\sigma
. Therefore we can decompose
\mathcal{H}_{\omega}

with respect to the eigenvalues of
L\sigma
. From (
B.13b
) we obtain

\displaystyle L\sigma=

\displaystyle\omega\beta-\frac{d-1}{2},

(B.37)

which has on
\mathcal{H}_{\omega}
two distinct eigenvalues

\pm\omega-\frac{d-1}{2}.

(B.38)

Both sings are realized because
D
anticommutes with
\beta
and preserves
\mathcal{H}_{\omega}
.

Clearly
L^{2}=J^{2}-L\sigma-\frac{\sigma^{2}}{4}
has on
\mathcal{H}_{\omega}
two distinct
eigenvalues corresponding to (
B.38
). As seen from
(
B.9
), the representation of orbital angular momentum is uniquely determined by
L^{2}
.
Therefore, for some
\ell_{+},\ell_{-}\in\mathbb{N}
,
\ell_{+}>\ell_{-}
,

\mathcal{H}_{\omega}\subset(\mathcal{W}_{\ell_{+}}{\oplus}\mathcal{W}_{\ell_{-}})\otimes\mathcal{K}.

(B.39)

Comparing the identities

\displaystyle J^{2}

\displaystyle=\ell_{\pm}(\ell_{\pm}+d-2)\mp|\omega|-\frac{d-1}{2}+\frac{d(d-1)}{8},

(B.40)

\displaystyle J^{2}

\displaystyle=\omega^{2}-\frac{(d-1)(d-2)}{8},

(B.41)

we obtain the equation

|\omega|(|\omega|\pm 1)=\Big(\ell_{\pm}+\frac{d-1}{2}\Big)\Big(\ell_{\pm}+\frac{d-3}{2}\Big).

(B.42)

whose solutions take the form
\ell_{+}+\frac{d-3}{2}\in\{|\omega|,-|\omega|-1\}
,
\ell_{-}+\frac{d-3}{2}\in\{|\omega|-1,-|\omega|\}
. In both cases the second solution has to be discarded because
\ell_{\pm}+\frac{d-3}{2}\geq 0
. Hence (
B.36b
) holds and
\ell_{-}=\ell_{+}-1
. Then (
B.36c
) is obtain by feeding (
B.36b
) into (
B.32
).
∎

We remark that the sign of
\omega
cannot be obtained from the
above calculation. Indeed,
the spectrum of
\kappa
on

L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\otimes\mathcal{K}
is always invariant with respect to
\omega\mapsto-\omega
.
If
d
is odd, then
\prod\limits_{j=1}^{d}\alpha_{j}
commutes with

L
and
\alpha_{i}
, but anticommutes with
\beta
and hence with

\kappa
.
If
d
is even, then
\kappa
anticommutes with the parity operator

f(x_{1},\dots,x_{d})\mapsto\beta\alpha_{1}f(-x_{1},\dots,x_{d}),\qquad f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathcal{K}).

(B.43)

However, this operation does not preserve the type of angular momentum representation. Indeed, it anticommutes with
\beta
and hence exchanges the two spinor representations.

B.5
Analysis in various dimensions

Let us review the lowest dimensions.

\bf d=1
. There is no angular momentum and one has
\omega=0
.

\bf d=2
. Unitary irreducible representations of
\mathfrak{so}(2)
are enumerated by
spin values
m\in\mathbb{R}
. The corresponding quadratic Casimir is equal to

m^{2}
.
There are two types
\mathcal{K}_{\pm\frac{1}{2}}
of spinor representations, corresponding
to
m=\pm\frac{1}{2}
.
Spherical representations correspond to
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}
.

One convenient choice of Clifford representation is given by Pauli matrices:
\alpha_{1}=\sigma_{1}
,
\alpha_{2}=\sigma_{2}
,
\beta=\pm\sigma_{3}
. Then
\kappa=\pm J
and hence

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{\omega}=(\mathcal{W}_{\ell}{\otimes}\mathcal{K}_{-\frac{1}{2}})\oplus(\mathcal{W}_{\ell-1}{\otimes}\mathcal{K}_{\frac{1}{2}}).

(B.44)

with
\omega=\pm(\ell-\frac{1}{2})\in\mathbb{Z}+\frac{1}{2}
. Sign in the relation between
\omega
and total angular momentum depends on the choice of sign in
\beta
, but after fixing Clifford matrices it is one-to-one.

\bf d=3
. Unitary irreducible representations of
\mathfrak{so}(3)
are parametrized by
spin
j\in\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{N}
or the quadratic Casimir

j(j+1)
. All spinor representations have the spin
\frac{1}{2}
.
The representation on
\mathcal{H}_{\omega}

has spin
\ell-\frac{1}{2}
. We have
\omega=\pm\ell\in\{\pm 1,\pm 2,\dots\}
, i.e. two distinct values of
\omega
correspond to the same total spin.

\bf d=4
.
We have
\mathfrak{so}(4)\simeq\mathfrak{so}(3)\oplus\mathfrak{so}(3)
. More explicitly,

\displaystyle J_{1}^{\pm}:=\frac{1}{2}(\pm J_{12}+J_{34}),

\displaystyle\quad J_{2}^{\pm}:=\frac{1}{2}(\pm J_{13}+J_{42}),\quad J_{3}^{\pm}:=\frac{1}{2}(\pm J_{14}+J_{23})

(B.45)

span two algebras isomorphic to
\mathfrak{so}(3)
and commuting with one
another. Let
(J^{\pm})^{2}
be the corresponding quadratic Casimirs. We
have

J^{2}=2(J^{+})^{2}+2(J^{-})^{2}.

(B.46)

Thus irreducible representations of
\mathfrak{so}(4)
are parametrized by pairs of spins

(j^{+},j^{-})\in(\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{N})^{2}
with the quadratic Casimir

2j^{+}(j^{+}+1)+2j^{-}(j^{-}+1)
. We have also the obvious analogs of (
B.45
) and (
B.46
) for
L_{ij}
and

\frac{1}{2}\sigma_{ij}
.

Representations of
\mathfrak{so}(4)
on spherical
harmonics satisfy

L_{12}L_{34}+L_{13}L_{42}+L_{14}L_{23}=0.

(B.47)

Therefore,

(L^{+})^{2}=(L^{-})^{2}
. Hence a spherical representation of degree
\ell

corresponds to the pair of spins
(\frac{\ell}{2},\frac{\ell}{2})

with the quadratic Casimir

\ell(\ell+2)=2\frac{\ell}{2}\Big(\frac{\ell}{2}+1\Big)+2\frac{\ell}{2}\Big(\frac{\ell}{2}+1\Big)
. Spinor
representations of
\mathfrak{so}(4)
are of types
(\frac{1}{2},0)
and
(0,\frac{1}{2})
,
distinguished by the eigenvalue of
\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}\alpha_{3}\alpha_{4}
. They satisfy

\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}\alpha_{3}\alpha_{4}\,\sigma_{i}^{\pm}=\mp\sigma_{i}^{\pm},\qquad(\sigma^{\pm})^{2}=\mp\frac{3}{2}\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}\alpha_{3}\alpha_{4}.

(B.48)

Furthermore, we have
\beta=\pm\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}\alpha_{3}\alpha_{4}
, with the sign in this relation distinguishing Clifford
representation. Using these relations we derive

\kappa=\mp 2(J^{+})^{2}\pm 2(J^{-})^{2}.

(B.49)

From spherical representations and spinor representation it is
possible to build total angular momentum representations of two types:

(\frac{\ell}{2},\frac{\ell-1}{2})
and

(\frac{\ell-1}{2},\frac{\ell}{2})
. They have the same quadratic Casimir

J^{2}=\ell(\ell+1)-\frac{1}{2}

(B.50)

but can be distinguished by
\omega
:

\omega=\mp\left(\ell+\frac{1}{2}\right)\qquad\text{and}\qquad\omega=\pm\left(\ell+\frac{1}{2}\right).

(B.51)

The inclusion (
B.36a
) may now be stated more precisely:

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{\omega}\cong\left(\frac{\ell}{2},\frac{\ell-1}{2}\right)\subset

\displaystyle\Big(\frac{\ell-1}{2},\frac{\ell-1}{2}\Big){\otimes}\Big(\frac{1}{2},0\Big)\oplus\Big(\frac{\ell}{2},\frac{\ell}{2}\Big){\otimes}\Big(0,\frac{1}{2}\Big),\qquad\text{for }\pm\omega<0

(B.52a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{\omega}\cong\left(\frac{\ell-1}{2},\frac{\ell}{2}\right)\subset

\displaystyle\Big(\frac{\ell-1}{2},\frac{\ell-1}{2}\Big){\otimes}\Big(0,\frac{1}{2}\Big)\oplus\Big(\frac{\ell}{2},\frac{\ell}{2}\Big){\otimes}\Big(\frac{1}{2},0\Big),\qquad\text{for }\pm\omega>0.

(B.52b)

As in dimension
2
, the relation between the total angular momentum representation and
\omega
, taking valued in
\{\pm\frac{3}{2},\pm\frac{5}{2},\dots\}
, is one-to-one after fixing Clifford matrices.

For general dimensions we label irreducible
representations as in
[
23
, Section 19]
.

\bf d=2n+1,\,n\geq 2
.
Irreducible representations
are in
1-1
correspondence with labels
(a_{1},\dots,a_{n})\in\mathbb{N}^{n}
. Spherical harmonics
of degree
\ell

have type
(\ell,0,\dots)
, while spinor representations have type
(0,\dots,1)
.
Their tensor product decomposes as

\displaystyle(\ell,\dots,0)\otimes(0,\dots,1)

\displaystyle=(\ell,\dots,1)\oplus(\ell-1,\dots,1),\qquad\ell\geq 1.

(B.53)

Thus the only possible types of
\mathcal{H}_{\omega}
are
(\ell-1,\dots,1)
. This representation occurs as a subrepresentation only in two tensor products:

(\ell,\dots,0){\otimes}(0,\dots,1),\qquad(\ell-1,\dots,0){\otimes}(0,\dots,1).

(B.54)

We have
|\omega|=\ell+n-1
, thus
\omega
takes values

\{\pm n,\pm(n+1),\dots\}
, with opposite
\omega
corresponding to the same total angular momentum. In particular it is not possible to express
\kappa
as a polynomial in
J_{ij}
.

\bf d=2n,\,n\geq 3
.
Types of irreducible representations
are parametrized by
(a_{1},\dots,a_{n})\in\mathbb{N}^{n}
.
\ell
th degree spherical harmonics are of type

(\ell,0,\dots)
. Spinor representations are of two types:

(0,\dots,1,0)
.
and
(0,\dots,0,1)
.
We have tensor products decompositions (
\ell\geq 1
):

\displaystyle(\ell,\dots,0)\otimes(0,\dots,1,0)

\displaystyle=(\ell,\dots,1,0)\oplus(\ell-1,\dots,0,1),

(B.55a)

\displaystyle(\ell,\dots,0)\otimes(0,\dots,0,1)

\displaystyle=(\ell,\dots,0,1)\oplus(\ell-1,\dots,1,0).

(B.55b)

It follows that
\mathcal{H}_{\omega}
must be of the type
(\ell-1,\dots,0,1)
or
(\ell-1,\dots,1,0)
. These two
representations have the same quadratic Casimir, however they are
exchanged by the parity operator (
B.43
). Hence they can be
distinguished by the sign of the following Casimir element, defined
as the
n
th wedge power of the 2-form
J
:

\bigwedge_{j=1}^{n}J:=\frac{1}{2^{n}}\epsilon^{i_{1}\dots i_{2n}}J_{i_{1}i_{2}}\cdots J_{i_{2n-1}i_{2n}}.

(B.56)

Here
\epsilon
is the Levi-Civita symbol.

We will show that (
B.56
) is actually proportional to
\kappa
.
Using the fact that skew-symmetrization of the product of two or more
L_{ij}
vanishes and Clifford relations we derive

\bigwedge_{j=1}^{n}J=\frac{n(2n-2)!}{2^{2n-2}}\left((-\mathrm{i})^{n}\alpha_{1}\cdots\alpha_{2n}\right)\left(L\sigma+\frac{2n-1}{2}\right).

(B.57)

A Clifford representation is determined up to isomorphism by
specifying the sign in the relation
\beta=\pm(-\mathrm{i})^{n}\alpha_{1}\cdots\alpha_{2n}
. Then we have

\bigwedge_{j=1}^{n}J=\pm\frac{n(2n-2)!}{2^{2n-2}}\kappa.

(B.58)

As in lower even dimensions, for fixed Clifford matrices angular
momentum types are in one-to-one correspondence with the values
\omega\in\{\pm\left(n-\frac{1}{2}\right),\pm\left(n+\frac{1}{2}\right),\dots\}
.

B.6
Dirac operators on manifolds

The operator
\kappa
, which is central to the separation of variables of the radially symmetric Dirac equation, is closely related to the Dirac equation on the sphere.
We would like to give a short discussion of this topic.

Before we discuss the case of a sphere, in this subsection we
give a short introduction to Dirac operators on Riemannian manifolds. We take Clifford module bundles as central objects. A popular alternative is based on the concept of a
spin structure
. Spinor bundles are then constructed by the associated bundle construction, see
[
35
, p. 7–44, 77–135]
for an exposition. A comparison between the two approaches is presented in
[
44
]
.

Given a Euclidean vector space
E
with the scalar
product of
u,v\in E
denoted
u\cdot v
, we let
\mathrm{Cl}(E)
be the
corresponding
Clifford algebra
, that is the quotient of the
tensor algebra of
E
by the ideal generated by elements of the form

u\otimes u-u\cdot u
. Then
\mathbb{R}
and
E
are naturally embedded in

\mathrm{Cl}(E)
(in concrete matrix realizations of
\mathrm{Cl}(E)
the latter
embedding is realized by contraction of vectors with
\alpha
matrices
such as (
B.12
)). In this subsection we identify elements of
E

with their images in
\mathrm{Cl}(E)
.

The automorphism
\alpha
of
\mathrm{Cl}(E)
characterized by the equation
\alpha(u)=-u
for
u\in E
is called the
main automorphism
or the
parity
. Elements of
\mathrm{Cl}(E)
fixed (negated) by
\alpha
are said to be even (odd). The
transposition
is the anti-automorphism of
\mathrm{Cl}(E)
characterized by
(u_{1}\dots u_{n})^{\mathrm{T}}=u_{n}\dots u_{1}
for
u_{1},\dots,u_{n}\in E
.

The
spin group

\mathrm{Spin}(E)
is the group of even invertible elements
g\in\mathrm{Cl}(E)
such that

gug^{-1}\in E\text{ for every }u\in E,\qquad g^{\mathrm{T}}g=1.

(B.59)

If
g\in\mathrm{Spin}(E)
, then the endomorphism
u\mapsto gug^{-1}
of
E
belongs to the special orthogonal group
\mathrm{SO}(E)
. Thus we have a homomorphism
\mathrm{Spin}(E)\to\mathrm{SO}(E)
. This homomorphism is surjective with kernel
\{\pm 1\}
. Since this is a central subgroup of
\mathrm{Cl}(E)
, the adjoint action of
\mathrm{Spin}(E)
on
\mathrm{Cl}(E)
descends to an action of
\mathrm{SO}(E)
on
\mathrm{Cl}(E)
. The Lie algebra
\mathfrak{spin}(E)
of
\mathrm{Spin}(E)
is the subspace of
\mathrm{Cl}(E)
spanned by elements of the form
[u_{1},u_{2}]
with
u_{1},u_{2}\in E
. We have an isomorphism
\mathfrak{spin}(E)\cong\mathfrak{so}(E)
, which takes
[u_{1},u_{2}]
to the endomorphism

u_{3}\mapsto[[u_{1},u_{2}],u_{3}]=4u_{1}(u_{2}\cdot u_{3})-4u_{2}(u_{1}\cdot u_{3}).

(B.60)

Therefore,
A\in\mathfrak{so}(E)
is mapped to

\frac{1}{8}\sum_{ij}[e_{i},e_{j}](e_{i}\cdot Ae_{j}),

(B.61)

where
e_{i}
form an orthonormal basis of
E
.

Every
\mathrm{Cl}(E)
-module
\mathcal{V}
is a direct sum of irreducible
modules. Let
\mathcal{V}
be an irreducible complex representation. The even
subalgebra of
\mathrm{Cl}(E)
(and in particular the spin group
\mathrm{Spin}(E)
)
is represented faithfully on
\mathcal{V}
. There exists a positive-definite
hermitian form
(\cdot|\cdot)
on
M
, called a
spinor scalar
product
, such that
(\psi_{1}|c\psi_{2})=(c^{\mathrm{T}}\psi_{1}|\psi_{2})
for
c\in\mathrm{Cl}(E)
and
\psi_{1},\psi_{2}\in\mathcal{V}
. It is unique up to
positive scalars. Furthermore, there exists an antilinear operator

\Theta
on
\mathcal{V}
, called a
spinor conjugation
, such that

\Theta c\Theta^{-1}=\begin{cases}c&\text{if }n\not\equiv 3\text{ mod }4,\\
\alpha(c)&\text{if }n\equiv 3\text{ mod }4,\end{cases}\qquad\Theta^{2}=\begin{cases}1&\text{if }n\in\{0,1,2,7\}\text{ mod }8,\\
-1&\text{if }n\in\{3,4,5,6\}\text{ mod }8.\end{cases}

(B.62)

\Theta
is unique up to a phase factor.

Now let
M
be a Riemannian manifold with tangent bundle
TM
and the Levi-Civita connection
\nabla
. For every
x\in M
consider the Clifford algebra
\mathrm{Cl}(T_{x}M)
. Together these Clifford algebras form a bundle
\mathrm{Cl}(TM)
of Clifford algebras over
M
. If
M
is oriented, we can locally choose positively oriented orthonormal framings
\{e_{i}\}_{i=1}^{d}
and put

\mathrm{vol}_{M}=e_{1}\cdots e_{d}.

(B.63)

The right hand side does not depend on the choice of framing, hence it defines a global section of
\mathrm{Cl}(TM)
.

The Levi-Civita connection extends uniquely to a connection on
\mathrm{Cl}(TM)
satisfying the Leibniz rule:

\nabla(c_{1}c_{2})=(\nabla c_{1})c_{2}+c_{1}\nabla c_{2}

(B.64)

for sections
c_{1},c_{2}
of
\mathrm{Cl}(TM)
. This connection commutes with the main automorphism and the transposition. If defined,
\mathrm{vol}_{M}
is covariantly constant.

A vector bundle
\Sigma
whose fiber
\Sigma_{x}
is a representation of
\mathrm{Cl}(T_{x}M)
(with the module structure smoothly varying with
x
) is called a
Clifford module bundle
. A connection
\nabla
on
\Sigma
will be called
Clifford covariant
if it satisfies

\nabla(c\psi)=(\nabla c)\psi+c\nabla\psi.

(B.65)

If in addition for every
x\in M
and every null-homotopic loop
\gamma
based at
x
the holonomy endomorphism
\mathrm{hol}_{\Sigma,\gamma}\in\mathrm{GL}(\Sigma_{x})
is an element of
\mathrm{Spin}(T_{x}M)
, we call
\nabla
a
locally spin connection
. If this is true for all loops, we say that
\nabla
is a
spin connection
. A Clifford module bundle equipped with a spin connection will be called a
spinor bundle
.

Lemma 59
.

If
\nabla
is a spin connection, then
the holonomy endomorphism
\mathrm{hol}_{\Sigma,\gamma}\in\mathrm{Spin}(T_{x}M)
lifts the holonomy
\mathrm{hol}_{TM,\gamma}\in\mathrm{SO}(T_{x}M)
of the Levi-Civita connection.

Proof.

By the Clifford covariance (
B.65
), for any

c\in\mathrm{Cl}(T_{x}M)
we have

\mathrm{hol}_{\mathrm{Cl}(TM),\gamma}(c)\mathrm{hol}_{\Sigma,\gamma}\psi=\mathrm{hol}_{\Sigma,\gamma}(c\psi)=\mathrm{hol}_{\Sigma,\gamma}c\,\mathrm{hol}_{\Sigma,\gamma}^{-1}\mathrm{hol}_{\Sigma,\gamma}\psi.

(B.66)

Thus

\mathrm{hol}_{\mathrm{Cl}(TM),\gamma}(c)=\mathrm{hol}_{\Sigma,\gamma}c\,\mathrm{hol}_{\Sigma,\gamma}^{-1}.

(B.67)

As
c
we can choose
u\in T_{x}M\subset\mathrm{Cl}(T_{x}M)
and rewrite
(
B.67
) as

\mathrm{hol}_{TM,\gamma}u=\mathrm{hol}_{\Sigma,\gamma}u\,\mathrm{hol}_{\Sigma,\gamma}^{-1}.\qed

From now on we assume that
M
is orientable. As a
consequence, the holonomies of the Levi-Civita connection are
always contained in
SO(T_{x}M)
. (On non-orientable manifolds they
may be contained in
O(T_{x}M)
)

The following lemma
allows us to conveniently check whether a given connection is spin
[
44
]
.

Lemma 60
.

Let
\Sigma
be a bundle of irreducible Clifford modules with a Clifford covariant connection
\nabla
. Then
\nabla
is a spin connection if and only if there exist a spinor scalar product
(\cdot|\cdot)
and a spinor conjugation
\Theta
on
\Sigma
such that

\nabla\Theta\psi=\Theta\nabla\psi,\qquad\mathrm{d}(\psi_{1}|\psi_{2})=(\nabla\psi_{1}|\psi_{2})+(\psi_{1}|\nabla\psi_{2}).

(B.68)

Proof.

\Rightarrow
. We focus on one connected component
M_{0}
of
M
and choose a point
x
therein. Then we choose a spinor scalar product
(\cdot|\cdot)
and a spinor conjugation
\Theta
in
\Sigma_{x}
. By assumption, they are invariant under
\mathrm{hol}_{\Sigma,\gamma}
for every loop based at
x
. Now parallel transport
(\cdot|\cdot)
and
\Theta
to all other fibers over
M_{0}
. Invariance under holonomies implies that the result is independent of the choice of paths, smooth and covariantly constant, hence satisfies (
B.68
).

\Leftarrow
. Let
\gamma
be a loop based at
x
and let
c\in\mathrm{Cl}(T_{x}M)
. Let
g\in\mathrm{Spin}(T_{x}M)
be a lift of
\mathrm{hol}_{TM,\gamma}\in\mathrm{SO}(T_{x}M)
. Arguing as in the proof of Lemma
59
we see that
\mathrm{hol}_{\Sigma,\gamma}c\,\mathrm{hol}_{\Sigma,\gamma}^{-1}=gcg^{-1}
. By irreducibility of
\Sigma_{x}
, this implies that
\mathrm{hol}_{\Sigma,\gamma}=zg
for some
z\in\mathbb{C}
. Since both
\mathrm{hol}_{\Sigma,\gamma}
and
g
preserve the scalar product,
|z|=1
. Since both commute with
\Theta
,
z\in\mathbb{R}
. Thus
\mathrm{hol}_{\Sigma,\gamma}
coincides with
g
or
-g
and hence belongs to
\mathrm{Spin}(T_{x}M)
.
∎

Lemma 61
.

Every spinor bundle is a direct sum of spinor bundles whose fibers are irreducible Clifford modules.

Proof.

Analogous to the proof of
\Rightarrow
in Lemma
60
.
∎

Recall that for a vector bundle
\Sigma
with a connection
\nabla
, the expression

\Omega(\mathbf{U},\mathbf{V}):=\nabla_{\mathbf{U}}\nabla_{\mathbf{V}}-\nabla_{\mathbf{V}}\nabla_{\mathbf{U}}-\nabla_{[\mathbf{U},\mathbf{V}]_{\mathrm{Lie}}}.

(B.69)

defines an
\mathrm{End}(\Sigma)
-valued
2
-form, called the
curvature
of
\nabla
. Here
[\cdot,\cdot]_{\mathrm{Lie}}
is
the Lie bracket of vector fields
\mathbf{U},\mathbf{V}
. If
\Sigma=TM
and
\nabla
is the Levi-Civita connection, then
\Omega
is
denoted by
R
and called the
Riemann tensor
. One checks that
\left.R(\mathbf{U},\mathbf{V})\right|_{x}
is an element of
\mathfrak{so}(T_{x}M)
.

Lemma 62
.

If
\nabla
is a spin connection, then its curvature takes the form

\Omega(\mathbf{U},\mathbf{V})=\frac{1}{8}\sum_{i,j}(e_{i}\cdot R(\mathbf{U},\mathbf{V})e_{j})[e_{i},e_{j}].

(B.70)

A partial converse holds: every Clifford covariant connection with curvature given by the formula above is a locally spin connection.

Proof.

The curvature may be extracted from holonomies along
infitesimal parallelograms. Therefore by Lemma
59
, the
curvature of
\nabla
at
x
is an element of
\mathfrak{spin}(T_{x}M)
,
coinciding with
R(\mathbf{U},\mathbf{V})
taken in the
representation (
B.61
).

Now we prove the converse. If
\gamma
is any path from
y
to
x
and
\mathrm{hol}_{\Sigma,\gamma}\in\mathrm{Hom}(\Sigma_{x},\Sigma_{y})
is the corresponding parallel transport, then by the Clifford covariance

\mathrm{hol}_{\Sigma,\gamma}\Omega(\mathbf{U},\mathbf{V})\mathrm{hol}_{\Sigma,\gamma}^{-1}=\frac{1}{8}\sum_{i,j}(e_{i}\cdot R(\mathbf{U},\mathbf{V})e_{j})[\mathrm{hol}_{\mathrm{T}M,\gamma}(e_{i}),\mathrm{hol}_{\mathrm{T}M,\gamma}(e_{j})]\in\mathfrak{spin}(T_{x}M).

(B.71)

Let
\gamma_{s}
be a family of loops
[0,1]\to M
based at
x
. For
t\in[0,1]
let
\gamma_{s}^{t}:=\left.\gamma_{s}\right|_{[0,t]}
. Then

\mathrm{hol}_{\Sigma,\gamma_{s}}^{-1}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}s}\mathrm{hol}_{\Sigma,\gamma_{s}}=\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{hol}_{\Sigma,\gamma_{s}^{t}}^{-1}\Omega\left(\frac{\partial\gamma_{s}(t)}{\partial s},\frac{\partial\gamma_{s}(t)}{\partial t}\right)\mathrm{hol}_{\Sigma,\gamma_{s}^{t}}\mathrm{d}t.

(B.72)

It follows that for a null-homotopic loop
\gamma
based at
x
we have that
\mathrm{hol}_{\Sigma,\gamma}\in\mathrm{Spin}(T_{x}M)
.
∎

Next we define the Dirac operator on sections of a spinor bundle
\Sigma
. Let us choose a locally defined orthonormal framing
\{e_{i}\}
of
TM
. Now put

D\psi=-\mathrm{i}\sum_{i}e_{i}\cdot\nabla_{e_{i}}\psi.

(B.73)

Here the multiplication by
e_{i}
is the Clifford multiplication (
e_{i}
being regarded as a section of
\mathrm{Cl}(TM)
). It is not dificult to check that
D\psi
does not depend on the choice of framing, so local expressions on the right hand side of (
B.73
) can be glued to obtain a globally defined differential operator.

If
\Sigma
is a spinor bundle over an oriented Riemannian manifold
M
, there exist two
distinguished second order differential operators acting on sections of
\Sigma
: the square of the Dirac operator
D^{2}
and the Bochner Laplacian.
To describe the latter, let
(\cdot|\cdot)
be a spinor scalar product. It yields a scalar product on
T^{*}M\otimes\Sigma
. Now the Bochner Laplacian,
at least formally, is (minus) the operator associated to the quadratic form

-(\psi,\Delta\psi):=\int_{M}\big(\nabla\psi(x)|\nabla\psi(x)\big)\mathrm{d}x.

(B.74)

Equivalently, the Bochner Laplacian can be defined without invoking the scalar product by

\Delta:=\sum_{i}\left(\nabla_{e_{i}}\nabla_{e_{i}}-\nabla_{\nabla_{e_{i}}e_{i}}\right),

(B.75)

Note that the Bochner Laplacian uses the
covariant Hessian

\mathrm{Hess}(\mathbf{U},\mathbf{V})=\nabla_{\mathbf{U}}\nabla_{\mathbf{V}}-\nabla_{\nabla_{\mathbf{U}}\mathbf{V}},

(B.76)

which is bilinear over

C^{\infty}(M)

and satisfies

\mathrm{Hess}(\mathbf{U},\mathbf{V})-\mathrm{Hess}(\mathbf{V},\mathbf{U})=\Omega(\mathbf{U},\mathbf{V}).

(B.77)

(
B.77
) follows from the torsion-freeness of the Levi-Civita connection, that is

\nabla_{\mathbf{U}}\mathbf{V}-\nabla_{\mathbf{V}}\mathbf{U}-[\mathbf{U},\mathbf{V}]_{\mathrm{Lie}}=0.

(B.78)

In the following proposition
we recall the celebrated
Lichnerowicz formula
:

Proposition 63
.

The square of the Dirac operator and the Bochner Laplacian are related by

D^{2}=-\Delta+\frac{1}{4}\mathrm{Sc},

(B.79)

where
\mathrm{Sc}
is the scalar curvature.

Proof.

Let
\mathrm{Hess}^{\mathrm{s}}
be the symmetric part of the Hessian.
We choose an orthonormal framing
\{e_{i}\}
. Then

\displaystyle(\mathrm{i}D)^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,j}e_{i}\nabla_{e_{i}}e_{j}\nabla_{e_{j}}=\sum_{i,j}\left(e_{i}e_{j}\nabla_{e_{i}}\nabla_{e_{j}}+e_{i}(\nabla_{e_{i}}e_{j})\nabla_{e_{j}}\right)

(B.80)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,j}e_{i}e_{j}\left(\mathrm{Hess}(e_{i},e_{j})+\nabla_{\nabla_{e_{i}}e_{j}}\right)+\sum_{i,j}e_{i}(\nabla_{e_{i}}e_{j})\nabla_{e_{j}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,j}e_{i}e_{j}\left(\mathrm{Hess}^{\mathrm{s}}(e_{i},e_{j})+\frac{1}{2}\Omega(e_{i},e_{j})\right)+\sum_{i,j}\left(e_{i}e_{j}\nabla_{\nabla_{e_{i}}e_{j}}+e_{i}(\nabla_{e_{i}}e_{j})\nabla_{e_{j}}\right)

\displaystyle=\Delta+\frac{1}{32}\sum_{i,j,n,m}[e_{i},e_{j}][e_{n},e_{m}]\left(e_{n}\cdot R(e_{i},e_{j})e_{m}\right).

Below we will show that the last two terms in the third line cancel. The last term in the fourth line may be shown to be equal to
-\frac{1}{4}\mathrm{Sc}=-\frac{1}{4}\sum_{ij}e_{i}\cdot R(e_{i},e_{j})e_{j}
using Clifford relations and symmetries of the Riemann tensor.

Connection coefficients are defined by the formula

\nabla_{e_{i}}e_{j}=\sum_{k}c_{ijk}e_{k}.

(B.81)

\nabla_{e_{i}}(e_{j}\cdot e_{k})=0
and metric compatibility of the connection give
c_{ijk}+c_{ikj}=0
. We have

\sum_{j}\left(e_{j}\nabla_{\nabla_{e_{i}}e_{j}}+(\nabla_{e_{i}}e_{j})\nabla_{e_{j}}\right)=\sum_{j,k}c_{ijk}\left(e_{j}\nabla_{e_{k}}+e_{k}\nabla_{e_{j}}\right).

(B.82)

Now switch the roles of
j,k
in the second term to see that
(
B.82
) vanishes.
∎

Now suppose that
N
is an orientable submanifold of
M
of codimension
1
.
Then there exists a smooth field of unit normal vectors
\nu
. We have the following relation between the Levi-Civita connection on
M
and on
N
:

\displaystyle\nabla^{N}_{\mathbf{U}}\mathbf{V}

\displaystyle=\nabla^{M}_{\mathbf{U}}\mathbf{V}-(\nabla^{M}_{\mathbf{U}}\mathbf{V}\cdot\nu)\nu=\nabla^{M}_{\mathbf{U}}\mathbf{V}+(\mathbf{V}\cdot\nabla^{M}_{\mathbf{U}}\nu)\nu,

(B.83)

where
\mathbf{U},\mathbf{V}
are tangent to
N
. That is,
\nabla^{N}_{\mathbf{U}}\mathbf{V}
is the projection of
\nabla^{M}_{\mathbf{U}}\mathbf{V}
onto
TN
.

(
B.83
) can be rewritten as follows:

\displaystyle\nabla^{N}_{\mathbf{U}}\mathbf{V}

\displaystyle=\nabla^{M}_{\mathbf{U}}\mathbf{V}+\frac{1}{2}\nu\left((\nabla^{M}_{\mathbf{U}}\nu)\mathbf{V}+\mathbf{V}(\nabla^{M}_{\mathbf{U}}\nu)\right)=\nabla^{M}_{\mathbf{U}}\mathbf{V}+\frac{1}{2}[\nu\nabla^{M}_{\mathbf{U}}\nu,\mathbf{V}].

where now
\mathbf{V},

\nu
and
\nabla_{\mathbf{U}}^{M}\nu
are treated as sections of the Clifford bundle
\mathrm{Cl}(TM)
.
This is immediately generalized to general Clifford fields

\nabla^{N}_{\mathbf{U}}=\nabla^{M}_{\mathbf{U}}+\frac{1}{2}[\nu\nabla^{M}_{\mathbf{U}}\nu,\cdot]

(B.84)

Now assume that
\Sigma^{M}
is a Clifford module bundle over
M
with a Clifford covariant connection
\nabla^{M}
. The restriction of
\Sigma^{M}
to
N
, denoted
\Sigma^{N}
, is a bundle of Clifford modules. (
B.84
) motivates defining the following connection on
\Sigma^{N}
:

\nabla^{N}_{\mathbf{U}}=\nabla^{M}_{\mathbf{U}}+\frac{1}{2}\nu\nabla^{M}_{\mathbf{U}}\nu.

(B.85)

By construction,
\nabla^{N}
is Clifford covariant.

Lemma 64
.

If
\Sigma^{M}
is a spinor bundle, so is
\Sigma^{N}
.

Proof.

By Lemma
61
we may assume that
\Sigma^{M}
is a bundle of irreducible Clifford modules. If
d:=\dim(M)
is even, then
\Sigma^{N}
splits into eigenbundles of
\mathrm{vol}_{N}
, which are bundles of irreducible Clifford modules. If
d
is odd,
\Sigma^{N}
is irreducible.

Now choose a spinor scalar product
(\cdot|\cdot)

and a spinor conjugation
\Theta^{M}
on
\Sigma^{M}
. Let
\Theta^{N}=\Theta^{M}
if
d\in\{1,2\}
mod
4
and
\Theta^{N}=\nu\Theta^{M}
if
d\in\{0,3\}
mod
4
,
in both cases restricted to
\Sigma^{N}
. The restriction of
(\cdot|\cdot)
to

\Sigma^{N}
and
\Theta^{N}
are a spinor scalar
product and a spinor conjugation satisfying
(
B.68
).
If
d
is even, this is still true if we further restrict to eigenbundles of
\mathrm{vol}_{N}
.
The result follows from Lemma
60
.
∎

Assume now that we have a covariantly constant section
\beta
of

\mathrm{End}(\Sigma)
satisfying
\beta^{2}=1
and anticommuting with

TM\subset\mathrm{Cl}(TM)
. Let us consider the operator
\Gamma=-\mathrm{i}\beta\nu
acting on sections of
\Sigma^{N}
. It satisfies
\Gamma^{2}=1
and
commutes with all sections of
\mathrm{Cl}(TN)
. Hence its eigenbundles

\Sigma_{\pm}^{N}
for eigenvalues
\pm 1
are also Clifford module
bundles over
N
. Using (
B.85
) one checks that
\Gamma
commutes also with the covariant differentiation, so
\Sigma_{\pm}^{N}
inherit the spin connection.

Operator
\mathrm{vol}_{M}
commutes with covariant differentiation and
anticommutes with
\Gamma
, hence it takes sections of
\Sigma_{\pm}^{N}

to sections of
\Sigma_{\mp}^{N}
. If
d
is odd,
\mathrm{vol}_{M}
commutes with
Clifford fields and hence defines an isomorphism of spinor bundles

\Sigma_{+}^{N}\cong\Sigma_{-}^{N}
. If
d
is even,
\Sigma_{+}^{N}
and

\Sigma_{-}^{N}
are non-isomorphic as Clifford module bundles.
In this case, we can take
\beta:=\pm\mathrm{i}^{\frac{d}{2}}\mathrm{vol}_{M}

and
\Gamma
coincides up to phase with the
\mathrm{Cl}(TN)
section

\mathrm{vol}_{N}
.

If
\Sigma
is irreducible for
\mathrm{Cl}(TM)
and
\beta
, by dimensional consideration,
\Sigma_{\pm}^{N}
are bundles of
irreducible
Clifford modules.

B.7
Dirac operators on spheres

Now let us consider the sphere
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
of radius
1
(thus we put

|x|=1
below).
We will apply the formalism of the previous section with
M:=\mathbb{R}^{d}
and

N:=\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
.
For brevity, we will write
\mathbb{S}
for
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
.
We will use the notation of Subsections

B.1

and
B.2
, such as
S
,
R
,
T
and
\kappa
.

The normal vector
\nu
is identified with
S
.
The Levi-Civita connection on
\mathbb{S}
is

\nabla_{\mathbf{U}}^{\mathbb{S}}\mathbf{V}=\partial_{\mathbf{U}}\mathbf{V}+(\mathbf{U}\cdot\mathbf{V})x

(B.86)

for vector fields
\mathbf{U},\mathbf{V}
tangent to the sphere. Here
\partial_{\mathbf{U}}=\sum\limits_{i=1}^{d}\mathbf{U}_{i}\partial_{i}
. Consider the vector space
\mathcal{K}
from previous subsections.
\mathbb{S}\times\mathcal{K}
is a Clifford module bundle with connection

\nabla_{\mathbf{U}}^{\mathbb{S}}=\partial_{\mathbf{U}}+\frac{1}{2}S\mathbf{U}.

(B.87)

Proposition 65
.

The connection (
B.87
) is a spin connection with curvature

\Omega(\mathbf{U},\mathbf{V})=\frac{1}{4}[\mathbf{U},\mathbf{V}].

(B.88)

If
d=2
, the holonomy of
\nabla
along
\mathbb{S}^{1}
is equal to
-1
.

Proof.

All but the last statement follow from Lemma
64
. (
B.88
) may also be obtained from a simple direct computation. Now let
d=2
. We parametrize
\mathbb{S}^{1}
as
x=(\cos(\alpha),\sin(\alpha))
. Then (
B.87
) takes the form

\nabla_{\frac{\partial}{\partial\alpha}}\psi=\frac{\partial\psi}{\partial\alpha}+\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{vol}_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\psi.

(B.89)

It follows that solutions of the parallel transport equation
\nabla_{\frac{\partial}{\partial\alpha}}\psi=0
satisfy
\psi(2\pi)=-\psi(0)
.
∎

Eigenbundles
\mathcal{K}_{\pm}\subset\mathbb{S}\times\mathcal{K}
of
\Gamma=-\mathrm{i}\beta S
to eigenvalues
\pm 1
are irreducible spinor bundles, isomorphic if
d
is odd and non-isomorphic otherwise.

Choose a local orthonormal framing
\{e_{i}\}_{i=1}^{d-1}
of
T\mathbb{S}
. Denote the Dirac operator on
\mathbb{S}
by

D_{\mathbb{S}}

and on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
by
D
. Using (
B.87
) we manipulate its definition to the form

\displaystyle D_{\mathbb{S}}

\displaystyle=-\mathrm{i}\sum_{i}e_{i}\nabla_{e_{i}}\psi=-\mathrm{i}\sum_{i}e_{i}\partial_{e_{i}}\psi-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\sum_{i}e_{i}Se_{i}\psi

(B.90)

\displaystyle=D+\mathrm{i}S\partial_{\nu}\psi+\mathrm{i}\frac{d-1}{2}S\psi=D-SR.

Next let
\{e_{i}\}_{i=1}^{d}
be the canonical basis of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Multiplying the above by
S
from the left we find

\displaystyle SD_{\mathbb{S}}

\displaystyle=SD-R=-\mathrm{i}\sum_{i,j}x_{i}e_{i}e_{j}\partial_{j}-SR

(B.91)

\displaystyle=-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\sum_{i,j}\left([e_{i},e_{j}]+[e_{i},e_{j}]_{+}x_{i}\partial_{j}\right)-R=\mathrm{i}T.

Hence we have

D_{\mathbb{S}}=\mathrm{i}ST.

(B.92)

Let us note that this is exactly the off-diagonal term of
D
with respect to decomposition into eigenspaces of
S
. Next observe that

\kappa=\Gamma D_{\mathbb{S}}=D_{\mathbb{S}}\Gamma.

(B.93)

It follows that on sections of
\mathcal{K}_{\pm}
, operator
\kappa
acts as
\pm D_{\mathbb{S}}
.

We also remark that if
\psi
is a
\mathcal{K}
-valued polynomial homogeneous of degree

\ell
annihillated by
D
, then
(S\mp\mathrm{i})\psi
is an eigenvector of

D_{\mathbb{S}}
to eigenvalue
\pm(\ell+\frac{d-1}{2})
. Indeed,

D_{\mathbb{S}}\psi=-SR\psi=\mathrm{i}\left(\ell+\frac{d-1}{2}\right)S\psi,

(B.94)

which implies

\displaystyle D_{\mathbb{S}}(S\mp\mathrm{i})\psi

\displaystyle=(-S\mp\mathrm{i})D_{\mathbb{S}}\psi

(B.95)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{i}\left(\ell+\frac{d-1}{2}\right)(-S\mp\mathrm{i})S\psi=\pm\left(\ell+\frac{d-1}{2}\right)(S\mp\mathrm{i})\psi.

By the relation between
D_{\mathbb{S}}
and
\kappa
, this calculation reproduces the spectrum of
\kappa
found in Proposition
58
.

We claim that a complete set of eigenfunctions of
D_{\mathbb{S}}
is
obtained by the construction above, similarly as spherical harmonics
are obtained by restricting scalar-valued homogeneous harmonic
polynomials, e.g.
[
1
, p. 73–81]
. This may be seen from the
Stone-Weierstrass theorem and the following lemma. Besides this
application, the lemma elucidates the decomposition of spaces of
spinor-valued polynomials into irreducible representations of

\mathrm{Spin}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and relates eigenvectors of
D_{\mathbb{S}}
(and hence also of
\kappa
) to harmonic polynomials.

Consistently with our notation, in the following lemma
x
denotes the element of the Clifford algebra
x=\sum_{i}x_{i}e_{i}
, whereas
x_{i}
are real numbers.
x^{j}
is the
j
-th power of
x
.

Lemma 66
.

Let
\mathcal{K}_{\ell}
be the space of
\mathcal{K}
-valued polynomials homogeneous of degree
\ell
and let
\mathcal{K}_{\ell}^{0}
be the kernel of
D
acting in
\mathcal{K}_{\ell}
. Then

\mathcal{K}_{\ell}=\mathcal{K}_{\ell}^{0}\oplus x\cdot\mathcal{K}_{\ell-1}.

(B.96)

In particular we have a vector space decomposition

\mathcal{K}_{\ell}=\bigoplus_{j=0}^{\ell}x^{j}\mathcal{K}_{\ell-j}^{0}.

(B.97)

Moreover,
\dim(\mathcal{K}_{\ell}^{0})=\binom{d+\ell-2}{\ell}\dim(\mathcal{K})
, and (clearly)

\dim(\mathcal{K}_{\ell})=\binom{d+\ell-1}{\ell}\dim(\mathcal{K})
.

Let
\mathcal{H}_{\ell}
be the space of scalar-valued harmonic polynomials homogeneous of degree
\ell
. Then

D:\mathcal{H}_{\ell}\otimes\mathcal{K}\to\mathcal{K}_{\ell-1}^{0}

(B.98)

is a surjection with kernel
\mathcal{K}^{0}_{\ell}
. In particular there is an exact sequence

\dots\rightarrow\mathcal{H}_{\ell+1}\otimes\mathcal{K}\xrightarrow{D}\mathcal{H}_{\ell}\otimes\mathcal{K}\xrightarrow{D}\mathcal{H}_{\ell-1}\otimes\mathcal{K}\rightarrow\dots

(B.99)

Proof.

Let
\{e_{i}\}_{i=1}^{d}
be an orthonormal basis of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. A general element of
\mathcal{K}_{\ell}
has the form

\psi=\sum_{i_{1},\dots,i_{\ell}=1}^{d}x_{i_{1}}\cdots x_{i_{\ell}}\psi_{i_{1}\dots i_{\ell}}

(B.100)

with coefficients
\psi_{i_{1}\dots i_{\ell}}
fully symmetric. Acting with
D
we find that
D\psi=0
if and only if

\psi_{1i_{2}\dots i_{\ell}}=\sum_{j\neq 1}e_{1}e_{j}\psi_{ji_{2}\dots i_{\ell}}.

(B.101)

It is easy to see that this system of equation may be uniquely solved once
\psi_{i_{1}\dots i_{\ell}}
is fixed for all indices
i_{1},\dots,i_{\ell}
different than
1
. The formula for
\dim(\mathcal{K}^{0}_{\ell})
follows.

Using the above result it is easy to check that

\dim(\mathcal{K}_{\ell})=\dim(\mathcal{K}_{\ell}^{0})+\dim(x\cdot\mathcal{K}_{\ell-1}).

(B.102)

Hence (
B.96
) will follow once we establish that
\mathcal{K}_{\ell}^{0}\cap x\cdot\mathcal{K}_{\ell-1}=\{0\}
.

We proceed by induction. There is nothing to prove for
\ell=0
. Suppose that (
B.96
) holds for
\ell\leq\ell^{\prime}
. Then also (
B.97
) holds for
\ell\leq\ell^{\prime}
. Let us put
\ell=\ell^{\prime}+1
and let
\psi\in\mathcal{K}_{\ell}^{0}\cap x\cdot\mathcal{K}_{\ell-1}
. By the induction hypothesis we have

\psi=\sum_{j=1}^{\ell}x^{j}\psi_{j}

(B.103)

with
\psi_{j}\in\mathcal{K}_{\ell-j}
. Now let us observe that

\displaystyle[\mathrm{i}D,x^{2}]=2x,\qquad

\displaystyle\text{hence }[\mathrm{i}D,x^{2k}]=2kx^{2k-1},

(B.104a)

\displaystyle[\mathrm{i}D,x]_{+}=2\sum_{i}x_{i}\partial_{i}+d,\qquad

\displaystyle\text{hence}[\mathrm{i}D,x^{2k+1}]_{+}=x^{2k}(2\sum_{i}x_{i}\partial_{i}+d+2k).

(B.104b)

Thus since
\psi
is annhilated by
D
and
\sum_{i}x_{i}\partial_{i}\psi_{j}=(\ell-j)\psi_{j}
, we obtain

0=\mathrm{i}D\psi=\sum_{j\text{ even}}jx^{j-1}\psi_{j}+\sum_{j\text{ odd}}(\ell+d-1)x^{j-1}\psi_{j}.

(B.105)

By induction hypothesis,
x^{j-1}\psi_{j}
and
x^{j^{\prime}-1}\psi_{j^{\prime}}
belong to subspaces of
\mathcal{K}_{\ell-1}
with trivial intersection if
j\neq j^{\prime}
. Therefore each term in the above sum has to vanish separately. Since operators
x^{j-1}
are injective, all
\psi_{j}
vanish. Thus
\psi=0
.

For the last part, note that
\mathcal{H}_{\ell}\otimes\mathcal{K}
is the space of
harmonic
\mathcal{K}
-valued polynomials homogeneous of degree
\ell
. It is
annihilated by
D^{2}
, so
D
maps it into
\mathcal{K}_{\ell-1}^{0}
. Statement
about the kernel is obvious. Then
\dim(\mathcal{H}_{\ell}\otimes\mathcal{K})=\dim(\mathcal{K}_{\ell}^{0})+\dim(\mathcal{K}_{\ell-1}^{0})

follows from the well-known formula

\dim(\mathcal{H}_{\ell})=\binom{d+\ell-1}{\ell}-\binom{d+\ell-3}{\ell-2}.

(B.106)

This implies the surjectivity.
∎

As for any oriented Riemannian manifold, two natural second order operators act on sections of spinor bundles: the square of the Dirac operator
D^{2}_{\mathbb{S}}
and the Bochner Laplacian
\Delta_{\mathbb{S}}
.
In the case of spheres we have an additional natural second order operator: the square of the total angular momentum

J^{2}
.
It turns out that for
d\geq 3
the operator
J^{2}
is distinct from
both
D_{\mathbb{S}}^{2}
and
-\Delta_{\mathbb{S}}
. More
precisely, we have

\displaystyle D^{2}_{\mathbb{S}}

\displaystyle=-\Delta_{\mathbb{S}}+\frac{(d-1)(d-2)}{4},

(B.107)

\displaystyle D^{2}_{\mathbb{S}}

\displaystyle=J^{2}+\frac{(d-1)(d-2)}{8}.

(B.108)

(
B.107
) is the Lichnerowicz
formula for the spheres (indeed,
the scalar curvature of
\mathbb{S}
is

{(d-1)(d-2)}
). Moreover,
D^{2}_{\mathbb{S}}=\kappa^{2}
. Hence
(
B.108
) is essentially the formula (
B.32
).
Thus, as we were surprised to find out, (
B.32
) is
distinct from the Lichnerowicz formula.

Appendix C
Mellin transformation

For a Schwartz function
b
on
\mathbb{R}
we define its Fourier
transform by

(\mathfrak{F}b)(k)=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}b(t)\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}kt}\mathrm{d}t.

(C.1)

It is extended to the space of Schwartz distributions in the usual way. Restriction of
\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\mathfrak{F}
to
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
is a unitary operator.

An isomorphism
\iota:C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})\to C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
is
defined by
(\iota f)(x)=x^{-\frac{1}{2}}f(\ln(x))
. Dualizing,
we extend it to an isomorphism between spaces of distributions on
\mathbb{R}

and on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
. Restriction of
\iota
to
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
is a unitary
operator onto
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
. By
Schwartz class functions
and
tempered distributions on

\mathbb{R}_{+}
we shall mean smooth functions (respectively distributions) on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
which correspond through
\iota
to Schwartz class functions (respectively tempered distributions) on
\mathbb{R}
.

Mellin transform is defined as the composition
\mathfrak{M}=\mathfrak{F}\iota^{-1}
. It is an isomorphism between spaces of tempered distributions on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
and on
\mathbb{R}
. If
f\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
, then

(\mathfrak{M}f)(k)=\int_{0}^{\infty}f(x)x^{-\frac{1}{2}-\mathrm{i}k}\mathrm{d}x.

(C.2)

Recall that
A
,
J
and
K
are defined in Subsection

1.1
. We note the following identities:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{M}Jf(k)=\mathfrak{M}f(-k),

(C.3a)

\displaystyle A=\mathfrak{M}^{-1}K\mathfrak{M}.

(C.3b)

The following lemma will be used in Proposition
73
.
The Mellin transformation plays here a secondary role.

Lemma 67
.

Suppose that
f_{\epsilon}
is a family of tempered distributions on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
with parameter
\epsilon\in]0,1]
satisfying the following conditions:

•

\mathfrak{M}f_{\epsilon}\in L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R})
,

•

there exists
g\in L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R})
such that
\mathfrak{M}f_{\epsilon}\to g
pointwise for
\epsilon\to 0
,

•

there exist
c,N\geq 0
independent of
\epsilon
such that
|\mathfrak{M}f_{\epsilon}(k)|\leq c(1+k^{2})^{N}
for almost every
k
.

Then there exists a tempered distribution
f_{0}
on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that
f_{\epsilon}\to f_{0}
for
\epsilon\to 0
in the sense of tempered distributions. Moreover,
\mathfrak{M}f_{0}=g
.

Lemma 68
.

Let
b
be a tempered distribution whose Mellin transform is a Borel function. Put

(B^{\mathrm{pre}}f)(x)=\int_{0}^{\infty}b(xk)f(k)\mathrm{d}k,\qquad f\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{+}).

(C.4)

Then
B^{\mathrm{pre}}f
is in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
and
B^{\mathrm{pre}}
is a closable operator on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
with closure

B=\mathfrak{M}b(A)J.

(C.5)

Appendix D
Whittaker functions

In this appendix we review some properties of special functions used
in this paper.
In particular, we discuss Whittaker
functions, which play the central role in our paper. We follow the conventions of

[
13
]
and
[
10
]
.

D.1
Confluent equation

Before discussing the Whittaker equation and its solutions, let us say
a few words about the
closely related confluent equation and the hypergeometric equation.

The
confluent
equation
has the form

\big(z\partial_{z}^{2}+(c-z)\partial_{z}-a\big)v(z)=0.

(D.1)

Let us list three of its standard solutions:

\displaystyle{}_{1}F_{1}(a;c;z)

\displaystyle\qquad\text{characterized by }\sim 1\text{ near }0;

(D.2a)

\displaystyle z^{1-c}{}_{1}F_{1}(a+1-c;2-c;z)

\displaystyle\qquad\text{characterized by }\sim z^{1-c}\text{ near }0;

(D.2b)

\displaystyle z^{-a}{}_{2}F_{0}(a,a+1-c;-;-z^{-1})

\displaystyle\qquad\text{characterized by }\sim z^{-a}\text{ near }+\infty.

(D.2c)

We note that the function
{}_{1}\mathbf{F}_{1}(a;c;z)=\frac{1}{\Gamma(c)}{}_{1}F_{1}(a;c;z)
is holomorphic in all variables. The other two solutions are defined for
z\notin]-\infty,0]
; thus
{}_{2}F_{0}(a,b;-;z)
is defined on
\mathbb{C}\backslash[0,\infty[
.

We will also need the hypergeometric equation

\big(z(1-z)\partial_{z}^{2}+(c-(a+b+1)z)\partial_{z}-ab\big)v(z)=0.

(D.3)

Among its 6 standard solutions, members of the famous Kummer’s
table, let us list three:

\displaystyle{}_{2}F_{1}(a,b;c;z)

\displaystyle\qquad\text{characterized by }\sim 1\text{ near }0;

(D.4a)

\displaystyle z^{1-c}{}_{2}F_{1}(a+1-c,b-c+1;2-c;z)

\displaystyle\qquad\text{characterized by }\sim z^{1-c}\text{ near }0;

(D.4b)

\displaystyle{}_{2}F_{1}(a,b;a+b+1-c;1-z)

\displaystyle\qquad\text{characterized by }\sim 1\text{ near }1.

(D.4c)

{}_{2}F_{1}(a,b;c;z)
may be defined by a power series convergent for

z
in the unit disc. It admits analytic continuation along any path
in
\mathbb{C}\backslash\{1\}
. To make it a single-valued function, it is customary to restrict its domain to

z\in\mathbb{C}\backslash[1,\infty[
.
Then
{}_{2}\mathbf{F}_{1}(a,b;c;z)=\frac{1}{\Gamma(c)}{}_{2}F_{1}(a,b;c;z)
is holomorphic in all variables. It satisfies

{}_{2}\mathbf{F}_{1}(a,b;0;z)=abz{}_{2}\mathbf{F}_{1}(a+1,b+1;2;z),

(D.5)

We have the following identities valid for
z\not\in]-\infty,0]
:

\displaystyle\frac{\sin(\pi c)}{\pi}z^{-a}{}_{2}F_{0}(a,a+1-c;-;-z^{-1})

(D.6)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{{}_{1}\mathbf{F}_{1}(a;c;z)}{\Gamma(a+1-c)}-\frac{z^{1-c}{}_{1}\mathbf{F}_{1}a+1-c;2-c;z)}{\Gamma(a)},

\displaystyle\frac{\sin(\pi c)}{\pi}{}_{2}\mathbf{F}_{1}(a,b;a+b+1-c;1-z)

(D.7)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{{}_{2}\mathbf{F}_{1}(a,b;c;z)}{\Gamma(a-c+1)\Gamma(b-c+1)}-\frac{z^{1-c}{}_{2}\mathbf{F}_{1}(a-c+1,b-c+1;2-c;z)}{\Gamma(a)\Gamma(b)}.

(
D.6
) can be taken as an alternative definition of
{}_{2}F_{0}
.

Lemma 69
.

Let
\epsilon>0
,
\mathrm{Re}(z)<\frac{1}{2}
. Then

{}_{2}F_{1}(a,b+\lambda,c+\lambda;z)=(1-z)^{-a}+O(\lambda^{-1}),\qquad|\arg(\lambda)|\leq\pi-\epsilon,\ |\lambda|\to\infty.

(D.8)

Proof.

Assumption about
\arg(\lambda)
guarantees that for
|\lambda|
sufficiently large
c+\lambda\notin-\mathbb{N}
, so the left hand side of (
D.8
) is finite. We apply the Pfaff transformation:

{}_{2}F_{1}(a,b+\lambda,c+\lambda;z)=(1-z)^{-a}{}_{2}F_{1}\left(a,c-b;c+\lambda;\frac{z}{z-1}\right).

(D.9)

The claim follows from the standard series defining
{}_{2}F_{1}
, because
\left|\frac{z}{z-1}\right|<1
.
∎

Lemma 70
.

The following asymptotic expansion holds for
\mathrm{Re}(z)<\frac{1}{2}
,
z\notin]-\infty,0]
,
s\to\pm\infty
:

{}_{2}\mathbf{F}_{1}(a,b-\mathrm{i}s;c;1-z)\sim\mathrm{sgn}(s)\cdot\left(\frac{(1-z)^{-a}(\mathrm{i}s)^{-a}}{\Gamma(c-a)}+\frac{z^{c-a-b+\mathrm{i}s}(1-z)^{b-c+\mathrm{i}s}(-\mathrm{i}s)^{a-c}}{\Gamma(a)}\right)

(D.10)

locally uniformly in
a,b,c,z
.

Proof.

Using (
D.7
) we get

\displaystyle\frac{\sin\left(\pi(a+b-c+1-\mathrm{i}s)\right)}{\pi}{}_{2}\mathbf{F}_{1}(a,b-\mathrm{i}s,c;1-z)

(D.11)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{{}_{2}\mathbf{F}_{1}(a,b-\mathrm{i}s;a+b-c+1-\mathrm{i}s;z)}{\Gamma(c-b+\mathrm{i}s)\Gamma(c-a)}-z^{c-a-b+\mathrm{i}s}\frac{{}_{2}\mathbf{F}_{1}(c-b+\mathrm{i}s,c-a;c-a-b+1+\mathrm{i}s;z)}{\Gamma(a)\Gamma(b-\mathrm{i}s)}.

Then (
D.8
) gives for large
|s|
:

\displaystyle\frac{\sin\left(\pi(a+b-c+1-\mathrm{i}s)\right)}{\pi}{}_{2}\mathbf{F}_{1}(a,b-\mathrm{i}s;c;z)

(D.12)

\displaystyle\sim

\displaystyle\frac{(1-z)^{-a}}{\Gamma(c-b+\mathrm{i}s)\Gamma(c-a)\Gamma(a+b-c+1-\mathrm{i}s)}-\frac{z^{c-a-b+\mathrm{i}s}(1-z)^{b-c-\mathrm{i}s}}{\Gamma(a)\Gamma(b-\mathrm{i}s)\Gamma(c-a-b+1+\mathrm{i}s)}.

Using
\sin\left(\pi(a+b-c+1-\mathrm{i}s)\right)\sim\frac{1}{2\mathrm{i}}\mathrm{e}^{\pi|s|+\mathrm{i}\pi\,\mathrm{sgn}(s)(a+b-c+1)}
and Stirling’s formula

\Gamma(z_{0}+z)\sim\sqrt{\frac{2\pi}{z}}\mathrm{e}^{-z}z^{z+z_{0}},\qquad|\arg(z)|\leq\pi-\epsilon,\ |z|\to\infty

(D.13)

yields, after algebraic manipulations, formula (
D.10
).
∎

D.2
Hyperbolic-type Whittaker equation

The standard form of
the Whittaker equation is

\displaystyle\Big(-\partial_{z}^{2}+\Big(m^{2}-\frac{1}{4}\Big)\frac{1}{z^{2}}-\frac{\beta}{z}+\frac{1}{4}\Big)g=0.

(D.14)

In this section we briefly describe solutions of the Whittaker equation, following mostly
[
13
,
10
]
.

We will sometimes call (
D.14
) the
hyperbolic-type Whittaker
equation
, to distinguish it from the
trigonometric-type Whittaker
equation
, which differs by the sign in front of
\frac{1}{4}
.

There are two kinds of standard solutions to the Whittaker equation.

The function
\mathcal{I}_{\beta,m}
is defined by

\mathcal{I}_{\beta,m}(z)=z^{\frac{1}{2}+m}{\mathrm{e}^{\mp\frac{z}{2}}}{}_{1}\mathbf{F}_{1}\Big(\frac{1}{2}+m\mp\beta;1+2m;\pm z\Big).

(D.15)

The standard domain of
\mathcal{I}_{\beta,m}
is
\mathbb{C}\backslash]-\infty,0]
. We have

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{-\beta,m}(-z)=\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\pi(\frac{1}{2}+m)\mathrm{sgn}(\mathrm{Im}(z))}\mathcal{I}_{\beta,m}(z)\qquad\text{for }z\in\mathbb{C}\backslash\mathbb{R},

(D.16a)

\displaystyle\overline{\mathcal{I}_{\overline{\beta},\overline{\vphantom{\beta}m}}(\overline{z})}=\mathcal{I}_{\beta,m}(z).

(D.16b)

The case
2m\in\mathbb{Z}
is called degenerate, and then

\mathcal{I}_{\beta,-m}(z)=\Big(-\beta-m+\frac{1}{2}\Big)_{2m}\,\mathcal{I}_{\beta,m}(z).

(D.17)

The function
\mathcal{K}_{\beta,m}
is defined by

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{\beta,m}(z)

\displaystyle:=z^{\beta}\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{z}{2}}{}_{2}F_{0}\Big(\frac{1}{2}+m-\beta,\frac{1}{2}-m-\beta;-;-z^{-1}\Big)

(D.18)

\displaystyle=\frac{\pi}{\sin(2\pi m)}\Big(-\frac{\mathcal{I}_{\beta,m}(z)}{\Gamma(\frac{1}{2}-m-\beta)}+\frac{\mathcal{I}_{\beta,-m}(z)}{\Gamma(\frac{1}{2}+m-\beta)}\Big).

It satisfies

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{\beta,-m}(z)=\mathcal{K}_{\beta,m}(z),

(D.19a)

\displaystyle\overline{\mathcal{K}_{\overline{\beta},\overline{\vphantom{\beta}m}}(\overline{z})}=\mathcal{K}_{\beta,m}(z).

(D.19b)

The Wronskian of
\mathcal{I}_{\beta,m}(\cdot)
and
\mathcal{K}_{\beta,m}(\cdot)
takes the form

\mathcal{W}(\mathcal{I}_{\beta,m},\mathcal{K}_{\beta,m})=-\frac{1}{\Gamma\Big(\frac{1}{2}+m-\beta\Big)}.

(D.20)

If
\frac{1}{2}+m-\beta\in-\mathbb{N}
, then the Wronskian vanishes and we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{\beta,m}(z)=

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\pi(\frac{1}{2}+m-\beta)}\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{2}+m+\beta\right)\mathcal{I}_{\beta,m}(z).

(D.21)

In this case functions in (
D.21
) essentially coincide with Laguerre polynomials

\mathcal{I}_{\pm(\frac{1}{2}+m+n),m}(z)=\frac{n!\;\!z^{\frac{1}{2}+m}\mathrm{e}^{\mp\frac{z}{2}}}{\Gamma(1+2m+n)}L_{n}^{(2m)}(\pm z).

(D.22)

Asymptotics of
\mathcal{I}_{\beta,m}
for small arguments are of the form

\mathcal{I}_{\beta,m}(z)=\frac{z^{\frac{1}{2}+m}}{\Gamma(1+2m)}\Big(1-\frac{\beta}{1+2m}z+O(z^{2})\Big),\text{ if }m\neq-\frac{1}{2},-1,-\frac{3}{2},\dots,\quad z\to 0,

(D.23)

The function
\mathcal{K}_{\beta,m}
satisfies, for
z\to 0
,

\begin{array}[]{rll}\mathcal{K}_{\beta,m}(z)=&z^{\frac{1}{2}}\Big(\frac{\Gamma(-2m)}{\Gamma(\frac{1}{2}-m-\beta)}z^{m}+\frac{\Gamma(2m)}{\Gamma(\frac{1}{2}+m-\beta)}z^{-m}\Big(1-\frac{\beta}{1-2m}z\Big)\Big)&\\
&\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt+O(|z|^{\frac{3}{2}+\mathrm{Re}(m)})+O(|z|^{\frac{5}{2}-\mathrm{Re}(m)})&\text{ for }\mathrm{Re}(m)\in[0,1[,\,m\neq 0,\frac{1}{2}\\
\mathcal{K}_{\beta,0}(z)=&-\frac{z^{\frac{1}{2}}}{\Gamma(\frac{1}{2}-\beta)}\big(\mathrm{ln}(z)+\psi(\frac{1}{2}-\beta)+2\lambda\big)+O(|z|^{\frac{3}{2}}\ln(z)),&\text{ for }m=0,\,\beta\notin\frac{1}{2}+\mathbb{N}\\
\mathcal{K}_{\beta,0}(z)=&(\beta-\frac{1}{2})!(-1)^{\beta-\frac{1}{2}}z^{\frac{1}{2}}+O(|z|^{\frac{3}{2}}),&\text{ for }m=0,\,\beta\in\frac{1}{2}+\mathbb{N}\\
\mathcal{K}_{\beta,\frac{1}{2}}(z)=&\frac{1}{\Gamma(-\beta)}\big(-\frac{1}{\beta}+z\ln(z)+z(\psi(1-\beta)+2\lambda-1+\frac{1}{2\beta})\big)&\\
&\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt+O(z^{2}\ln(z)),&\text{ for }m=\frac{1}{2},\beta\notin\mathbb{N}\\
\mathcal{K}_{0,\frac{1}{2}}(z)=&1-\frac{z}{2}+O(z^{2}),&\text{ for }m=\frac{1}{2},\beta=0\\
\mathcal{K}_{\beta,\frac{1}{2}}(z)=&\beta!(-1)^{\beta-1}z+O(z^{2}),&\text{ for }m=\frac{1}{2},\beta\in\mathbb{N}^{\times}\\
\mathcal{K}_{\beta,m}(z)=&\frac{\Gamma(2m)}{\Gamma(\frac{1}{2}+m-\beta)}z^{\frac{1}{2}-m}+O(|z|^{\frac{3}{2}-\mathrm{Re}(m)}),&\text{ for }\mathrm{Re}(m)\geq 1.\end{array}

(D.24)

Here
\gamma
denotes Euler’s constant and
\psi
is the digamma function.

Asymptotics for
|z|\to\infty
are given by (
\epsilon>0
):

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{\beta,m}(z)

\displaystyle=z^{\beta}\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{z}{2}}\big(1+O(z^{-1})\big),\quad|\mathrm{arg}(z)|\leq\frac{3}{2}\pi-\epsilon,

(D.25a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{\beta,m}(z)

\displaystyle=\frac{z^{-\beta}\mathrm{e}^{\frac{z}{2}}}{\Gamma(\frac{1}{2}+m-\beta)}\left(1+O(z^{-1})\right),\quad|\mathrm{arg}(z)|\leq\frac{\pi}{2}-\epsilon.

(D.25b)

The analytic continuations of
\mathcal{K}
, and more precisely the functions

z\mapsto\mathcal{K}_{-\beta,m}(\mathrm{e}^{\pm\mathrm{i}\pi}z)
are also solutions of
the Whittaker Equation (
D.14
). One can define
\mathcal{K}_{-\beta,m}(\mathrm{e}^{\pm i\pi}z)
as the unique holomorphic function of
z\in\mathbb{C}\backslash]-\infty,0]
which coincides with
\mathcal{K}_{-\beta,m}(-z)
on
\mathbb{C}_{\mp}
. Then

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{-\beta,m}(\mathrm{e}^{\pm\mathrm{i}\pi}z)

\displaystyle=\frac{\pi}{\sin(2\pi m)}\Big(-\frac{\mathrm{e}^{\pm\mathrm{i}\pi(\frac{1}{2}+m)}\mathcal{I}_{\beta,m}(z)}{\Gamma(\frac{1}{2}-m+\beta)}+\frac{\mathrm{e}^{\pm\mathrm{i}\pi(\frac{1}{2}-m)}\mathcal{I}_{\beta,-m}(z)}{\Gamma(\frac{1}{2}+m+\beta)}\Big).

(D.26)

\big(\mathcal{K}_{\beta,m}(z),\mathcal{K}_{-\beta,m}(\mathrm{e}^{\pm\mathrm{i}\pi}z)\big)
are linearly independent pairs of functions. In particular,
\mathcal{I}_{\beta,m}(z)
can be expressed in terms of these functions:

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{\beta,m}(z)=\mathrm{e}^{\pm\mathrm{i}\pi\beta}\Big(\frac{\mathrm{e}^{\mp\mathrm{i}\pi(m-\frac{1}{2})}\mathcal{K}_{\beta,m}(z)}{\Gamma(\frac{1}{2}+m+\beta)}+\frac{\mathcal{K}_{-\beta,m}(\mathrm{e}^{\pm\mathrm{i}\pi}z)}{\Gamma(\frac{1}{2}+m-\beta)}\Big).

D.3
Trigonometric-type Whittaker functions

It is convenient, in parallel to (
D.14
), to consider
the
trigonometric-type Whittaker equation

\displaystyle\Big(-\partial_{z}^{2}+\Big(m^{2}-\frac{1}{4}\Big)\frac{1}{z^{2}}-\frac{\beta}{z}-\frac{1}{4}\Big)g(z)=0.

(D.27)

It can be easily reduced to the hyperbolic-type Whittaker equation.

The function
\mathcal{J}_{\beta,m}
is defined by the formula

\mathcal{J}_{\beta,m}(z)=\mathrm{e}^{\mp\frac{\mathrm{i}\pi}{2}(m+\frac{1}{2})}\mathcal{I}_{\mp\mathrm{i}\beta,m}(\mathrm{e}^{\pm\frac{\mathrm{i}\pi}{2}}z).

(D.28)

It may also be described without invoking analytic continuations beyond the principal branch:

\mathcal{J}_{\beta,m}(z)=\begin{cases}\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}\pi}{2}\left(\frac{1}{2}+m\right)}\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{i}\beta,m}(-\mathrm{i}z),&-\frac{\pi}{2}<\mathrm{arg}(z)<\pi,\\
\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\mathrm{i}\pi}{2}\left(\frac{1}{2}+m\right)}\mathcal{I}_{-\mathrm{i}\beta,m}(\mathrm{i}z),&-\pi<\mathrm{arg}(z)<\frac{\pi}{2}.\end{cases}

(D.29)

The two expression agree for
|\mathrm{arg}(z)|<\frac{\pi}{2}
, by (
D.16a
). Combined with (
D.16b
) this implies

\overline{\mathcal{J}_{\overline{\beta},\overline{\vphantom{\beta}m}}}(\overline{z})=\mathcal{J}_{\beta,m}(z).

(D.30)

We also have a pair of functions
\mathcal{H}_{\beta,m}^{\pm}
defined by

\mathcal{H}_{\beta,m}^{\pm}(z)=\mathrm{e}^{\mp\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{2}\left({\frac{1}{2}+m}\right)}\mathcal{K}_{\pm\mathrm{i}\beta,m}(\mp\mathrm{i}z)

(D.31)

initially for
\mathrm{Re}(z)>0
and extended analytically to
z\in\mathbb{C}\backslash]-\infty,0]
. By (
D.19b
), they satisfy

\overline{\mathcal{H}^{\pm}_{\overline{\beta},\overline{\vphantom{\beta}m}}(\overline{z})}=\mathcal{H}^{\mp}_{\beta,m}(z).

(D.32)

The following connection formula holds:

\mathcal{J}_{\beta,m}(z)=\mathrm{e}^{-\pi\beta}\Big(\frac{\mathcal{H}_{\beta,m}^{+}(z)}{\Gamma\left({\frac{1}{2}+m+\mathrm{i}\beta}\right)}+\frac{\mathcal{H}_{\beta,m}^{-}(z)}{\Gamma\left({\frac{1}{2}+m-\mathrm{i}\beta}\right)}\Big).

(D.33)

For the behaviour around
\infty
, we have for
x>0
,
x\to\infty

\mathcal{H}_{\beta,m}^{\pm}(x)\sim\mathrm{e}^{\mp\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{2}(\frac{1}{2}+m)}\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\pi\beta}{2}}x^{\pm\mathrm{i}\beta}\;\!\mathrm{e}^{\pm\mathrm{i}\frac{x}{2}}\big(1+O(x^{-1})\big).

(D.34)

D.4
Recurrence relations

Whittaker functions satisfy several recurrence relations.
There are 6 basic ones, which we quote after Appendix A5 of

[
10
]
.

\displaystyle\Big(\sqrt{z}\partial_{z}+\frac{-\frac{1}{2}-m}{\sqrt{z}}-\frac{\sqrt{z}}{2}\Big)\mathcal{I}_{\beta,m}(z)

\displaystyle=\Big(-\frac{1}{2}-m-\beta\Big)\mathcal{I}_{\beta+\frac{1}{2},m+\frac{1}{2}}(z),

(D.35a)

\displaystyle\Big(\sqrt{z}\partial_{z}+\frac{-\frac{1}{2}+m}{\sqrt{z}}+\frac{\sqrt{z}}{2}\Big)\mathcal{I}_{\beta,m}(z)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{I}_{\beta-\frac{1}{2},m-\frac{1}{2}}(z),

(D.35b)

\displaystyle\Big(\sqrt{z}\partial_{z}+\frac{-\frac{1}{2}+m}{\sqrt{z}}-\frac{\sqrt{z}}{2}\Big)\mathcal{I}_{\beta,m}(z)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{I}_{\beta+\frac{1}{2},m-\frac{1}{2}}(z),

(D.35c)

\displaystyle\Big(\sqrt{z}\partial_{z}+\frac{-\frac{1}{2}-m}{\sqrt{z}}+\frac{\sqrt{z}}{2}\Big)\mathcal{I}_{\beta,m}(z)

\displaystyle=\Big(\frac{1}{2}+m-\beta\Big)\mathcal{I}_{\beta-\frac{1}{2},m+\frac{1}{2}}(z),

(D.35d)

\displaystyle\Big(z\partial_{z}+\beta-\frac{z}{2}\Big)\mathcal{I}_{\beta,m}(z)

\displaystyle=\Big(\frac{1}{2}+m+\beta\Big)\mathcal{I}_{\beta+1,m}(z),

(D.35e)

\displaystyle\Big(z\partial_{z}-\beta+\frac{z}{2}\Big)\mathcal{I}_{\beta,m}(z)

\displaystyle=\Big(\frac{1}{2}+m-\beta\Big)\mathcal{I}_{\beta-1,m}(z);

(D.35f)

\displaystyle\Big(\sqrt{z}\partial_{z}+\frac{-\frac{1}{2}-m}{\sqrt{z}}-\frac{\sqrt{z}}{2}\Big)\mathcal{K}_{\beta,m}(z)

\displaystyle=-\mathcal{K}_{\beta+\frac{1}{2},m+\frac{1}{2}}(z),

(D.36a)

\displaystyle\Big(\sqrt{z}\partial_{z}+\frac{-\frac{1}{2}+m}{\sqrt{z}}+\frac{\sqrt{z}}{2}\Big)\mathcal{K}_{\beta,m}(z)

\displaystyle=\Big(-\frac{1}{2}+m+\beta\Big)\mathcal{K}_{\beta-\frac{1}{2},m-\frac{1}{2}}(z),

(D.36b)

\displaystyle\Big(\sqrt{z}\partial_{z}+\frac{-\frac{1}{2}+m}{\sqrt{z}}-\frac{\sqrt{z}}{2}\Big)\mathcal{K}_{\beta,m}(z)

\displaystyle=-\mathcal{K}_{\beta+\frac{1}{2},m-\frac{1}{2}}(z),

(D.36c)

\displaystyle\Big(\sqrt{z}\partial_{z}+\frac{-\frac{1}{2}-m}{\sqrt{z}}+\frac{\sqrt{z}}{2}\Big)\mathcal{K}_{\beta,m}(z)

\displaystyle=\Big(-\frac{1}{2}-m+\beta\Big)\mathcal{K}_{\beta-\frac{1}{2},m+\frac{1}{2}}(z),

(D.36d)

\displaystyle\Big(z\partial_{z}+\beta-\frac{z}{2}\Big)\mathcal{K}_{\beta,m}(z)

\displaystyle=-\mathcal{K}_{\beta+1,m}(z),

(D.36e)

\displaystyle\Big(z\partial_{z}-\beta+\frac{z}{2}\Big)\mathcal{K}_{\beta,m}(z)

\displaystyle=\Big(\frac{1}{2}+m-\beta\Big)\Big(\frac{1}{2}-m-\beta\Big)\mathcal{K}_{\beta-1,m}(z).

(D.36f)

They have an interesting algebraic interpretation—they correspond to
the roots of the Lie algebra of generalized symmetries of the heat
equation in 2 dimensions, see
[
9
]
(where they are presented
using confluent functions, which as we know are equivalent to
Whittaker functions).

These recurrence relations involve 1st order differentiation, so it is
tempting to expect that they are closely related to the
Dirac-Coulomb Hamiltonian. It turns out, however, that the relationship
is not direct. By easy algebraic manipulations involving
(
D.35a
)-(
D.35d
) and (
D.36a
)-(
D.36d
),
we derive an additional pair of
recurrence relations described in the following proposition.
In some sense, (
D.35
) and (
D.36
) are “lower order” than
(
D.37
) and (
D.38
).
In fact, in (
D.35
) and (
D.36
) the parameters
\mu,\beta

appear only in 0th order terms, whereas in (
D.37
) and (
D.38
)
the differential operator is multiplied by
\mu
.

Proposition 71
.

\displaystyle\Big(2\mu\partial_{x}+\frac{2\mu^{2}}{x}-\beta\Big)\mathcal{I}_{\beta,\mu+\frac{1}{2}}(x)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{I}_{\beta,\mu-\frac{1}{2}}(x),

(D.37a)

\displaystyle\Big(2\mu\partial_{x}-\frac{2\mu^{2}}{x}+\beta\Big)\mathcal{I}_{\beta,\mu-\frac{1}{2}}(x)

\displaystyle=(\mu^{2}-\beta^{2})\mathcal{I}_{\beta,\mu+\frac{1}{2}}(x);

(D.37b)

\displaystyle\Big(2\mu\partial_{x}+\frac{2\mu^{2}}{x}-\beta\Big)\mathcal{K}_{\beta,\mu+\frac{1}{2}}(x)

\displaystyle=-(\mu+\beta)\mathcal{K}_{\beta,\mu-\frac{1}{2}}(x),

(D.38a)

\displaystyle\Big(2\mu\partial_{x}-\frac{2\mu^{2}}{x}+\beta\Big)\mathcal{K}_{\beta,\mu-\frac{1}{2}}(x)

\displaystyle=(-\mu+\beta)\mathcal{K}_{\beta,\mu+\frac{1}{2}}(x).

(D.38b)

Proof.

We first rewrite (
D.35a
)-(
D.35d
) and
(
D.36a
)-(
D.36d
) as follows:

\displaystyle\Big(\sqrt{z}\partial_{z}-\frac{\mu}{\sqrt{z}}-\frac{\sqrt{z}}{2}\Big)\mathcal{I}_{\beta,\mu-\frac{1}{2}}(z)

\displaystyle=\Big(-\mu-\beta\Big)\mathcal{I}_{\beta+\frac{1}{2},\mu}(z),

(D.39a)

\displaystyle\Big(\sqrt{z}\partial_{z}+\frac{\mu}{\sqrt{z}}+\frac{\sqrt{z}}{2}\Big)\mathcal{I}_{\beta,\mu+\frac{1}{2}}(z)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{I}_{\beta-\frac{1}{2},\mu}(z),

(D.39b)

\displaystyle\Big(\sqrt{z}\partial_{z}+\frac{\mu}{\sqrt{z}}-\frac{\sqrt{z}}{2}\Big)\mathcal{I}_{\beta,\mu+\frac{1}{2}}(z)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{I}_{\beta+\frac{1}{2},\mu}(z),

(D.39c)

\displaystyle\Big(\sqrt{z}\partial_{z}-\frac{\mu}{\sqrt{z}}+\frac{\sqrt{z}}{2}\Big)\mathcal{I}_{\beta,\mu-\frac{1}{2}}(z)

\displaystyle=\Big(\mu-\beta\Big)\mathcal{I}_{\beta-\frac{1}{2},\mu}(z);

(D.39d)

\displaystyle\Big(\sqrt{z}\partial_{z}-\frac{\mu}{\sqrt{z}}-\frac{\sqrt{z}}{2}\Big)\mathcal{K}_{\beta,\mu-\frac{1}{2}}(z)

\displaystyle=-\mathcal{K}_{\beta+\frac{1}{2},\mu}(z),

(D.40a)

\displaystyle\Big(\sqrt{z}\partial_{z}+\frac{\mu}{\sqrt{z}}+\frac{\sqrt{z}}{2}\Big)\mathcal{K}_{\beta,\mu+\frac{1}{2}}(z)

\displaystyle=\Big(\mu+\beta\Big)\mathcal{K}_{\beta-\frac{1}{2},\mu}(z),

(D.40b)

\displaystyle\Big(\sqrt{z}\partial_{z}+\frac{\mu}{\sqrt{z}}-\frac{\sqrt{z}}{2}\Big)\mathcal{K}_{\beta,\mu+\frac{1}{2}}(z)

\displaystyle=-\mathcal{K}_{\beta+\frac{1}{2},\mu}(z),

(D.40c)

\displaystyle\Big(\sqrt{z}\partial_{z}-\frac{\mu}{\sqrt{z}}+\frac{\sqrt{z}}{2}\Big)\mathcal{K}_{\beta,\mu-\frac{1}{2}}(z)

\displaystyle=\Big(-\mu+\beta\Big)\mathcal{K}_{\beta-\frac{1}{2},\mu}(z).

(D.40d)

Then we compute

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{z}}\big(-\eqref{rei1.}+(-\mu+\beta)\eqref{rei2.}-(\mu+\beta)\eqref{rei3.}+\eqref{rei4.}\big),

(D.41)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{z}}\big((\mu-\beta)\eqref{rei1.}+(-\mu^{2}+\beta^{2})\eqref{rei2.}+(\mu^{2}-\beta^{2})\eqref{rei3.}+(\mu+\beta)\eqref{rei4.}\big),

(D.42)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{z}}\big(-(\mu+\beta)\eqref{rek1.}+(\mu-\beta)\eqref{rek2.}+(\mu+\beta)\eqref{rek3.}+(\mu+\beta)\eqref{rek4.}\big),

(D.43)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{z}}\big((\mu-\beta)\eqref{rek1.}+(\mu-\beta)\eqref{rek2.}-(\mu-\beta)\eqref{rek3.}+(\mu+\beta)\eqref{rek4.}\big).

(D.44)

∎

(
D.37
) and (
D.38
) are closely related to the
Dirac-Coulomb Hamiltonian. To see this relation let
us introduce
\omega
satisfying
\omega^{2}=\mu^{2}-\beta^{2}
. Then
(
D.37
) and (
D.38
) can be rewritten in the following form:

\displaystyle 0=

\displaystyle 2\mu\partial_{x}\big(\mathcal{I}_{\beta,\mu-\frac{1}{2}}(x)+\mathrm{i}\omega\mathcal{I}_{\beta,\mu+\frac{1}{2}}(x)\big)

(D.45)

\displaystyle+\Big(-\frac{2\mu^{2}}{x}+\beta-\mathrm{i}\omega\Big)\big(\mathcal{I}_{\beta,\mu-\frac{1}{2}}(x)-\mathrm{i}\omega\mathcal{I}_{\beta,\mu+\frac{1}{2}}(x)\big),

\displaystyle 0=

\displaystyle 2\mu\partial_{x}\big(\mathcal{I}_{\beta,\mu-\frac{1}{2}}(x)-\mathrm{i}\omega\mathcal{I}_{\beta,\mu+\frac{1}{2}}(x)\big)

(D.46)

\displaystyle+\Big(-\frac{2\mu^{2}}{x}+\beta+\mathrm{i}\omega\Big)\big(\mathcal{I}_{\beta,\mu-\frac{1}{2}}(x)+\mathrm{i}\omega\mathcal{I}_{\beta,\mu+\frac{1}{2}}(x)\big);

\displaystyle 0=

\displaystyle 2\mu\partial_{x}\big((\mu+\beta)\mathcal{K}_{\beta,\mu-\frac{1}{2}}(x)-\mathrm{i}\omega\mathcal{K}_{\beta,\mu+\frac{1}{2}}(x)\big)

(D.47)

\displaystyle+\Big(-\frac{2\mu^{2}}{x}+\beta-\mathrm{i}\omega\Big)\big((\mu+\beta)\mathcal{K}_{\beta,\mu-\frac{1}{2}}(x)+\mathrm{i}\omega\mathcal{K}_{\beta,\mu+\frac{1}{2}}(x)\big),

\displaystyle 0=

\displaystyle 2\mu\partial_{x}\big((\mu+\beta)\mathcal{K}_{\beta,\mu-\frac{1}{2}}(x)+\mathrm{i}\omega\mathcal{K}_{\beta,\mu+\frac{1}{2}}(x)\big)

(D.48)

\displaystyle+\Big(-\frac{2\mu^{2}}{x}+\beta+\mathrm{i}\omega\Big)\big((\mu+\beta)\mathcal{K}_{\beta,\mu-\frac{1}{2}}(x)-\mathrm{i}\omega\mathcal{K}_{\beta,\mu+\frac{1}{2}}(x)\big).

The eigenvalue equations for
\xi_{p}^{\pm}
and
\zeta_{p}^{\pm}
follow
directly from these identities.

D.5
Integral transforms

Let us compute a useful integral transform of the confluent function:

Lemma 72
.

Assuming
\mathrm{Re}(b)>0
and
|\mathrm{Re}(w)|<\mathrm{Re}(z)
, one has

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}x^{b-1}\mathrm{e}^{-zx}{}_{1}\mathbf{F}_{1}(a;c;wx)\mathrm{d}x=z^{-b}\Gamma(b){}_{2}\mathbf{F}_{1}(a,b;c;z^{-1}w).

(D.49)

Furthermore, if
\mathrm{Re}(b)>0
,
\mathrm{Re}(b+1-c)>0
,
\mathrm{Re}(z)>0
,
w,z^{-1}w\notin]-\infty,0]
, then

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}x^{b-1}\mathrm{e}^{-zx}{}_{2}F_{0}\big(a,a+1-c;-;-(wx)^{-1}\big)(wx)^{-a}\mathrm{d}x

(D.50)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle z^{-b}\Gamma(b)\Gamma(1+b-c){}_{2}\mathbf{F}_{1}(a,b;a+b+1-c;1-z^{-1}w).

Proof.

We expand the confluent function in a power series and integrate term
by term:

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{(a)_{n}}{\Gamma(c+n)}\mathrm{e}^{-zx}x^{b+n-1}w^{n}\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{\Gamma(b+n)(a)_{n}}{\Gamma(c+n)}\frac{w^{n}}{z^{n+b}}=z^{-b}\Gamma(b)\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{(a)_{n}(b)_{n}}{\Gamma(c+n)}\left(\frac{w}{z}\right)^{n}.

(D.51)

This proves the first identity under additional assumption
|w|<\mathrm{Re}(z)
. The integrand can be
majorized by an integrable function for
|\mathrm{Re}(w)|<\mathrm{Re}(z)
. Therefore, we
can extend the identity by analytic continuation to this domain,
yielding (
D.49
).

(
D.49
), (
D.6
) and (
D.7
) and analytic
continuation imply (
D.50
).
∎

The following identity, valid for
v>0
,
\mathrm{Re}(\epsilon)>0
,
\mathrm{Re}(m+1-\mathrm{i}s)>0
, follows from (
D.49
):

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{e}^{-\epsilon x}x^{-\frac{1}{2}-\mathrm{i}s}\mathcal{J}_{\beta,m}(vx)\mathrm{d}x

(D.52)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle v^{m+\frac{1}{2}}\Big(\epsilon\pm\mathrm{i}\frac{v}{2}\Big)^{-m-1+\mathrm{i}s}\Gamma(m+1-\mathrm{i}s){}_{2}\mathbf{F}_{1}\left(m+\frac{1}{2}\pm\mathrm{i}\beta,m+1-\mathrm{i}s;2m+1;\frac{v}{\frac{v}{2}\mp\mathrm{i}\epsilon}\right).

Proposition 73
.

Let
v>0
,
\mathrm{Re}(m)>-1
. Then
x\mapsto\mathrm{e}^{-0x}\mathcal{J}_{\beta,m}(vx):=\lim\limits_{\epsilon\downarrow 0}\mathrm{e}^{-\epsilon x}\mathcal{J}_{\beta,m}(vx)
is a tempered
distribution on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
with the Mellin transform

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{e}^{-0x}x^{-\frac{1}{2}-\mathrm{i}s}\mathcal{J}_{\beta,m}(vx)\mathrm{d}x

(D.53)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle v^{-\frac{1}{2}+\mathrm{i}s}2^{m+1-\mathrm{i}s}(\pm\mathrm{i})^{-m-1+\mathrm{i}s}\Gamma(m+1-\mathrm{i}s){}_{2}\mathbf{F}_{1}\left(m+\frac{1}{2}\pm\mathrm{i}\beta,m+1-\mathrm{i}s;2m+1;2\pm\mathrm{i}0\right),

which is bounded by
c_{\beta,m}\left(\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\pi}{2}(|s|+s)}|s|^{\mathrm{Im}(\beta)}+\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\pi}{2}(|s|-s)}|s|^{-\mathrm{Im}(\beta)}\right)
, with
c_{\beta,m}
a locally bounded function of
\beta,m
.

Similarly, for any
\mu
(including
\mu=0
),
x\mapsto\mathrm{e}^{-0x}\frac{1}{\mu}\big(\mathcal{J}_{\beta,\mu-\frac{1}{2}}(vx)+\beta\mathcal{J}_{\beta,\mu+\frac{1}{2}}(vx)\big)
is a tempered
distribution on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
, whose Mellin transform can be computed
from (
D.53
) and is bounded by
c_{\beta,\mu}\left(\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\pi}{2}(|s|+s)}|s|^{\mathrm{Im}(\beta)}+\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\pi}{2}(|s|-s)}|s|^{-\mathrm{Im}(\beta)}\right)
, with
c_{\beta,\mu}
a locally bounded function of
\beta,\mu
.

Proof.

We use the criterion from Lemma
67
. Let

f_{\epsilon}(x)=\mathrm{e}^{-\epsilon x}\mathcal{J}_{\beta,m}(vx)
. Then
t\mapsto\mathrm{e}^{\frac{t}{2}}f_{\epsilon}(\mathrm{e}^{t})
is smooth and vanishes exponentially for
t\to-\infty
and superexponentially for
t\to\infty
. In particular
f_{\epsilon}
is a tempered distribution on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
. Its Mellin transform is given by the absolutely convergent integral (
D.52
). It is a smooth function with smooth pointwise limit
\epsilon\to 0
. Required bounds follow from (
D.10
). This completes the proof of the first part.

Next, we compute

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{e}^{-\epsilon x}x^{-\frac{1}{2}-\mathrm{i}s}\frac{1}{\mu}\left(\mathcal{J}_{\beta,\mu-\frac{1}{2}}(vx)+\beta\mathcal{J}_{\beta,\mu+\frac{1}{2}}(vx)\right)\mathrm{d}x

(D.54)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle v^{\mu}\left(\epsilon\pm\mathrm{i}\frac{v}{2}\right)^{-\mu-\frac{1}{2}+\mathrm{i}s}\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{2}+\mu-\mathrm{i}s\right)

\displaystyle\times

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\mu}\Bigg({}_{2}\mathbf{F}_{1}\left(\mu+\mathrm{i}\beta,\frac{1}{2}+\mu-\mathrm{i}s;2\mu;\frac{v}{\frac{v}{2}-\mathrm{i}\epsilon}\right)-\mathrm{i}\beta\left(\frac{1}{2}+\mu-\mathrm{i}s\right)\frac{v}{\frac{v}{2}-\mathrm{i}\epsilon}{}_{2}\mathbf{F}_{1}\left(1+\mu+\mathrm{i}\beta,\frac{3}{2}+\mu-\mathrm{i}s;2\mu+2;\frac{v}{\frac{v}{2}-\mathrm{i}\epsilon}\right)\Bigg).

Expression in the last line is nonsingular for
\mu\to 0
on the
account of (
D.5
). Bounds on the growth at infinity are derived as in the first case.
∎
